# Chapter 1 · al-Fātiḥah — merged master (all ten sources)

> **Merge note.** This master gathers, under one head per element of `../tafsir/elements.txt`, the
> evidence that each of the ten source records in this folder carries. The method is mechanical and the
> blocks are **verbatim from the per-source files**, each opening with its own bold attribution
> (**Al-Ṭabarī.** · **Ibn Kathīr.** · **The Study Quran.** …), so every item in the master names its origin.
> * A head appears here only where at least one source has material for it; a source absent under a head is
>   silent on that element for that verse — its silence is shown in its own file's `_Coverage:_` line.
> * Sources are ordered as in `README.md` (their folders, alphabetical); the super-source's own sigla
>   (*Q, Ṭ, Ṭb, …*) are kept inside its blocks.
> * The verse line is given once per verse; the ten sources' `> Structural note`s are gathered verbatim in
>   the appendix, since each explains a re-mapping of its corpus file.
> * Ibn ʿUthaymīn has been withdrawn from the source set; its blocks are not included.


---

## Sūrah-level elements

### S1 · Names & titles of the sūrah (*asmāʾ al-sūrah*)

**Al-Alūsī.** "This noble sūrah has **names, which some have carried to more than twenty** — I shall give the
ones on which the chain of transmission settles." He enumerates **twenty-two**:

> "**1.** *Fātiḥat al-Kitāb* — **because it is its beginning in the familiar order** — *not* because teaching or
> recitation in prayer is opened with it, as **al-Suyūṭī** claimed, and *not* because it was the first sūrah
> revealed: for beginning in the order of teaching or revelation would require the same order in all the
> Book's parts, and the teaching and revelation orders are not the familiar order."
> "**2.** *Fātiḥat al-Qurʾān* — by the measure just given."
> "**3–4.** *Umm al-Kitāb* and *Umm al-Qurʾān*: the ḥadīth *'Let none of you say Umm al-Kitāb — let him say
> Fātiḥat al-Kitāb'* **has no basis**; rather its being so named is established in the *Ṣaḥīḥ*s. It is called
> *umm* — mother — because the beginning, in writing, recitation, revelation or prayer, is with it, and what
> follows is its follower, **so it is like the mother after whom the child forms**; and a **banner** is also
> called *umm* because it goes first and the army follows it — hence *Umm al-Qurā* (Makkah) — **or because it
> comprises** the aims of the meanings in the Qurʾān: praise of Allah as He deserves, worship by command and
> prohibition, and warning (*waʿīd*)."
> "**5–7.** *al-Kanz* (the Treasure), *al-Wāfiyah* (the Completing), *al-Kāfiyah* (the Sufficing) — **because
> it comprises the treasured essences, so it completes and suffices**; or because **it is not halved in prayer
> and nothing else suffices in its place**."
> "**8.** *al-Asās* (the Foundation) — **because it is the root of the Qurʾān and its first sūrah**."
> "**9–13.** *Sūrat al-Ḥamd*, *Sūrat al-Shukr*, *Sūrat al-Duʿāʾ*, *Sūrat Taʿlīm al-Masʾalah* (the sūrah of
> teaching the asking), *Sūrat al-Suʾāl* (the asking) — **because it comprises these**."
> "**14–15.** *Sūrat al-Munājāh* (the intimate converse) and *Sūrat al-Tafwīḍ* (the committing) — **because the
> servant converses with his Lord by** *iyyāka naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn*, **and by what follows, the
> committing of the affair is realised.**"
> "**16–18.** *al-Ruqyah*, *al-Shifāʾ*, *al-Shāfiyah* — **and the sound ḥadīths indicate this.**"
> "**19.** *Sūrat al-Ṣalāh* — **because it is obligatory, or a required part, in it** — the recommended-ness
> being a madhhab of some mujtahids, and a report from some on its place in supererogatory prayer; and from
> its names is *al-Ṣalāh*, by the ḥadīth *'I have divided the prayer between Me and My servant into halves'* —
> **and He meant the sūrah**."
> "**20.** *al-Nūr* — **for its great prominence through frequent use**; or for its lighting the hearts; or
> because its gathered meanings express the Qurʾān as light. **21.** *al-Qurʾān al-ʿAẓīm* — evident from what
> preceded. **22.** *al-Sabʿ al-Mathānī* — **because it is seven verses by agreement**."

**And the *sūrah*'s own etymology** (from the same introduction): the word *sūrah* may be *mahmūzah* or not;
if from *al-suʾr* — **"the remainder, because the remainder of a thing is part of it"**; if from *sūr
al-bināʾ* — **"the enclosure, or a city wall, because it encloses its verses"**; or from *al-tasawwur*,
**"rising high, because it rises by being Allah's speech"** — quoting **al-Nābighah**: *"Do you not see that
Allah has given you a **sūrah** around which every king is seen to sway?"* And: "It is also used for a lofty
station, as in the poet's word… And one statement holds it is a self-contained group of verses, so that the
verse of the Kursī [which the Prophet's report marked off] is separately titled by *tawqīf*."

**Al-Baghawī.** "Sūrat Fātiḥat al-Kitāb — **it has three well-known names: *Fātiḥat al-Kitāb*, *Umm
al-Qurʾān*, and *al-Sabʿ al-Mathānī*.** It was called *Fātiḥat al-Kitāb* **because Allah opened the Qurʾān
with it**; and it was called *Umm al-Qurʾān* and *Umm al-Kitāb* **because it is the root of the Qurʾān —
from it the Qurʾān began**, and the *umm* of a thing is its root; Makkah is called *Umm al-Qurā* because it
is the origin of the lands, the earth being spread out from beneath it. And it is said: **because it is a
preface and an imām for the sūrahs that follow it — writing in the *muṣḥaf* begins with it and recitation in
prayer begins with it.** And *al-Sabʿ al-Mathānī* **because it is seven verses by the agreement of the
scholars**; and it was called *mathānī* **because it is repeated in prayer — it is recited in every
*rakʿah***. And **Mujāhid** said: it was called *mathānī* **because Allah reserved it (*istathnāhā*) for
this community and stored it up for them (*fa-dhakharahā lahum*).**"

**Al-Qurṭubī** counts **twelve names**, in "the fourth *masʾalah* on its names":

1. **al-Ṣalāh** — from "Allah's words: *I have divided the prayer between Me and My servant into two
   halves*"; the sūrah is called *prayer* because "prayer is not valid except by it".
2. **Sūrat al-Ḥamd** — "because it contains the mention of praise, as one says *Sūrat al-Aʿrāf*, *al-Anfāl*,
   *al-Tawbah* and the like."
3. **Fātiḥat al-Kitāb** — "with no disagreement among the scholars; it is so called because the recitation of
   the Qurʾān is begun with it in speech, the writing in the *muṣḥaf* is begun with it in script, and the
   prayers are begun with it."
4. **Umm al-Kitāb** — "in this name there is disagreement: the majority permitted it; **Anas, al-Ḥasan and
   Ibn Sīrīn disliked it**. Al-Ḥasan said: *Umm al-Kitāb is the lawful and the unlawful*, by 3:7 (*āyātun
   muḥkamātun hunna ummu l-kitāb*); Anas and Ibn Sīrīn said: *Umm al-Kitāb is the name of the Preserved
   Tablet*, by 43:4."
5. **Umm al-Qurʾān** — "disagreed over likewise: the majority permitted it, Anas and Ibn Sīrīn disliked it,
   and the established *aḥādīth* refute both these sayings." (Tirmidhī from Abū Hurayrah — "a *ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ*
   ḥadīth": "al-Ḥamdu li-llāh is *Umm al-Qurʾān* and *Umm al-Kitāb* and *al-Sabʿ al-Mathānī*.") Al-Bukhārī's
   comment: "it was named *Umm al-Kitāb* because its writing begins the *maṣāḥif* and its recitation begins
   the prayer." And Yaḥyā b. Yaʿmur: "*Umm al-Qurā* is Makkah, *Umm Khurāsān* is Marw, and *Umm al-Qurʾān* is
   Sūrat al-Ḥamd" — because "it is the first of it and contains all its sciences; and by that Makkah is
   called *Umm al-Qurā* because it is the first of the earth and from it it was spread out".
6. **al-Mathānī** — "so called because it is repeated (*tuthannā*) in every *rakʿah*; and it is said because
   it was **reserved** (*ustuthniyat*) for this community, so it did not come down on anyone before them as a
   treasure laid up for them."
7. **al-Qurʾān al-ʿAẓīm** — "so called because it contains all the sciences of the Qurʾān: it comprises
   praise of Allah by the attributes of His perfection and majesty, the command of worship and sincerity in
   it, the confession of inability to fulfil any of it except by His help, the supplication to Him for
   guidance to the straight path, sufficing in the circumstances of the deniers, and the declaration of the
   end of the rejecters."
8. **al-Shifāʾ** — Dārimī from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī: "Fātiḥat al-Kitāb is a cure for every poison."
9. **al-Ruqyah** — "established from the ḥadīth of Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, in which the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said
   to the man who had recited over the chief of the tribe: *What made you know that it is a ruqyah?*"
10. **al-Asās** — "A man complained to al-Shaʿbī of a pain in his side and he said: *You must take the
    foundation of the Qurʾān, Fātiḥat al-Kitāb*", and he heard Ibn ʿAbbās say: "Everything has a foundation:
    the foundation of the world is Makkah, the foundation of the heavens is *ʿArībā* — the seventh heaven,
    the foundation of the earth is *ʿAjībā* — the lowest seventh earth, the foundation of the gardens is
    Jannat ʿAdn, the foundation of the Fire is Jahannam, the foundation of creation is Ādam, the foundation
    of the prophets is Nūḥ, the foundation of the Children of Israel is Yaʿqūb, the foundation of the Books
    is the Qurʾān, **the foundation of the Qurʾān is al-Fātiḥah, and the foundation of al-Fātiḥah is *bismi
    llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm***; so when you fall ill or complain, take al-Fātiḥah and you will be cured."
11. **al-Wāfiyah** ("the Complete") — "the saying of Sufyān b. ʿUyaynah, because it is not halved and does
    not admit of being shortened; if one recited half of any other sūrah in one *rakʿah* and half in another
    it would do, but were al-Fātiḥah split over two *rakʿahs* it would not do."
12. **al-Kāfiyah** ("the Sufficient") — Yaḥyā b. Abī Kathīr: "because it suffices for other than it, and
    nothing else suffices in its place"; supported by Muḥammad b. Khallād al-Iskandarānī from the Prophet ﷺ:
    "*Umm al-Qurʾān is a substitute for other than it, and nothing else is a substitute for it.*"

And separately (the third *masʾalah* of the first *bāb*), **ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭālib** from the Prophet ﷺ: "Fātiḥat
al-Kitāb, *Āyat al-Kursī*, *shahida llāhu annahu lā ilāha illā huwa*, and *qul allāhumma mālika l-mulk* —
**these verses are suspended by the Throne, with no veil between them and Allah**."

**Ibn Kathīr.** The sūrah is called:

* **al-Fātiḥah** — “the Opener of the Book, the Surah with which prayers are begun.”
* **Umm al-Kitāb** (the Mother of the Book) — “according to the majority of the scholars”; and **Umm al-Qurʾān**.
  Al-Bukhārī said: “It is called Umm Al-Kitab, because the Qur'an starts with it and because the prayer is
  started by reciting it.” Ibn Jarīr added that “the Arabs call every comprehensive matter that contains
  several specific areas an *Umm*” — the membrane around the brain (*Umm al-Raʾs*), the flag that gathers
  the army's ranks, and Makkah as *Umm al-Qurā*, “the grandest and the leader of all villages”.
* **al-Sabʿ al-Mathānī** (the seven oft-repeated), **al-Ḥamd** and **al-Ṣalāh** — “because the Prophet ﷺ
  said that his Lord said: *The prayer is divided into two halves between Me and My servants…*”; and
  “Al-Fatihah was called the Salah, because reciting it is a condition for the correctness of Salah — the prayer.”
* **al-Shifāʾ** (the Cure) and **al-Ruqyah** (remedy) — “since in the Sahih, there is the narration of Abū
  Saʿīd telling the story of the Companion who used Al-Fatihah as a remedy for the tribal chief who was
  poisoned”, after which the Messenger of Allah ﷺ asked: “*How did you know that it is a Ruqyah?*”

**Ḥadīth cited for the names.** At-Tirmidhī, who graded it Ṣaḥīḥ, from Abū Hurayrah: the Messenger of
Allah ﷺ said: “*Al-Ḥamdu lillāhi Rabbil-ʿālamīn* is the Mother of the Qur'an, the Mother of the Book, and
the seven repeated Ayat of the Glorious Qur'an.” Imam Ahmad recorded from Abū Hurayrah: “It is Umm
Al-Qur'an, the seven repeated (verses) and the Glorious Qur'an”; and aṭ-Ṭabarī recorded from Abū Hurayrah:
“It is Umm Al-Qur'an, Al-Fatihah of the Book and the seven repeated (verses).”

**The Study Quran.** The primary meaning of *al-Fātiḥah* is “The Opening,” “which indicates the sūrah's
function as ‘the opening of the Book’ (*Fātiḥat al-kitāb*) and as the first sūrah to be recited in each
cycle (*rakʿah*) of all the canonical prayers as well as the manner in which it serves as an opening for
many functions in everyday Islamic life. It can also be taken as a reference to this sūrah's ability to
open one's breast to faith in God.” Other titles:

* **Umm al-kitāb**, “Mother of the Book” — “a term also applied to other aspects of the Quran (3:7) and to
  the celestial archetype of the Quran and in fact all sacred scripture (see 13:39; 43:4)”; and **“The
  Mother of the Quran”** (IK, Ṭ) — “a reference to its containing the meaning of the entire Quran” (IK).
* **“The Seven Oft-Repeated”** (*al-sabʿ al-mathānī*, 15:87); **“The Cure”** (*al-Shifāʾ*), “because it is
  said to have healing powers for both body and soul”; **“The Foundation”** (*al-Asās*), “because it serves
  as a foundation for the whole of the Quran”; **Sūrat al-Ḥamd**, “The Chapter of Praise”; and **Sūrat
  al-Ṣalāh**, “The Chapter of the Prayer”.

Its use: “recited at the beginning of each cycle of prayer by all Sunnis and many Shiites. In Shiite law
one is allowed to recite the Fātiḥah in the third and fourth cycles or to recite, ‘Glory be to God, and
praise be to God. There is no god but God, and God is great.’” And: “It is also recited by Muslims on
occasions as diverse as a funeral, a wedding, the birth of a child, the inauguration of an official event,
the signing of contracts, and the commencement of an individual endeavor, such as the beginning of a
journey. In some lands, funeral services are referred to as *fātiḥah*, because they mark an opening from
one life to another.”


### S2 · Period & place of revelation (*Makkī* / *Madanī*)

**Al-Alūsī.** "**It is differed over: the majority hold it Makkan — indeed, some hold it among the first of
what was revealed of the Qurʾān, and this is reported from ʿAlī, Ibn ʿAbbās, Qatādah and most of the
Companions. And from Mujāhid: it is Madinan — and he alone said it, so much so that it is counted a slip of
his.** And it is said: **it came down at Makkah when the prayer was prescribed, and at Madinah when the
*qiblah* was turned, so that it be known that it stands in prayer as it stood**; and it is said: some of it
Makkan and some Madinan — **and its weakness is not hidden.**"

And on the proof: "**People have made much of inferring its being Makkan from the verse of al-Ḥijr** —
*wa-laqad ātaynāka sabʿan mina l-mathānī wa-l-qurʾāna l-ʿaẓīm* — and al-Ḥijr is Makkan by the scholars'
explicit statement and the report from Ibn ʿAbbās…" — **but al-Alūsī will not rest on it**: "**the strongest is
the inference from the transmission from the Companions who witnessed the revelation and the sending down —
for that depends first on interpreting *al-Sabʿ al-Mathānī* as the Fātiḥah, which, though sound and
established in the ḥadīths, is also soundly reported from Ibn ʿAbbās and others as the Seven Long Sūrahs; and
secondly on the impossibility of favouring with a thing before giving it, while Allah favoured him — ﷺ — by
the word** *innā fataḥnā laka fatḥan mubīnā* **years before the conquest** (the past tense being the
realisation of the occurrence)."

**Al-Baghawī.** "**It is Makkan on the saying of the majority; and Mujāhid said: Madinan; and it is said: it
came down twice — once at Makkah and once at Madinah, and for that it was called *mathānī*.** **And the
first is sounder, that it is Makkan, because Allah favoured the Messenger ﷺ by His word** *wa-laqad
ātaynāka sabʿan mina l-mathānī* (15:87) **— and what is meant by it is Fātiḥat al-Kitāb, and Sūrat al-Ḥijr
is Makkan, so He would not have been favouring him with it before its revelation.**"

**Al-Qurṭubī** (the second *masʾalah* of the second *bāb*). "The scholars differed whether it is Makkan or
Madinan. **Ibn ʿAbbās, Qatādah and Abū al-ʿĀliyah al-Riyāḥī** and others said: **it is Makkan**. **Abū
Hurayrah, Mujāhid, ʿAṭāʾ b. Yasār, al-Zuhrī** and others said: **it is Madinan**. And it is said: **half of
it came down at Makkah and half at Madinah** (related by Abū al-Layth Naṣr b. Muḥammad al-Samarqandī). **The
first is sounder**, by His word *wa-laqad ātaynāka sabʿan mina l-mathānī wa-l-Qurʾāna l-ʿaẓīm* (15:87) — and
al-Ḥijr is Makkan by consensus — and because the obligation of prayer was at Makkah, and "it is not preserved
that there was ever in Islam a prayer without *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn*". And on the reports about
the first thing revealed: Ṭayyibī al-Qāḍī mentioned the disagreement (*al-Muddaththir*, *Iqraʾ*,
al-Fātiḥah); al-Bayhaqī in *Dalāʾil al-Nubuwwah* gives Abū Maysarah ʿAmr b. Shuraḥbīl's report that the
Prophet ﷺ told Khadījah of the voice he heard, and that Warqah b. Nawfal said: "When he comes to you, be
firm until you hear what he says, then come to me and tell me" — and when he was alone he was called: "*O
Muḥammad, say* bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm, al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn *until he reached* wa-lā
l-ḍāllīn*; say:* lā ilāha illā llāh." _(Al-Bayhaqī said: this report is interrupted.)_ Al-Qurṭubī's own
resolution, on the Muslim ḥadīth of Ibn ʿAbbās that a "gate of heaven was opened" and an angel came down
saying "Rejoice in two lights given to you, not given to a prophet before you: Fātiḥat al-Kitāb and the
closing verses of Sūrat al-Baqarah": "**Jibrīl came down with its recitation at Makkah and the angel came
down with its reward at Madinah** … and it has been said it is Makkan-Madinan, coming down twice — and what
we have said is preferable, for it joins the Qurʾān and the Sunnah."

**As-Saʿdī.** The sūrah is Makkan. *(Text: «وهي مكية».)*

**Ibn Kathīr.** “Al-Fatihah was revealed in Makkah as Ibn ʿAbbās, Qatādah and Abū al-ʿĀliyah stated” — with
the supporting verse, allāh said: “*And indeed, We have bestowed upon you the seven Mathani (seven
repeatedly recited verses)* (15:87).”

**The Study Quran.** “Al-Fātiḥah is considered by the vast majority of scholars to be among the first
sūrahs to have been revealed in Makkah. According to Mujāhid… this sūrah was revealed in Madinah (W). Most
scholars claim that this is impossible, however, as the Muslims would not have been able to perform their
required prayers without it (Q, Ṭ, W).”


### S3 · Verse, word & letter counts (*ʿadad al-āyāt*)

**Al-Alūsī** (the Fātiḥah's longest discussion). Statement of the issue: "**People have differed over the
*basmalah* outside al-Naml — where by agreement it is part of a verse — on ten sayings**: (1) **it is not a
verse of the sūrahs at all**; (2) **it is a verse of all of them except al-Barāʾah**; (3) **a verse of
al-Fātiḥah only**; (4) **part of a verse of al-Fātiḥah only**; (5) **a separate verse (*āyah fadhdhah*)
revealed to mark the heads of the sūrahs, for blessing and for separation**; (6) **it may be made a verse of
them and not a verse, because it was revealed twice with the two descriptions**; (7) **part of a verse of all
the sūrahs**; (8) **a verse of al-Fātiḥah and part of a verse of the other sūrahs**; (9) the reverse;
(10) **separate verses, even if revealed repeatedly.**"

His attribution of the schools: "**Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn al-Mubārak, the people of Makkah such as Ibn Kathīr, and
the people of Kūfah such as ʿĀṣim, al-Kisāʾī and others apart from Ḥamzah, and most of the Shāfiʿīs and the
Imāmiyyah — on the second** [a verse of every sūrah except al-Barāʾah]; **some Shāfiʿīs and Ḥamzah, and it is
attributed to Imām Aḥmad — on the third** [al-Fātiḥah only]; **the people of Madinah — among them Mālik — and
of Syria — among them al-Awzāʿī — and of Baṣrah — among them Abū ʿAmr and Yaʿqūb — on the fifth** [a separate
marking verse]; **and this is the well-known of our madhhab** [Ḥanafī]."

**And his own position, stated after the long refutation of al-Rāzī's sixteen proofs:** "*The sound position
of our madhhab is that* ***bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm is a separate verse, and is of the Qurʾān, though it
is not of al-Fātiḥah itself***; and **many of us have made its recitation in prayer obligatory** — and
al-Zaylaʿī in *Sharḥ al-Kanz* said the soundest is that it is *wājib*, and al-Zāhidī from *al-Mujtabā* that
**the sound position is that it is obligatory in every *rakʿah* in which recitation is obligatory, and this
is the sound report from Abū Ḥanīfah**." And: "**Whoever omits it out of forgetfulness in a *rakʿah* makes
the prostration of forgetfulness — 'the majority said its being obligatory entails it'"** (Ibn Wahbān's
verses). And from *Ghunyat al-Mutamallī*: "**it is the more cautious, and I hold it** — against Qāḍī Khān,
the author of *al-Khulāṣah* and others, and the truth is worthier of being followed." And his sharp verdict
on the opposite camp's zeal: "the saying of some on this is **the utmost tyranny of the pen**, a limit of
partisanship without mastery."

The counting consequence: "**They agreed that al-Fātiḥah is seven verses.** For him who counts the basmalah
of al-Fātiḥah [as its verse], the first verse is *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* and the beginning of the last
verse is *ṣirāṭa lladhīna*; and for him who does not count it, its beginning is *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi
l-ʿālamīn* and the beginning of the last verse is *ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim*." And in the names material:
"***al-Sabʿ al-Mathānī*** — **because it is seven verses by agreement; and we have not seen any comparable
case except *'a-raʾayta'*** [in Sūrat al-Māʿūn, counted variously]; **and the saying that it is eight or nine
is anomalous, not regarded** — or a supposition of the transmitter; **though some of them counted the
*basmalah* a verse without *anʿamta ʿalayhim*, and some the reverse** — the pivot being transmission."

**And the letter-scheme al-Alūsī reports under this head** (his own excursion): "*It is not said*: if the
Fātiḥah gathers the Qurʾān's meanings, why have **seven letters fallen out of it** — *thāʾ, jīm, khāʾ, zāy,
shīn, ẓāʾ, fāʾ*? — *for we say*: **perhaps that is to point to spiritual perfection not entailing formal
completeness**… **and it was from the dark letters (*al-ḥurūf al-ẓalmāniyyah*) not found among the openings of
the sūrahs**; **gathered, after dropping the repeated, they give:** ***'a path upon truth we hold fast'*** —
**and the luminous (*nūrāniyyah*) fourteen letters are all in it**, pointing to the **prevalence of beauty
over majesty** — the recurrence of mercy-words in the Fātiḥah. **And why the remaining seven were not dropped
from the luminous side is so that it be known the affair is mixed** — 'none feels secure from Allah's plan but
the people of loss' (7:99)… **and in the words** *nabbiʾ ʿibādī annī anā l-ghafūru l-raḥīm • wa-anna
ʿadhābī huwa l-ʿadhābu l-alīm* (15:49–50) **is a sign and what a sign of that**… **pointing to the two classes
of the believers** — *fa-minhum ẓālimun li-nafsihi wa-minhum muqtaṣidun wa-minhum sābiqun bi-l-khayrāti*
(35:32) — 'the darkness of his sins does not extinguish his light', and *'the adulterer does not commit
adultery while a believer'* is borne on *perfection*."

**And al-Rāzī's rival explanation of the seven dropped letters, and its rebuttal:** "**Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī**
made the cause of dropping these letters their **indicating punishment** — *thāʾ* from *thubūr* (ruin), *jīm*
the first letter of *Jahannam*, *khāʾ* from *khizhī* (disgrace), *zāy* and *shīn* from *zafīr* and *shahīq*
(the fire's sighs), *zāy* again from *Zaqqūm*, *shīn* from *shaqāʾ*, *ẓāʾ* the first of *ẓill* — *inṭaliqū ilā
ẓillin dhī thalāthi shuʿab* (77:30), and also from *laẓā*, *fāʾ* from *firāq* — then that Allah set against the
seven gates of Jahannam — *lahā sabʿatu abwābin li-kulli bābin minhum juzʾun maqsūm* (15:44) — the dropping of
seven letters, so that **whoever recites this sūrah, believes in it and knows its realities is safe from the
seven depths of Jahannam.**" — **Al-Alūsī**: "'The flaw in it is not hidden and its answer does not profit
him nor suffice him, for one may say: then let *dhāl*, *wāw*, *nūn*, *ḥāʾ*, *ʿayn*, *mīm* and *ghayn* fall
out too — *wāw* from *al-wayl*, *dhāl* from *al-dhillah*, *nūn* from *al-nār*, *ḥāʾ* from *al-ḥamīm*, *ʿayn*
from *al-ʿadhāb*, *mīm* from *al-mihād*, *ghayn* from *al-ghawāshī* — and the verses are plain, all of them
concerning the people of the Fire, **and the use would be the same with no difference at all**… and I do not
accept this for al-Fakhr, he being the master…" And the attribution of that scheme to **Amīr al-Muʾminīn ʿAlī**
— "when Caesar of Rome asked Muʿāwiyah about it and he did not answer, then asked ʿAlī and he answered" — "**it
has no basis**; and granting it, what the Amīr aimed at was but to alert the questioner to what is hidden from
you of the secrets."

**Al-Baghawī.** "**They agreed that the Fātiḥah is seven verses.** For him who counts the basmalah of the
Fātiḥah, the first verse is *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* and the beginning of the last verse is *ṣirāṭa
lladhīna*; and for him who does not count it of the Fātiḥah, its beginning is *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi
l-ʿālamīn* and the beginning of the last verse is *ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim*."
And the schools, as he states them:

* "**The reciters of Madinah and Baṣrah and the jurists of Kūfah** — that it is not of Fātiḥat al-Kitāb nor
  of any other sūrah, and that the opening with it is for seeking blessing (*li-l-tayammun wa-l-tabarruk*)."
* "**The reciters of Makkah and Kūfah and most of the jurists of the Ḥijāz** — that it is of the Fātiḥah and
  not of the other sūrahs, and that it was written as a separator."
* "**A group** — that it is of the Fātiḥah and of **every sūrah except Sūrat al-Tawbah**; and it is the
  saying of **al-Thawrī, Ibn al-Mubārak and al-Shāfiʿī**, because it was written in the *muṣḥaf* in the
  script of the rest of the Qurʾān."

The two proofs he cites: **for its being a verse of the sūrahs**, al-Shāfiʿī's chain (Ibn Jurayj from his
father from **Saʿīd b. Jubayr**, on 15:87 *wa-laqad ātaynāka sabʿan mina l-mathānī*): "*It is Umm al-Qurʾān*"
— "and Saʿīd b. Jubayr recited it to me until he finished it, then said: *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* is
**the seventh verse**"; "Saʿīd said: I recited it to **Ibn ʿAbbās** as I recited it to you, and he said:
*bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* is the seventh verse; and Ibn ʿAbbās said: **He stored it up for you, and
did not bring it out for anyone before you.**" **For those who do not count it**, Mālik's chain from **Anas
b. Mālik**: "I stood behind Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq, ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb and ʿUthmān b. ʿAffān — **all of them did
not recite *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* when they began the prayer**"; and **Saʿīd b. Jubayr from Ibn
ʿAbbās**: "**the Messenger of Allah ﷺ did not know the ending of a sūrah until *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni
l-Raḥīm* came down.**" And further, on its being a separator: "**from Ibn Masʿūd** — he said: ***We did not
know the separation between the two sūrahs until *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* came down***; and
**al-Shaʿbī** said: **the Messenger of Allah ﷺ at the beginning wrote in the manner of Quraysh *bi-smika
Allāhumma* until there came down** *wa-qāla rkabū fīhā bismi llāhi majrāhā* (11:41), **so he wrote *bismi
llāh*; until there came down** *quli dʿū llāha awi dʿū l-Raḥmān* (17:110), **so he wrote *bismi llāhi
l-Raḥmān*; until there came down** *innahu min Sulaymāna wa-innahu bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* (27:30),
**so he wrote the like of it.**"

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "The community is agreed that Fātiḥat al-Kitāb is **seven verses**, except what is related
from Ḥusayn al-Juʿfī that it is six — and that is *shādhdh* — and except what is related from **ʿAmr b.
ʿUbayd**, who made *iyyāka naʿbudu* a verse, making it **eight** — and that is *shādh*; and His word
*wa-laqad ātaynāka sabʿan mina l-mathānī* and the ḥadīth *I have divided the prayer* refute both sayings."
He then sets out, in the fifth *masʾalah*, on the basmalah's status:

* **Mālik** — "not a verse of al-Fātiḥah nor of any other" (*sūrah*); **[Ibn al-Mubārak]** — "a verse of
  every sūrah"; **al-Shāfiʿī** — "a verse in al-Fātiḥah, and his saying wavered over the rest of the sūrahs";
  and "there is no disagreement among them that it is a verse of the Qurʾān in Sūrat al-Naml."
* Al-Qurṭubī's verdict: "**the sound of these sayings is Mālik's**, because the Qurʾān is not established by
  isolated reports; its way is only the decisive, unbroken transmission in which there is no disagreement.
  Ibn al-ʿArabī said: *it suffices you that it is not of the Qurʾān that people disagree over it, and the
  Qurʾān is not disagreed over.*"
* The *āyāt*-count of the rest he reports: "**the people of Madinah, Syria and Baṣrah and most of the
  reciters counted *anʿamta ʿalayhim* a verse**… Qatādah from Abū Naḍrah from Abū Hurayrah: *the sixth verse
  is* anʿamta ʿalayhim. **The people of Kūfah among reciters and jurists counted the basmalah and did not
  count *anʿamta ʿalayhim***."
* On ʿAbd Allāh b. Masʿūd's *muṣḥaf* (he did not write the Fātiḥah): the report Abū Bakr al-Anbārī gives from
  al-Aʿmash (from Ibrāhīm) — ʿAbd Allāh was asked "why did you not write Fātiḥat al-Kitāb in your *muṣḥaf*?"
  and said: "**If I wrote it, I would write it with every sūrah**" — al-Anbārī's explanation: "he meant that
  every *rakʿah*'s way is to be opened with Umm al-Qurʾān before the sūrah recited after it; so he said: *I
  dispensed with writing it, I trusted the Muslims' preservation of it, and I would not fix it in one place
  and then have to write it with every sūrah*."
* **Twenty-five words** (Ibn al-ʿArabī, in the first *bāb*): "it is **twenty-five words** that contain all
  the sciences of the Qurʾān."

**Ibn Kathīr.** “There is no disagreement over the view that Al-Fatihah contains seven Ayat. According to
the majority of the reciters of Al-Kufah, a group of the Companions, the Tābiʿīn, and a number of scholars
from the successive generations, the Bismillah is a separate Ayah in its beginning.” And: “The scholars
say that Al-Fatihah consists of twenty-five words, and that it contains one hundred and thirteen letters.”

**The Study Quran.** “There is a debate as to whether the *basmalah* is a verse of each sūrah or an
indication of the beginning of a new sūrah.” And, at v. 7: “According to some accounts, this verse is in
fact two verses, the first of which ends with *hast blessed* (Ṭ).”


### S4 · Virtues & merits of the sūrah (*faḍāʾil al-sūrah*)

**Al-Alūsī.** On the widely-cited *"the Fātiḥah equals two-thirds of the Qurʾān"*: he reports **al-Shihāb's**
attempt (the three *dalālāt* — *muṭābaqah*, *taḍammun*, *iltizām*), calls it "the most wondrous", then gives
his own harmonisations:

> "**The two reports may be reconciled** — that the first [tradition] was first, and the reward was multiplied
> second; **and there is no restriction on the vast mercy**; or **the difference of report is due to the
> difference of circumstance**; or **what equals a thing entire equals two-thirds of it**; or **in one or both
> reports 'the Qurʾān' means the prayer** — as in *wa-qurʾāna l-fajri inna qurʾāna l-fajri kāna mashhūdā*
> (17:78) — **and that differs with people's ranks in their recitation and prayer: so let him reflect.**"

And he cites with report-criticism: "**al-Daylamī in *al-Firdaws*, from Abū al-Dardāʾ**: *'The Fātiḥah of the
Book suffices where nothing of the Qurʾān suffices; and were the Fātiḥah of the Book placed in one pan of the
balance and the Qurʾān in the other, the Fātiḥah of the Book would outweigh the Qurʾān seven times'*" —
"**for what first comes to mind from it is superiority in reward**…" And the reasons given for the name *Umm*:
"**because it is the best of the sūrahs; or because its sanctity is as the sanctity of the whole Qurʾān; or
because it is the refuge of the people of faith; or because it is *muḥkamah*, and the *muḥkamāt* are the
Mother of the Book.**"

**Al-Baghawī** ("a section on the virtue of al-Fātiḥah"). Three reports, with the chains he gives:

> 1. **Abū Hurayrah** — the Prophet ﷺ passed by **Ubayy b. Kaʿb** standing in prayer and called out to him,
> and Ubayy hurried in his prayer and came, and he said to him: *What prevented you, Ubayy, from answering me
> when I called you? Does not Allah say* yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū stajībū li-llāhi wa-li-l-rasūli idhā
> daʿākum limā yuḥyīkum*?* Ubayy said: "By Allah, Messenger of Allah, you shall not call me but I answer,
> even if I am praying." He said: *Would you like me to teach you a sūrah the like of which has not come down
> in the Torah, nor the Gospel, nor the Psalms, nor the Qurʾān?* He said: Yes. He said: *Do not leave the
> gate of the mosque until you know it* — and the Prophet ﷺ walked intending to leave the mosque, and when he
> reached the gate Ubayy said: *The sūrah, Messenger of Allah!* He stopped and said: *How do you recite in
> your prayer?* and Ubayy recited **Umm al-Qurʾān**, and the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: **By the One in whose
> hand is my soul, the like of it has not come down in the Torah, nor the Gospel, nor the Psalms, nor the
> Qurʾān — and it is the Seven Mathānī which Allah, Mighty and Exalted, gave me.** _(Al-Baghawī: this is a
> **ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ** ḥadīth.)_
> 2. **Ibn ʿAbbās** — "while the Messenger of Allah ﷺ had Jibrīl with him, he heard a sound from above, and
> Jibrīl raised his gaze to the sky and said: *This is a gate opened from the sky, never opened before*; a
> **malak** came down from it and came to the Prophet ﷺ and said: **Rejoice in two lights given to you, not
> given to a prophet before you — Fātiḥat al-Kitāb and the closing verses of Sūrat al-Baqarah; you will not
> recite a letter of them but you are given it.**" _(ṣaḥīḥ; Muslim's.)_
> 3. **Abū Hurayrah** — the Prophet ﷺ: "*Whoever prays a prayer in which he does not recite Umm al-Qurʾān,
> it is **deficient (*khidāj*), not complete**.*" — "I said: O Abū Hurayrah, sometimes I am behind an imam;
> and he **gripped my forearm** and said: ***Recite it within yourself, O Persian***; for I heard the
> Messenger of Allah ﷺ say: *Allah, Mighty and Exalted, said: **I have divided the prayer between Me and My
> servant into halves: half of it for Me and half of it for My servant, and My servant shall have what he
> asks**. The Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: **Recite** — when the servant says* al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi
> l-ʿālamīn*, Allah says: My servant has praised Me. When he says* al-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm, *He says: My servant
> has extolled Me. When he says* māliki yawmi l-dīn, *He says: My servant has magnified Me. When he says*
> iyyāka naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn, *Allah says: **this verse is between Me and My servant, and My servant
> shall have what he asks.** When he says* ihdinā l-ṣirāṭa l-mustaqīm… wa-lā l-ḍāllīn, *Allah says: **these
> are for My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks.***" _(ṣaḥīḥ; Muslim's, from Qutaybah from
> Mālik.)_

**Al-Qurṭubī** (first *bāb*, seven *masāʾil*). 1) Tirmidhī from **Ubayy b. Kaʿb** — the Prophet ﷺ: "*Allah
did not send down in the Torah nor in the Gospel the like of Umm al-Qurʾān — and it is the Seven Mathānī, and
it is divided between Me and My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks.*" And the Bukhārī report of
**Abū Saʿīd b. al-Muʿallā** (whose name, al-Qurṭubī notes, is reported variously; he died 74 AH "and was the
first to pray toward the Kaʿbah when it was turned"): "I was praying in the mosque and the Messenger of Allah
ﷺ called me and I did not answer him… I said: *Messenger of Allah, I was praying*; he said: *Did Allah not
say* is'tajībū li-llāhi wa-li-l-rasūli idhā daʿākum*?* Then he said: *I will teach you a sūrah which is the
**greatest sūrah in the Qurʾān** before you leave the mosque*… he took my hand, and when he was about to
leave I said: *Did you not say you would teach me a sūrah which is the greatest sūrah in the Qurʾān?* He
said: **al-Ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn — it is the Seven Mathānī and the Mighty Qurʾān which I was
given.**" And **Mujāhid's** report that "**Iblīs wailed four times** (*ranna*): when he was cursed, when he
was driven out of the Garden, when Muḥammad ﷺ was sent, and **when the Fātiḥah of the Book was sent down, and
it was sent down at Madinah**."

2) On preferment of parts of the Qurʾān over parts: "Those who said there is no preferment — Abū al-Ḥasan
al-Ashʿarī, al-Qāḍī Abū Bakr b. al-Ṭayyib, Abū Ḥātim Muḥammad b. Ḥibbān al-Bustī and a group of jurists — and
it is related from Mālik (Yaḥyā b. Yaḥyā: *the preferment of part of the Qurʾān over part is an error*; and
Mālik disliked that a sūrah be repeated and others not). Their argument: preferment suggests the deficiency
of the less preferred, the essence in all is one, the speech of Allah, in which there is no defect." **Al-Bustī:
"the Ḥadīth means that Allah does not give to the reciter of the Torah and the Gospel the like of the reward
He gives the reciter of Umm al-Qurʾān — a favour from Him to this community."** And those who affirm
preferment — **Isḥāq b. Rāhwayh** and others, and the choice of **al-Qāḍī Abū Bakr b. al-ʿArabī and Ibn
al-Ḥaṣṣār** — "because of the ḥadīth of Abū Saʿīd b. al-Muʿallā and the ḥadīth of Ubayy b. Kaʿb: the Prophet
ﷺ asked him *which verse of the Book of Allah with you is the greatest?* and he said: *Allāhu lā ilāha illā
huwa l-ḥayyu l-qayyūm*, and he struck his chest and said: **Congratulations on the knowledge, O Abū
al-Mundhir** (Bukhārī and Muslim)" — "the preferment is only by the meanings, how wondrous and how many, not
in the attribute [of speaking]; and **this is the truth**." Ibn al-ʿArabī: "the ḥadīth's silence about the
other books, like the scrolls and the Psalms, is because those named are the most excellent of them, and when
a thing is the best of the best it becomes the best of all" — and: "**in al-Fātiḥah there are attributes
that are not in others**, to the point that it is said **all the Qurʾān is in it**; it is twenty-five words
containing all the sciences of the Qurʾān; and of its honour is that Allah divided it between Himself and His
servant, that no act of nearness is valid without it, and that no other act attains its reward." And: "the
meaning of the ḥadīth of Abū Saʿīd (*the greatest sūrah*) is that it is greatest in **reward**, not that part
of the Qurʾān is better than part."

3) ʿAlī's report (Abū ʿAmr al-Dānī's *Kitāb al-Bayān*): the four "suspended by the Throne" verses (above,
S1). 8) Dārimī: **al-Shifāʾ**. 9) **al-Ruqyah** (the ḥadīth of the man who recited over the chief of the
tribe: "*What made you know that it is a ruqyah?*" — "the Imams narrated it; the man said: *Messenger of
Allah, something was cast into my heart*"). 12) **al-Kāfiyah**. And the fifth *masʾalah* of the first *bāb*:
"**al-Muhallab said: the place of the ruqyah in it is only *iyyāka naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn*.** And it was
said: **the whole sūrah is a ruqyah**, because he ﷺ said *What made you know that it is a ruqyah* and did not
say *that there is a ruqyah in it*."

**Ibn Kathīr.** The merits are established by a series of ḥadīths:

* **The greatest sūrah.** Imam Ahmad in the *Musnad*, from Abū Saʿīd bin al-Muʿallā: “I will teach you
  the greatest Surah in the Qur'an before you leave the Masjid” — and it was *al-Ḥamdu lillāhi
  Rabbil-ʿālamīn*; “It is the seven repeated (verses) and the Glorious Qur'an that I was given.” Al-Bukhārī,
  Abū Dāwūd, an-Nasāʾī and Ibn Mājah also recorded it.
* **Nothing like it revealed.** From Ubayy bin Kaʿb: “Would you like me to teach you a Surah the likes of
  which nothing has been revealed in the Tawrah, the Injil, the Zabur or the Furqan?” — and: “By Him in
  Whose Hand is my soul! Allah has never revealed in the Tawrah, the Injil, the Zabur or the Furqan a Surah
  like it. It is the seven repeated verses that I was given.”
* **Divided between Allah and His servant.** At-Tirmidhī and an-Nasāʾī from Ubayy bin Kaʿb: “Allah has
  never revealed in the Tawrah or the Injil anything similar to Umm Al-Qur'an. It is the seven repeated
  verses and it is divided into two halves between Allah and His servant.”
* **The best sūrah.** From Ibn Jābir: “Should I inform you of the best Surah in the Qur'an? … Read, *All
  praise be to Allah, the Lord of the existence*, until you finish it.”
* **Healing.** Al-Bukhārī, in the chapter on the virtues of the Qurʾān, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī: the
  Companion who read *Umm al-Kitāb* as a ruqyah for the poisoned chief, and the tribe's gift of thirty
  sheep. Ibn Kathīr concludes that “some scholars relied on this Hadith as evidence that some Ayat and
  Surahs have more virtues than others”.

**The Study Quran.** “Many sayings of the Prophet Muhammad attribute an exalted status to the Fātiḥah. In
one, the Prophet told a man that he would teach him the greatest sūrah; when asked what it was, the
Prophet responded, *‘It is Praise be to God, the Lord of the worlds, the seven oft-repeated, and the
Mighty Quran [15:87] that I was given’* (IK, W).” The ḥadīth qudsī: *“I have divided the prayer between
Myself and My servant, and My servant shall have that for which he prays…”* (Ṭ, Ṭs). And: “In one the
Prophet says, *‘By Him in Whose Hand lies my soul, in neither the Torah, the Psalms, the Gospel, nor the
Quran was the like of it revealed’* (IK, Sh). According to another saying attributed by some to Mujāhid (Q)
and by others to the Prophet himself (Sh), *‘Satan was frightened four times: when he was cursed by God;
when he was expelled from the Garden; when Muhammad was sent [as a messenger]; and when the Fātiḥah was
revealed.’*” The ʿAlī report is given: “The whole of the Quran is contained in the Fātiḥah, the whole of
the Fātiḥah in the *basmalah*, the whole of the *basmalah* in the *bāʾ*, and the whole of the *bāʾ* in the
diacritical point under the *bāʾ*” — which “can be understood to represent the first drop of ink from the
Divine Pen (*al-qalam*; see 68:1c; 96:4c) with which God wrote the archetypes of all things upon the
Preserved Tablet (*al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ*; see 85:22c) before their descent into the realm of creation.”


### S5 · Central theme & summary of contents (*maqāṣid*)

**Al-Alūsī.** "**The Fātiḥah comprises the aims of the meanings in the Qurʾān** — praise of Allah as He is
worthy of it, worship by command and prohibition, and the warning (*waʿīd*)" — and in full:

> "**Praise** is evident; **worship** either from *al-ḥamdu li-llāh*, because it is for teaching and an
> imperative is posited that it conveys — and a positive command entails, in general, the prohibition of its
> opposite — **or from** *ihdinā l-ṣirāṭa l-mustaqīm* **if what is meant is the religion of Islam, or from
> the positing of 'say:* bismi llāh*', or from the [imperative force of] the postponed verb**; **and either
> from** *iyyāka naʿbudu* — **for it is an informing of their specialising Him with worship** — **and from**
> *anʿamta ʿalayhim ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim* **or from 'the day of the religion', that is, the requital**
> — **the promise and the threat**."

And the famous ascent: "**the meanings of the Books are in the Qurʾān, the meanings of the Qurʾān in the
Fātiḥah, the meanings of the Fātiḥah in the *basmalah*, and the meanings of the *basmalah* in the *bāʾ***"
(quoted from what al-Suyūṭī carried from al-Sirminī). And the four kinds of knowledge:

> "**(1) The science of *uṣūl*** — its knots the knowledge of Allah and His attributes, pointed to by *rabbi
> l-ʿālamīn, al-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*; the knowledge of prophethood, intended by *anʿamta ʿalayhim*; and the return,
> indicated by *māliki yawmi l-dīn*. **(2) The science of the branches (*furūʿ*)** — its base acts of worship,
> intended by *iyyāka naʿbudu*, bodily and financial; and these need the affairs of livelihood, transactions
> and marriages, and these need governments — **so the branches are established on the roots. (3) The science
> by which perfection is obtained — ethics — the noblest of it the arrival at the Presence and travelling the
> path of uprightness**, pointed to by *iyyāka nastaʿīn* and *ihdinā l-ṣirāṭa l-mustaqīm*. **(4) The science of
> narratives and reports of the past nations, the happy and the wretched, and the promise and threat joined to
> them** — intended by *anʿamta ʿalayhim ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*."

And the ordering of the sūrah: "**the first is learned from the beginning of the sūrah to *yawmi l-dīn*, the
second from *iyyāka naʿbudu* and after it, the travelling of the straight path from *ihdinā*… and the
inspection from *anʿamta ʿalayhim* and what follows.**" And the note on al-Bayḍāwī's division: "**he made
*praise* a partner to *worship*, though reason gives it no ruling — perhaps as a hint that the gratitude of a
benefactor is obligatory by reason, observing the Muʿtazilī doctrine, and al-Bayḍāwī did not mind that**" —
and al-Alūsī notes: "**and the three [classes] of people: the many whom desire and fear drive; the middle who
hope and fear; and the elect, few, who have intimacy and awe — by the three, guidance to the interests of this
life and the next is complete.**"

**Al-Baghawī.** From the last report above, the sūrah's sense is "**half of it for Me and half of it for My
servant**": the first three verses **for Allah** (His praise, extolling and magnification), the fourth
(*iyyāka naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn*) **between Him and His servant**, and the last three (from *ihdinā
l-ṣirāṭa l-mustaqīm* to the end) **for the servant**; "and My servant shall have what he asks".

**Al-Qurṭubī.** From the ḥadīth of the division (Bukhārī/Muslim; and al-Qurṭubī's reading of it in the fifth
*masʾalah* of the basmalah chapter):

> "*Allah, Mighty and Exalted, said: I have divided the prayer between Me and My servant into halves, and My
> servant shall have what he asks. When the servant says* al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn, *Allah says: My
> servant has praised Me. When he says* al-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm, *He says: My servant has extolled Me. When he
> says* māliki yawmi l-dīn, *He says: My servant has magnified Me — and once: this is Mine, and to My servant
> [what remains]. When he says* iyyāka naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn, *He says: this is between Me and My
> servant, and My servant shall have what he asks. When he says* ihdinā l-ṣirāṭa l-mustaqīm, ṣirāṭa lladhīna
> anʿamta ʿalayhim ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim wa-lā l-ḍāllīn, *He says: this is for My servant, and My
> servant shall have what he asks.*"

His exposition: "His word *I have divided the prayer* means **al-Fātiḥah**; He called it *prayer* because
prayer is not valid except by it. **He made the first three verses for Himself and chose them for Himself**,
and the Muslims did not differ over them; then **the fourth verse He made between Him and His servant**,
because it comprises the servant's humbling himself and seeking help from Him, and that comprises the
magnifying of Allah; then **three verses completing seven**." And the deduction from the wording "these are
for My servant" (not "these two"), from Mālik's *Muwaṭṭaʾ* — "this proves that *anʿamta ʿalayhim* is a
verse"; Ibn Bukayr said: Mālik said "*anʿamta ʿalayhim* is a verse, then the seventh verse to its end". And
the further statement he gives (in 1:6, the twenty-seventh *masʾalah*): "one of the scholars said: **Allah
placed the magnitude of supplication, and its sum, in this sūrah: half of it is the gathering of praise and
half of it is the gathering of needs**; and He made this supplication better than the supplication a
supplicant makes himself, **because this speech was spoken by the Lord of the worlds — so you supplicate with
a supplication that is His speech.**" And (in 1:2, the seventh *masʾalah*): "the Fātiḥah comprises **tawḥīd,
worship, admonition and reminder**"; and al-Qurṭubī's own view of the two halves: "**al-ḥamd is more general
than al-shukr** … praise of the praised for His attributes without prior beneficence, while *shukr* is praise
for what beneficence has brought; and on this limit our scholars said: *al-ḥamd* is more general because it
includes praise, extolling and thanks, while requital is specific to one who has done you a kindness."

**As-Saʿdī.** For all its brevity, this sūrah comprises what no other sūrah of the Qurʾān comprises. It contains:

* **The three kinds of divine oneness.** *Tawḥīd al-rubūbiyyah* — taken from "Lord of the worlds". *Tawḥīd al-ilāhiyyah*, the singling out of Allah in worship — taken from the name *Allāh* and from "You alone we worship and You alone we seek help". *Tawḥīd al-asmāʾ wa-l-ṣifāt*, affirming the attributes of perfection for Allah, which He affirmed for Himself and His Messenger affirmed for Him — without denial (*taʿṭīl*), without likening (*tamthīl*) and without comparison (*tashbīh*) — indicated by the word "all praise".
* **Prophethood**, in "Guide us to the straight path", since that is impossible without the message.
* **Recompense for deeds**, in "Master of the Day of Judgement", and that the recompense is just, since *al-dīn* means recompense in justice.
* **Divine decree (*qadar*)**, and that the servant is an agent in reality — against the Qadariyyah and the Jabriyyah.
* **The refutation of all people of innovation and error**, in "Guide us to the straight path", since it is knowledge of the truth and action upon it, and every innovator and every astray one is contrary to that.
* **Sincerity of the religion for Allah**, in worship and in seeking help, in "You alone we worship and You alone we seek help".

**Ibn Kathīr.** In “The Summary of Al-Fatihah” he writes that the sūrah “contains seven Ayat including the
praise and thanks of Allah, glorifying Him and praising Him by mentioning His most Beautiful Names and
most high Attributes. It also mentions the Hereafter, which is the Day of Resurrection, and directs
Allah's servants to ask of Him, invoking Him and declaring that all power and strength comes from Him. It
also calls to the sincerity of the worship of Allah alone, singling Him out in His divinity, believing in
His perfection, being free from the need of any partners, having no rivals nor equals.”

It “directs the believers to invoke Allah to guide them to the straight path, which is the true religion,
and to help them remain on that path in this life, and to pass over the actual Sirat on the Day of
Judgment”; it “encourages performing good deeds, so that the believers will be in the company of the
good-doers on the Day of Resurrection”; and it “warns against following the paths of misguidance… including
those who have earned the anger and those who were led astray.”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “This Surah comprises seven verses. Of these, the first three are in praise of
Allah, while the last three contain a request or a prayer on the part of man, which Allah himself has, in
His infinite mercy, taught him. The verse in between the two sets has both the features — there is an
aspect of praise, and another of prayer.” And, at the close: “the prayer for the straight path is the
essence of the Surah al-Fatihah. Since knowing and following the straight path is the real knowledge and
the real achievement in this mortal world, a mistake in picking it up right takes peoples and nations to
ruins.”

**The Study Quran.** “The Fātiḥah is often believed to be a synthesis of the Quran's message and to be its
most important sūrah… It is a prayer that encapsulates all the metaphysical and eschatological realities
of which human beings must remain conscious; God asks human beings to recite it because it contains in
principle the nature of God and the disposition God wishes them to have toward Him. The Fātiḥah thus has a
threefold structure: the first three verses deal with the nature of God, the middle verse deals with the
relationship between God and human beings, and the last three verses deal with the various states of human
beings.”


### S6 · Thematic coherence & connection (*munāsabah · naẓm*)

**Al-Alūsī.** On the wisdom of the Qurʾān's being divided into sūrahs — **contrary to al-Zarkashī** — "**that
it be more lively for the reciter and more rousing to acquisition**; like the traveller: when he cuts a mile
or a league, that gives him relief and he is cheered for the road. **And when the memoriser takes up a sūrah,
he believes he has taken from Allah's Book a self-contained lot, so what he has memorised grows great in his
eyes. And also: a genus under which species and kinds are gathered is finer than to have under it a single
chapter** — **and in that is the realisation of each sūrah by itself being a miracle and a sign of Allah's
signs**; and the wisdom in their being long and short is more apparent than to be hidden."

**Al-Qurṭubī.** On 1:3 (the thirteenth *masʾalah*): "He described Himself after *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* as *al-Raḥmān
al-Raḥīm*, because when His being described as *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* contains **intimidation**, He joined it with
*al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm*, which contains **encouragement**, so as to join in His attributes fear of Him and hope
in Him, and so be a greater aid to His obedience and a stronger restraint — as He said: *nabbiʾ ʿibādī annī
anā l-ghafūru l-raḥīm, wa-anna ʿadhābī huwa l-ʿadhābu l-alīm* (15:49–50), and *ghāfiri l-dhanbi wa-qābili
l-tawbi shadīdi l-ʿiqāb* (40:3)" — with the Muslim ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah: "*Were the believer to know what
is with Allah of punishment, no one would hope for His Garden; and were the unbeliever to know what is with
Allah of mercy, no one would despair of His Garden.*" And on 1:5 (the twenty-third *masʾalah*): "He **returned
from the absent to the address** (*iltifāt*)" — "for from the beginning of the sūrah to here it is report
about Allah and praise of Him"; with the parallel of 76:21–22 (*wa-saqāhum rabbuhum sharāban ṭahūrā… inna
hādhā kāna lakum jazāʾan*) and its reverse in 10:22 (*ḥattā idhā kuntum fī l-fulki wa-jarayna bihim*).

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** The sūrah is read as one argument across the three tenses of a man's life: “A man's
life is subject to three states of time — past, present and future. The first two verses… remind man
that, as far as his past and present are concerned, he owes everything to Allah alone… The third verse:
*Master of the Day of Judgment* tells him that in the future too he will have to depend on Allah alone…
The three verses having made it clear that man is totally and absolutely dependent on Allah in all the
three states of his life, it logically and naturally leads to the conclusion that Allah alone is worthy of
being worshipped.”

**The Study Quran.** On the ordering of the Names: “in the progression of Divine Names from Allāh to
al-Raḥmān, al-Raḥīm, and al-Mālik (or al-Malik) there is a descent from the highest level of the Divine
Essence down to those levels that are in direct relation to the created order… In this sense, the
progression of Names in vv. 1–4 reflects the descent of the created order from the Divine Principle. The
second half of the Fātiḥah then pertains to the ascent from the created order back to the Divine
Principle.” And on v. 3: “Positioned between v. 2, which alludes to God being the Sovereign over all
dimensions of space, both seen and unseen, and v. 4, which alludes to God being the Master of all time,
since all things end on the Day of Judgment, this verse indicates that God's Mercy encompasses and
interpenetrates all time and all space, as in 7:156: *My Mercy encompasses all things.*”


## 1:1 — بِسْمِ اللَّهِ الرَّحْمَٰنِ الرَّحِيمِ

*[context]* In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

### V1 · Qurʾānic text & translation

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "The First — the scholars said: *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* is **an oath from our Lord**
which He sent down at the head of every sūrah: He swears to His servants that this which I have set out for
you, My servants, in this sūrah is true, and that I will fulfil for you all that I have guaranteed in this
sūrah of My promise, My kindness and My goodness"; and it is "of what Allah sent down in our Book and upon
this community in particular **after Sulaymān**" — and "some scholars said: *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*
comprises the whole of the Sharīʿah, because it points to the Essence and to the Attributes; and this is
correct."

**Ibn ʿAbbās (as rendered here).** The verse is given only as the rendering *"In the name of Allah, the
Beneficent, the Merciful"*; the work passes on to the commentary of 1:2 without a separate exposition of
the basmalah.

**Ibn Kathīr.** “The Companions started the Book of Allah with Bismillah”, and “the scholars also agree
that Bismillah is a part of an Ayah in Surat An-Naml (chapter 27). They disagree over whether it is a
separate Ayah before every Surah, or if it is an Ayah, or a part of an Ayah, included in every Surah where
the Bismillah appears in its beginning.” The positions he reports: that the basmalah is an āyah of every
sūrah except al-Barāʾah — attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn ʿUmar, Ibn az-Zubayr, Abū Hurayrah and ʿAlī, and
among the Tābiʿīn to ʿAṭāʾ, Ṭāwūs, Saʿīd bin Jubayr, Makḥūl and az-Zuhrī; the view of ʿAbdullāh bin
al-Mubārak, ash-Shāfiʿī, Aḥmad bin Ḥanbal, Isḥāq bin Rāhwayh and Abū ʿUbayd al-Qāsim bin Sallām; and the
opposing view of Mālik and Abū Ḥanīfah and their followers, “that Bismillah is not an Ayah in Al-Fatihah
or any other Surah”; while Dāwūd held it “a separate Ayah in the beginning of every Surah, not part of the
Surah itself”.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “There is consensus of all the Muslims on the fact that *Bismillah al-Raḥmān
al-Raḥīm* is a verse of the Holy Qur'an, being a part of the Surah al-Naml (The Ant); and there is also an
agreement that this verse is written at the head of every Surah except the Surah al-Taubah. But there is a
difference of opinion among the Mujtahids as to whether this verse is an integral part of the Surah
al-Fatihah or of all the Surahs or not. According to the great Imam Abū Ḥanīfah, it is not an integral part
of any Surah except al-Naml; rather it is in itself an independent verse of the Holy Qur'an which has been
revealed for being placed at the beginning of every Surah in order to separate and distinguish one Surah
from another.”

**The Study Quran.** “All but one of the 114 sūrahs of the Quran begin with *In the Name of God, the
Compassionate, the Merciful*, known as the *basmalah* in Arabic, which also appears in 27:30. There is a
debate as to whether the *basmalah* is a verse of each sūrah or an indication of the beginning of a new
sūrah.”

### V2 · Running exposition (*bayān*)

**Al-Alūsī.** The basmalah's own *abḥāth* run in three researches (he labels them *البحث*):

* **First research — is the basmalah one of the peculiarities of this community?** "**The learned Abū Bakr
  al-Tūnisī** transmitted **the consensus of the scholars of every religious community that Allah, Exalted,
  opened every scripture with it**; and **al-Suyūṭī** related from **al-Sirminī** — '*bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni
  l-Raḥīm* is the opening of every scripture' — and this transmitter went to the position that the basmalah is
  **one of the particularities**, because it is reported *'that he — ﷺ — used to write* ***bi-smika Allāhummā
  ('in Your name, O Allah')*** *until there came down* *bismi llāhi majrāhā* (11:41) *and he ordered the writing
  of* *bismi llāh*, *until there came down* *quli dʿū llāha awi dʿū l-Raḥmān* (17:110) *and he ordered the
  writing of* *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*, *until the verse of al-Naml came down and he ordered the writing
  of* *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm.'*" **Al-Alūsī's answer**: the argument does not entail the negation of the
  other scriptures' having it (their knowledge then may not have been given); what is peculiar to the Qurʾān is
  **the Arabic wording in this arrangement** — the ancient scriptures being non-Arabic (contrary to
  al-Ghayṭī), and what of them is in the Qurʾān is translated; "so perhaps these very words have a part in the
  comprehension of all the meanings, so they would not be in other than the Qurʾān as al-Sirminī supposed,
  even if there be a basmalah there."
* **Third research — the meaning of *bismi llāh*** (deferred to V8 below, and V2's own point): "**the *bāʾ* is
  either for *istiʿānah*, or *muṣāḥabah*, or adhesion, or elevation, or redundant, or for an oath** — the last
  four being nothing."

**Al-Jalālayn.** The commentary opens on the basmalah — “*In the Name of God*” — with the sense of
beginning: “*I commence and I recite*”. The three names are then unfolded:

* **In the Name of God** — “the name of a thing is that by which it can be known.”
* **Allāh** — “a name for the divine essence *qua* essence absolutely, without taking into consideration
  that it may be qualified by the attributes, or indeed taking into consideration that it may not be so
  qualified.” And: “It — Allāh — is the Greatest Name of God.”
* **the Compassionate** — “the one who causes existence and perfection to flow upon all things in the
  measure that divine wisdom requires and to the capacity of the receiving entities (*qawābil*) from the
  outset of their creation.”
* **the Merciful** (*al-Raḥīm*) — “the one who bestows the spiritual perfection (*kamāl maʿnawī*) that has
  been earmarked for the human species in the end — in the Hereafter.”

Hence, the commentary notes, “they say *O You Who are the Compassionate One of this world and of the
Hereafter* (*yā raḥmān al-dunyā wa-l-ākhira*), but say *O Merciful One of the Hereafter* (*raḥīm
al-ākhira*); the meaning then is in the perfect human all-encompassing form, general and specific mercy,
which is the locus of manifestation of the divine essence and the greatest truth with the totality of
attributes.”

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The *basmalah*'s sense is gathered from the reports and the sense of the *bāʾ*: "**the
eleventh** — the disagreement over the meaning of the addition of *ism*: Quṭrub said it was added to
magnify His mention; al-Akhfash said it was added that the mention might pass from the ruling of an oath to
the purpose of seeking blessing, for the basis of the speech is *bi-llāh*." "**The twelfth** — whether the
*bāʾ* came on the sense of a command (*begin with the name of Allah*) or the sense of a report (*I have begun
with the name of Allah*): the first for the reciters, the second for al-Zajjāj; and on both, *bismi llāh* is
in the accusative position. And it is said: the sense is *my beginning is with the name of Allah*, so *bismi
llāh* is in the nominative as the predicate of the subject." "The ninth — our scholars said: **in it there is
a refutation of the *Qadariyyah* and whoever says that their acts are decreed *for* them** … the argument
against them is that **Allah has commanded us at the beginning of every act to open with it** … so the sense
of *bismi llāh* is *bi-llāh*; and the sense of *bi-llāh* is: **by His creating and His decreeing is reach
what is reached**."; and "some said: *bismi llāh* means *I have begun with Allah's help, His granting of
success and His blessing* — and this is a teaching from Allah to His servants, that they may mention His name
at the opening of recitation and other things, so that the opening be with the blessing of Allah."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The exposition of the basmalah is built on what the preposition *bāʾ* requires: “the *bāʾ* in
‘*bismi llāh*’ requires a verb which it brings in, and there is no verb present with it” — but the hearer
needs no verb, because the speaker's purpose is known from the act he is beginning. Hence “the sense of a
man's words, when he says ‘*bismi llāh*’ and then begins to recite a sūrah, is *I recite with the naming of
Allah*”; and so at rising or sitting, “*I rise with the naming of Allah, I sit with the naming of Allah*”.
He grounds this in the report of Ibn ʿAbbās (no. 138, and again no. 139):

> **Ibn ʿAbbās** — Abū Kurayb → ʿUthmān b. Saʿīd → Bishr b. ʿUmāra → Abū Rawq → al-Ḍaḥḥāk → Ibn ʿAbbās:
> “The first thing Jibrīl brought down to Muḥammad was: *O Muḥammad, say: I seek refuge in the Hearing,
> the Knowing, from Satan the accursed*; then he said: *Say: In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate,
> the Most Merciful*. Jibrīl said to him: Say *bismi llāh*, O Muḥammad — that is, *recite with the mention
> of Allah your Lord, and rise and sit with the mention of Allah*.”
> _(Corpus editor's appraisal: this chain — Bishr b. ʿUmāra → Abū Rawq → al-Ḍaḥḥāk → Ibn ʿAbbās — is weak,
> as he states at no. 137 and repeats throughout.)_

He then sharpens the point against the objection that one should rather say “*bi-llāhi l-Raḥmāni
l-Raḥīm*”: the sense is not “I rise *by* Allah” but “I begin *with the naming of* Allah and His mention
before everything”, since “the servants were commanded to begin their affairs with the *naming* of Allah,
not with reporting of His greatness and His attributes, as they were commanded regarding slaughter,
hunting, eating, drinking and the rest of their acts”. He clinches it with the consensus: “there is no
disagreement among all the scholars of the community that if a man said at his slaughter ‘*bi-llāh*’ and
did not say ‘*bismi llāh*’, he has left what is prescribed for him at the slaughter” — which proves that
“*bismi llāh*” does not mean “*bi-llāh*”, nor is the name identical with the Named.

**As-Saʿdī.** The sense is: *I begin with every name belonging to Allah*, since the word *ism* is a
singular in construct (*mufrad muḍāf*) and therefore covers all the most beautiful names. **Allāh** is
the one worshipped (*al-maʾlūh*), the one deserving to be singled out with worship, because He is
characterised by the attributes of divinity, which are the attributes of perfection. **al-Raḥmān,
al-Raḥīm** are two names indicating that He possesses the vast and great mercy that encompasses
everything and reaches every living being, and that He has written for the God-fearing who follow His
prophets and messengers: theirs is mercy unqualified, and all besides them have a share of it.

**Ibn Kathīr.** The three names of the basmalah are expounded:

* **Allah** — “the Name of the Lord, the Exalted. It is said that Allah is the Greatest Name of Allah,
  because it is referred to when describing Allah by the various attributes”, as in 59:22–24: *He is Allah,
  beside Whom none has the right to be worshipped but He, the Knower of the unseen and the seen… To Him
  belong the Best Names*; and 7:180: *And all the Most Beautiful Names belong to Allah, so call on Him by
  them*; and 17:110: *Invoke Allah or invoke the Most Gracious… to Him belong the Best Names.*
* **Ar-Raḥmān, Ar-Raḥīm** — “two names derived from *Ar-Rahmah* (the mercy), but *Raḥmān* has more
  meanings that pertain to mercy than *Ar-Raḥīm*”; Ibn Jarīr reports consensus on this. Al-Qurṭubī said:
  “The proof that these names are derived (from *Ar-Rahmah*) is what At-Tirmidhī recorded — and graded
  Ṣaḥīḥ — from ʿAbdur-Raḥmān bin ʿAwf that he heard the Messenger of Allah ﷺ say: *Allah the Exalted
  said, ‘I Am Ar-Raḥmān. I created the Raḥam (womb, i.e. family relations) and derived a name for it from
  My Name…’*” — “This is a text that indicates the derivation.” And al-Qurṭubī again: “The Arabs denied the
  name *Ar-Raḥmān*, because of their ignorance about Allah and His attributes.”
* **The scope of each name** — “He is *Ar-Raḥmān* with all creation and *Ar-Raḥīm* with the believers.”
  Ibn Kathīr gathers: “*Ar-Raḥmān* carries a broader scope of meanings pertaining to the mercy of Allah
  with His creation in both lives. Meanwhile, *Ar-Raḥīm* is exclusively for the believers”, citing
  *And He is ever Raḥīm (merciful) to the believers* (33:43), while noting the supplication “*The Raḥmān
  and the Raḥīm of this life and the Hereafter*”. He concludes: “Allah first mentioned His Name — Allah —
  that is exclusively His and described this Name by *Ar-Raḥmān*, which no one else is allowed to use.”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** The merits of the basmalah: “It was a custom in the Age of Ignorance (*Jāhiliyyah*)
before the advent of Islam that people began everything they did with the names of their idols or gods. It
was to eradicate this practice that the first verse of the Holy Qur'an which the Archangel Jibrāʾīl brought
down to the Holy Prophet ﷺ commanded him to begin the Qur'an with the name of Allah: *Read with the name
of your Lord* (96:1).” Al-Suyūṭī is cited that all the divine books begin with the basmalah, and the two
views are reconciled: “all the divine books share the common trait of beginning with the name of Allah,
but the words *Bismillah al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm* are peculiar to the Holy Qur'an”, since the Prophet ﷺ used
*Bismika Allāhumma* until this verse was revealed and then adopted it. And: “The Holy Prophet ﷺ has said
that no important work receives the blessings of Allah unless it is begun with His name.” The work draws
the sense of the practice: “By instructing man to begin everything with the name of Allah, Islam has given
to the whole of his life an orientation towards Allah… Thus, all the economic and worldly activities of
man, each movement and gesture, becomes transformed into an act of worship… a regular alchemy, transmuting
the profane (*dunyā*) into the sacred (*dīn*).”

**The Study Quran.**

* **On the consecration of acts.** “The *basmalah*, or its shortened form, *In the Name of God*, is
  employed by pious Muslims to consecrate all licit actions, since from a Quranic perspective all things
  should be performed for the sake of God and in His Name.”
* **On the implied verb.** “The preposition *in* (*bi*) could imply a verb at the beginning of the
  sentence, such as ‘I begin’ or ‘I seek help’ in the Name of God (R, Ṭb), or a noun, such as ‘The beginning
  of the discussion is in the Name of God’ (R). Alternately, these same phrases could be seen as following
  the verse, for example, ‘In the Name of God I begin’ (R).” And: “*In the Name of God* is also understood
  to mean, ‘I began in the Name of God, so you too begin,’ as if God were saying, ‘I began through My Name,
  was united with My Name, and have commenced in My Name, so begin through My Name, unite with My Name, and
  commence in My Name’ (My).”
* **On the name Allāh.** “God renders *Allāh*, which according to some is a unique word with no root and
  according to others derives from the word *al-Ilāh*, or ‘the Divine’ (Ṭb). The Quranic usage of *Allāh*
  indicates that it was already known to the pre-Islamic Arabs, as in 43:87… *Wert thou to ask them, ‘Who
  created you?’ they would surely say, ‘God (Allāh)’* (cf. 29:61, 63; 31:25; 39:38).” Its rank: “*Allāh* is
  not only the most universal and all-embracing Name, but also the most specific of the Divine Names in
  that it cannot be used to describe any being other than God, whereas some other Divine Names may
  (al-Ghazzālī, *Divine Names*). Some refer to *Allāh* as the All-Encompassing Name, which comprises all of
  the Names and Attributes of God (Bq); see 112:1c.”
* **On the order of the three Names.** “The ordering of the three Divine Names in the *basmalah* can be
  seen as an allusion to the levels of Divinity in relation to the created order: God (*Allāh*) refers to
  the Divine Essence, Principle, or Self; the Compassionate (*al-Raḥmān*) refers to the unity of God's
  Attributes; and the Merciful (*al-Raḥīm*) to the unity of God's acts (K).”

### V4 · Qurʾān cross-references (*tafsīr al-Qurʾān bi-l-Qurʾān*)

**Al-Alūsī.** The verses that carry the discussion: **11:41** (*bismi llāhi majrāhā* — Nūḥ's words, basis of
the historic-phase argument and of the *muṣāḥabah* reading of the *bāʾ*); **27:30** (*innahu min Sulaymāna
wa-innahu bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* — the scriptural model, and the "writing" argument); **17:110**
(*quli dʿū llāha awi dʿū l-Raḥmān* — the phase in which the Name was added); **96:1** (*iqraʾ bi-smi
rabbika* — the opening revelation, on which al-Rāzī built a proof; see V7); **15:87** (*sabʿan mina
l-mathānī* — the seven; the S2 and S3 arguments turn on it); **7:128** (*istaʿīnū bi-llāhi wa-ṣbirū* — cited
for *istiʿānah* with the Name); **35:32** (*fa-minhum ẓālimun li-nafsihi* — the three classes, in the
letter-scheme); **15:44** (the seven gates of Jahannam) and **77:30** (*inṭaliqū ilā ẓillin dhī thalāthi
shuʿab*) — in the letter discussion; **15:49–50** (*nabbiʾ ʿibādī…*) — for the mixed brightness of the
Fātiḥah.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The verses by which the basmalah's questions are resolved: 27:30 (*innahu min Sulaymāna
wa-innahu bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*) for its revelation-form; 17:110 (*wa-lā tajhar bi-ṣalātika wa-lā
tukhāfit bihā*) for the silent recitation; 96:1 (*iqraʾ bi-smi rabbika*) for the writing of the *alif*; 11:41
(*bismi llāhi majrāhā wa-mursāhā*) and 6:118 for naming at every act; 25:60 and 13:30 (*wa-hum yakfurūna
bi-l-Raḥmān*) for *al-Raḥmān*'s being known to the Arabs; and 17:110 (*quli dʿū llāha awi dʿū l-Raḥmān*) for
its being peculiar to Allah.

**Ibn Kathīr.** 59:22–24 and 7:180 and 17:110 for the name *Allah* and the Best Names; 33:43 for
*Raḥīm* and the believers; 25:59 and 20:5 (*Ar-Raḥmān rose over the Throne*) to show that “His mercy
encompasses all of His creation”; 43:45; and for the meaning of *al-rajīm*, 67:5, 37:6–10, 15:16–18.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** 96:1 — *Read with the name of your Lord* — as the command from which the practice of
beginning with Allah's name comes.

**The Study Quran.** 27:30 for the basmalah's other occurrence; 43:87 and 29:61, 63; 31:25; 39:38 for the
name *Allāh* among the pre-Islamic Arabs; 112:1c, 68:1c, 96:4c, 85:22c, 3:7, 13:39 and 43:4 in the sūrah
introduction.

### V5 · Prophetic traditions (ḥadīth)

**Al-Alūsī.** He gathers and **weighs** the basmalah's ḥadīths — reporting them with their chains as he finds
them, then giving his verdict:

> **1. Umm Salamah** — via **al-Shāfiʿī ← Ibn Jurayj ← Abū Mulaykah ← Umm Salamah**: "**The Messenger of Allah
> ﷺ recited Fātiḥat al-Kitāb and counted** *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* **a verse**, *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi
> rabbi l-ʿālamīn* **a verse**, *al-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* **a verse**, *māliki yawmi l-dīn* **a verse**, *iyyāka
> naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn* **a verse**, *ihdinā l-ṣirāṭa l-mustaqīm* **a verse**, *ṣirāṭa lladhīna anʿamta
> ʿalayhim ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim wa-lā l-ḍāllīn* **a verse**." — **Al-Alūsī**: "**Abū Mulaykah's hearing
> from Umm Salamah is not established**; and granting the contemporaneity, **this wording is not found in the
> well-known [books]**, and he perhaps related by meaning, as appears to him, from some of the following
> reports" — he then cites the four-witness *taqṭīʿ* reports: **Abū ʿUbayd, Aḥmad and Abū Dāwūd**: "**the
> Messenger of Allah ﷺ used to cut his recitation verse by verse** — *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* •
> *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn* • *al-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* • *māliki yawmi l-dīn*"; **Ibn al-Anbārī and
> al-Bayhaqī** with "then he would stop"; **Ibn Khuzaymah and al-Ḥākim**: "the Prophet ﷺ recited in prayer
> *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* and counted it a verse, *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn* two, *al-Raḥmāni
> l-Raḥīm* three verses, *māliki yawmi l-dīn* four, and said **'thus *iyyāka naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn*' and
> joined five fingers**"; **al-Dāraquṭnī**: "he would recite *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*, *al-ḥamdu
> li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn* to its end, cutting it verse by verse and counting as the bedouins count, **and he
> counted *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* and did not count *ʿalayhim***." **Al-Alūsī's verdict on all
> four**: "**they prove only that it is a verse — and that we concede — but of the Qurʾān, not [that it is] of
> al-Fātiḥah**: and likewise the third and fourth reports prove only that he recited it in prayer and counted
> it because of stopping on it — which is also conceded by us — **it being the first verse of the Qurʾān, and
> the second being *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn***, and so on to the fifth — and he joined the fingers
> and the speech was cut off. **And the fourth report is not an explicit text either**… **and all that is
> established from this report is that the basmalah is a verse of the Qurʾān — which the two sides concede;
> as for its being of al-Fātiḥah, that is a thorn-bush to be traversed.**"
> **2. Abū Hurayrah** — **Saʿīd al-Maqburī ← his father ← Abū Hurayrah**: "**Fātiḥat al-Kitāb is seven
> verses, the first of them *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm***" — **al-Alūsī**: "**al-Ṭabarānī, Ibn Mardawayh
> and al-Bayhaqī** reported it in the wording: *'al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn* — seven verses, *bismi
> llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* is one of them; and it is *al-Sabʿ al-Mathānī* and *al-Qurʾān al-ʿAẓīm*, and it is
> Umm al-Qurʾān and it is Fātiḥat al-Kitāb'"; and **al-Dāraquṭnī**: "**When you recite *al-Ḥamd*, then recite
> *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* — it is Umm al-Qurʾān, Umm al-Kitāb, and the Seven Mathānī, and *bismi
> llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* is one of its verses**." — **Al-Alūsī's reading (his taʾwīl)**: "**the sense of the
> first report is: *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn* to the end [are] seven verses, and the Ḥanafīs said
> this**; and the Prophet, noting the listeners' supposing, from the omission of the basmalah with that former
> doubt, that it is not a verse of the Qurʾān, **removed that supposition in a telling way, saying: *bismi
> llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* is one of them — i.e., like one of them in being a verse of the Qurʾān.** And the
> sense of the second: if you mean to recite *al-Ḥamd* to its end, recite before it the basmalah…."
> **3. Abū Burdah ← his father** (al-Thaʿlabī, with his chain): "The Prophet ﷺ: *'Shall I not tell you of a
> verse that has not come down on anyone after Solomon son of David other than me?'* I said, 'Yes.' He said:
> *'With what do you open the Qurʾān when you open the prayer?'* I said, '*bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*.' He
> said: ***'It is it.'***" — and the rest of al-Thaʿlabī's reports: **Jaʿfar b. Muḥammad ← his father ← Jābir**
> ("*How do you say when you stand to prayer?*" — "*al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn*" — "*Say: bismi llāhi
> l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*"); **Umm Salamah** (he used to recite basmalah then al-Ḥamd); **ʿAlī** (he would open the
> sūrah with it and said, **"whoever omits its recitation has made a deficiency"**); **Saʿīd b. Jubayr ← Ibn
> ʿAbbās** on 15:87 (*ātaināka sabʿan mina l-mathānī*): "**Fātiḥat al-Kitāb**" — and it was said to Ibn ʿAbbās,
> **"Where is the seventh?"** and he said, "***bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm***"; **Abū Hurayrah** (the
> Prophet: "**When you recite Umm al-Qurʾān, do not leave *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*, for it is one of its
> verses**"); the *qasamtu al-ṣalāh* ḥadīth in the wording: "**I have divided the prayer between Me and My
> servant into two halves. When he says *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*, Allah says: My servant has magnified
> Me. When he says *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn*: My servant has praised Me. *al-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*: My
> servant has extolled Me. *māliki yawmi l-dīn*: My servant has committed his affair to Me. *iyyāka naʿbudu
> wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn*: This is between Me and My servant. *ihdinā l-ṣirāṭa l-mustaqīm*: This is for My
> servant, and My servant shall have what he asks**"); the report of **Abū Hurayrah** in the mosque (a man
> began with *al-ḥamdu li-llāh* and the Prophet said: "**O man, you have cut off your prayer — do you not
> know that *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* is of *al-Ḥamd*? Whoever leaves it has left one of its verses,
> and whoever leaves a verse of it has cut off his prayer; and there is no prayer except with it — and
> whoever leaves a verse of it, his prayer is invalid**"); **Ṭalḥah b. ʿUbayd Allāh** (the Prophet: "**Whoever
> leaves *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* has left a verse of Allah's Book**").
> **Al-Alūsī's verdicts on these**: "as for the first, second, third and sixth of [these] — with their
