> weakness — and the eighth, **they do not indicate the intended point, and we say what they indicate**; **the
> fourth is *mawqūf* on Ibn ʿAbbās, and we do not concede that its ruling is *rafʿ*** — since *ijtihād* is
> possible, though we say the sound position is that a verse is known only by *tawqīf* from the Lawgiver, as
> is a sūrah — which is why they counted *Alif Lām Mīm* a verse where it occurs and did not count *Alif Lām
> Mīm Rā*; **the fifth I doubt for its wording**, or it is in the wording al-Dāraquṭnī produced; **I have no
> reliance on al-Fakhr in ḥadīths — he is not one of their memorisers, and I see that when he relates by
> meaning he alters; and I do not have al-Thaʿlabī's tafsīr to see it, for the transmission is from him; the
> seventh has no freshness of the Messenger's speech on it, nor his eloquence — he being the most eloquent of
> those who spoke the *ḍād*; one who has handled ḥadīths would judge this one fabricated**; and by my life,
> had it been sound, the Shāfiʿīs would have contented themselves with it or put it before their other proofs
> — would that he had mentioned its *isnād* so we could see it."
> **And from the other side, on the audible/secret question** (see V10): **Muslim ← Anas**: "*I prayed behind
> the Prophet ﷺ, Abū Bakr, ʿUmar and ʿUthmān and did not hear any of them recite *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni
> l-Raḥīm*"; **Ibn ʿAbbās**: "*The Prophet ﷺ did not say it aloud until he died*"; **Aḥmad and al-Nasāʾī**
> with an *isnād* on the criterion of the two Sheikhs: "**they used not to say *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni
> l-Raḥīm* aloud**"; **al-Ṭabarānī**: "the Messenger of Allah ﷺ **used to conceal** *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni
> l-Raḥīm*, and Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān and ʿAlī"; **Ibn al-Mughaffal**: his son said it aloud and he said,
> "**O my son, beware of novelty in Islam — I prayed behind the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, Abū Bakr, ʿUmar and
> ʿUthmān, and they began the recitation with *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn*… so when you pray, say
> *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn***" — i.e., al-Alūsī reads it: **say it aloud and conceal the basmalah**
> — "and it is the madhhab of al-Thawrī, Ibn al-Mubārak, Ibn Masʿūd, Ibn al-Zubayr, ʿAmmār b. Yāsir, al-Ḥasan
> b. Abī al-Ḥusayn, al-Shaʿbī, al-Nakhaʿī, Qatādah, ʿUmar b. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, al-Aʿmash, al-Zuhrī, Mujāhid,
> Aḥmad and a great many others." **And on the audibility reports**: "**of the reports of saying it aloud none
> is sound except Ibn ʿAbbās's, which al-Shāfiʿī reported**: *'The Messenger of Allah used to say it aloud'* —
> and it is **opposed by what preceded from him** [his own "he did not say it aloud until he died"], or is
> borne on **his saying it aloud at times** to show that it is recited in prayer, as ʿUmar said the *thanāʾ*
> aloud for teaching… **and al-Dāraquṭnī, who wrote a book on saying it aloud, was adjured by some Mālikīs to
> name the sound one, and he said: 'No report of saying it aloud is sound.'**" And on Anas's six contradictory
> reports: "**it is not harmful to us — the stronger *isnād* is preferred at contradiction, and here it is
> what agrees with us** [the criterion of the two Sheikhs]; and the charge of lying against a transmitter who
> opposes, on Anas, **is lighter in my eyes than the charge of lying on Anas himself against the Messenger of
> Allah ﷺ and the foremost of his Companions.**"

**Al-Jalālayn.** “It is to this meaning that the Prophet's [ﷺ] alluded when he said ‘I have been given the
sum of all excellent speech — [the Qurʾān] — and have been sent to complete the noble character traits
(*makārim al-akhlāq*)’.”

**Al-Ṭabarī** (no. 140) — Ismāʿīl b. al-Faḍl → Ibrāhīm b. al-ʿAlāʾ → Ismāʿīl b. ʿAyyāsh → Ismāʿīl b.
Yaḥyā → Ibn Abī Mulaykah → one who told him → Ibn Masʿūd; and Misʿar b. Kidām → ʿAṭiyyah → Abū Saʿīd:
the Prophet ﷺ said: “*ʿĪsā ibn Maryam was handed by his mother to the *kuttāb* to teach him, and the teacher
said to him: Write *bism*. ʿĪsā said to him: And what is *bism*? The teacher said: I do not know. ʿĪsā
said: The *bāʾ* is the splendour (bahāʾ) of Allah, the *sīn* is His eminence (sanāʾuhu), and the *mīm* is
His dominion (mamlakatuhu).*” Al-Ṭabarī's own verdict: “I fear it is an error of the transmitter, and that
he meant *b-s-m* as children are taught the *abjad* letters… for it has no meaning when *bismi llāhi
l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* is recited as the reciter of the Book of Allah recites it.” _(Corpus editor's appraisal:
“this is a fabricated ḥadīth with no basis”, entered through Ismāʿīl b. Yaḥyā, whom the critics declared a
liar; the same chain carries no. 145 on the word of ʿĪsā: “Allah is the God of gods”.)_

**Ibn Kathīr.** “The Two Sahihs recorded that Abū Hurayrah said that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: *Allah
has ninety-nine Names, one hundred minus one; whoever counts (and preserves) them will enter Paradise*.”
And, on the merit of the basmalah, Imam Ahmad recorded that a man riding behind the Prophet ﷺ said,
“The Prophet's animal tripped, so I said, ‘Cursed Shaytan.’ The Prophet said: *Do not say, ‘Cursed
Shaytan,’ for if you say these words, Satan becomes arrogant and says, ‘With my strength I made him fall.’
When you say, ‘Bismillah,’ Satan will become as small as a fly.*” An-Nasāʾī and Ibn Mardūyah add the
wording: “*Do not say these words, because then Satan becomes larger; as large as a house. Rather, say,
‘Bismillah’, because Satan then becomes as small as a fly.*”

**The Study Quran.** “In this vein the Prophet said, *‘Any important matter not begun with the Name of God
shall be cut off’* (Ṭb).” And in the introduction, the ḥadīth of the greatest sūrah and the ḥadīth qudsī of
the division of the prayer (see S4), with *“I have divided the prayer between Myself and My servant, and My
servant shall have that for which he prays.”*

### V6 · Companions & Successors (āthār)

**Al-Alūsī.** Named in his argument: **Ibn ʿAbbās** (counting the basmalah the seventh under 15:87; and "the
Prophet did not say it aloud until he died"); **ʿAlī** ("whoever leaves its recitation has made a
deficiency"); **Abū Hurayrah** (both directions — the ḥadīths above); **Ibn Masʿūd, Ibn al-Zubayr, ʿAmmār b.
Yāsir, al-Ḥasan b. Abī al-Ḥusayn, al-Shaʿbī, al-Nakhaʿī, Qatādah, ʿUmar b. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, al-Aʿmash,
al-Zuhrī, Mujāhid, Aḥmad** — on **concealing** it; **al-Thawrī and Ibn al-Mubārak** — likewise (and Ibn
al-Mubārak on the other side of the verse-question, per the tenth view's attribution); **Ibn al-Mughaffal**
and his son (tradition above); and **ʿUmar's** saying the *thanāʾ* aloud **for teaching**. And on the
*tawqīf* question: **"the sound position is that a verse is known only by *tawqīf*"** — with Ibn ʿAbbās's
*ijtihād* discussed.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The second** — Saʿīd b. Abī Sukaynah said: it reached me that **ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭālib** saw a
man writing *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* and said to him: ***Make it beautiful, for a man made it
beautiful and was forgiven.*** Saʿīd said: it reached me that a man looked at a parchment on which was
*bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*, kissed it and placed it on his eyes, **and was forgiven** — and of this
meaning is the story of Bishr al-Ḥāfī, who when he lifted the note in which was the name of Allah and perfumed
it, his name was perfumed (mentioned by al-Qushayrī)." And the report of al-Nasāʾī from Abū al-Malīḥ from the
man who rode behind the Prophet ﷺ — the Prophet ﷺ said: "*When an animal stumbles under you, do not say 'may
Satan perish' — for he grows until he becomes like a house and says, by his strength I did it — but say*
bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm, *for he shrinks until he is like a fly*." And **ʿAlī b. al-Ḥusayn** on His
word *wa-idhā dhakarta rabbaka fī l-Qurʾāni waḥdahu wallaw ʿalā adbārihim nufūrā* (17:46): "**its meaning is:
when you say *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm***." And "Wakīʿ from al-Aʿmash from Abū Wāʾil from **ʿAbd Allāh
ibn Masʿūd** said: *whoever wishes Allah to save him from the nineteen *zabāniyah*, let him recite* bismi
llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm, *so that Allah makes for him, with every letter of it, a shield from each one*" —
hence "the *basmalah* is **nineteen letters**, by the number of the angels of the people of the Fire of whom
Allah said *ʿalayhā tisʿata ʿashar*, and they say in all their acts *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* — from
there is their strength". **Ibn ʿAṭiyyah's caution: "this is among the curiosities of tafsīr, not of the
firm science"** — and his parallels: 27 for *Laylat al-Qadr*, and the "thirty-odd letters" of *rabbanā
wa-laka l-ḥamdu ḥamdan kathīran ṭayyiban mubārakan fīhi* over which the Prophet ﷺ "saw thirty-odd angels
racing to write it".

**The Study Quran.** The reports of the first generations on the *basmalah*: “Anas ibn Mālik… is reported
to have said, ‘I have prayed behind the Prophet, Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, and ʿUthmān, and they each began their
recitation with *Praise be to God, Lord of the worlds*, without mentioning *In the Name of God, the
Compassionate, the Merciful*’ (IK, Q, Sh, Z).” But Abū Hurayrah “is reported to have recited the *basmalah*
while leading the prayer and to have then said, ‘Among you I perform the prayer that is closest to the
prayer of the Messenger of God’ (IK).” Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq's saying that the *basmalah* is “the greatest verse
in the Book of God” (*Ṭb*, Ṭs), and Ibn ʿAbbās's report “that the Prophet did not know the point at which
to separate one sūrah from the next until God revealed *In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the
Merciful* (Sh)”; also “others say that it is a verse, though separate from the sūrahs that begin with it
(IK)”.

### V7 · Exegesis of the classical mufassirūn (*aqwāl al-mufassirīn*)

**Al-Alūsī.** The chapter's great debate — al-Rāzī's sixteen proofs that the basmalah is a verse of al-Fātiḥah,
and al-Alūsī's refutations (the whole runs some thirteen printed pages; his own note: **"al-Fakhr made the
saying long in this place and brought sixteen proofs… and I stand, by Allah's enabling, his refuter — and there
is no boasting — and the champion of my madhhab with Allah's support."** He adds his own history: "**I used
before to count the noble Shāfiʿīs my goal and did not count myself but one of them — the freshness of their
sayings had taken my heart as Laylā of ʿĀmir took Qays's — until I came to be occupied with the sayings of the
noble Ḥanafīs and lodged in their meadows of anemones…**")). His verdict on the sixteen as a set: **"most of
it is answered, and some of it does not stand as a proof against us, because the sound position of our madhhab
is that *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* is a separate verse, of the Qurʾān, though not of al-Fātiḥah itself;
and many of us have made its recitation in prayer obligatory…"** — and then **the two reports of *qasamtu
al-ṣalāh*** (the Muslim wording without the basmalah being counted in the division, against al-Thaʿlabī's):

> "**And this shows that the basmalah is not of al-Fātiḥah, and that it [the Fātiḥah] is seven without it**,
> since He made the middle *iyyāka naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn*, the three before it for Allah and the three
> after it for the servant; **and in it is no negation of its being of the Qurʾān.** And no doubt this report
> is sounder than al-Thaʿlabī's report — **and I do not put Thaʿlabī before Muslim** — and likewise than
> al-Sijistānī's… and **where a trustworthy transmitter contradicts one more trustworthy than himself, by
> addition or omission, his ḥadīth is anomalous**; and this is not of the category of affirmation and negation
> as those ungrounded in the art supposed… **and one who loads 'half' onto half of meaning or kind is void of
> fairness.**"

And his own methodological terse notes against al-Rāzī's individual proofs (compressed): the *baʿḍiyyah*
comparison "the fifth [proof] is like the fifth" — i.e., **"we do not concede that its being obligatory at
the head of the Fātiḥah entails its being a verse of it; and his inference here from *iqraʾ bi-smi rabbika* is
very weak in ways more visible than the sun"**; "**the sixth proof is the strongest of what he infers for the
basmalah's being of the Qurʾān; as for its being of the Fātiḥah, no**" — and he chides those who on that
ground attacked the Qurʾān question: "**fairness is half of religion, and submission to the truth is of the
believers' character**"; "**the seventh is for us, not against us**"; "**the eighth is far from establishing
what it turns on — the weakening of our master Abū Ḥanīfah's word — there are firm mountains in the way**";
"**the ninth is like the fifth, by the measure of the arrow**; and his inference from *'every matter of
moment…'* is nothing: for the Fātiḥah is part of the prayer opened by the *takbīr* joined to the intention,
which is a pillar of it — and if it is not opened with the basmalah, it is a truncated [act]... and likewise
the bowing and prostration, the nearest a servant comes to his Lord — each of them a matter of moment — so
if not opened with the basmalah it is truncated" — and so on through the rest: "**the remaining proofs are
like many of the past ones — they do not profit in the debate with us except by blackening the page and
wasting precious breaths**… **and some of what he mentioned is contradicted by what Muslim and others
reported from Abū Hurayrah** [the *qasamtu* ḥadīth]."

And the **evaluation of the *bāʾ*** among the mufassirūn: "*Istiʿānah* or *muṣāḥabah* are the candidates among
the six senses — **al-Bayḍāwī's words signal the first as weightier, al-Zamakhsharī's the second** — and the
case made for *muṣāḥabah* (more frequent; the Name as *tabarruk* rather than a base instrument; the
associators' use of their gods' names for blessing; the ḥadīth *'the naming of Allah in the heart of every
Muslim, whether he names or not'*; that blessing-with-the-Name is a meaning everyone grasps, while the
instrument-meaning is only reached by fine reflection; that making His Name an instrument for reciting the
Fātiḥah does not work for him who holds the basmalah part of the sūrah; and that making the existent as the
non-existent is a rhetorical nicety), **and al-Alūsī's own choice**: "**with me, *istiʿānah* is worthier —
indeed it is almost certainly the one** — for in it is an ado of humility and a showing of servitude that the
claim of accompaniment lacks, and in it a hint from the first glance of the dropping of one's own might and
power and the denying of the creature's independence and influence; **it is the opening of the gate of mercy
and a winning of the treasure — 'there is no might nor power save with Allah'** — and this meaning is closer to
*iyyāka nastaʿīn*, and it is the more apt with *iqraʾ bi-smi rabbika*, so that it be a complete answer to his
word ﷺ *'I am not a reciter'*; **and all they mention in support of *muṣāḥabah* is rejected**" — with his
point-by-point rebuttals (the frequency-claim, the "base instrument" presumption — *istaʿīnū bi-llāhi* is
sound and the Name is not debased — the associators' practice is closer to *istiʿānah*, instrumentality
requires presence through the whole act, the ḥadīth is more apt for *istiʿānah* since it declares the
servant's freeing himself of his own strength, etc.).

And **the omitted verb** — the mufassirūn's evaluation: "**Imām Ibn Jarīr chose the estimate *atlū* (I
recite), because what is recited is [thereby] recited** — and likewise the particular verb is suppressed for
every doer of an act, making the naming the beginning of his act — a Qurʾānic meaning like its likes, their
being necessary in the language's usage; **and by it the word of al-Ṣādiqī is repelled**… **and when this was
unclear to some, he made the estimated [verb] an imperative addressed to the servants, so that the sayer of
the spoken and the estimated be one — al-Farrāʾ chose this** — and it is reported **from Ibn ʿAbbās** —
**because Allah put the naming first as an urging of the servants to do that, which suits teaching.** **And
the grammarians went to [the estimate] as general: *abtadiʾ* (I begin)**, supported by faces: the act of
beginning can be estimated in every naming, unlike the act of recitation; and the general estimate is
worthier — *see how they estimate the governor of the *jār* standing as predicate, description, state or
relative clause by *kawn* and *istiqrār* wherever it falls, and prefer it for the generality of its
soundness*… " **And al-Alūsī's tarjīḥ**: "**what Imām [Ibn Jarīr] went to is more apt, more particular to the
aim and more complete in comprehension, for it entails the recitation occurring in its entirety, joined to the
naming, seeking help by Allah's Name over all of it** — unlike the estimate *abtadiʾ*, which does not touch
that" — with the discussions of al-Naḥwiyyūn, of *ofthe beginning being not more general than recitation*,
of the estimate *ibtidāʾī*, and of **al-Shaykh al-Akbar** (Ibn ʿArabī): "**he made this *jār* the predicate of
an omitted subject: *the beginning of the world and its appearance* — because the cause of its existence is
the divine Names, which have authority over it** — as though making it attached to what follows, since Allah
is not praised except by His Names… **and I do not see objection to him as fairness**."
And on **the name and the named**: "there has long been contention whether **the name is the very named or
other than it** — **the Ashʿarīs on the first, the Muʿtazilah on the second** — and **Fakhr al-Dīn in
*al-Tafsīr al-Kabīr* said this discussion runs the course of futility** and offered a face he claimed
fine and subtle, **and al-Shihāb spared us the labour of refuting him**; **al-Suhaylī** had a saying he
claimed to be the truth, and **Ibn al-Sayyid wrote a separate epistle in refutation**; **al-Shihāb claimed it
had until now not been freed [of difficulty], and that with his wide reading he had not seen on this question
what cools the breast or cures the ailment**… **and I, by Allah's favour, shall mention a thing which, if
accepted, is the utmost of my hope…**" — *and there the page ends and the corpus's abridgement note cuts the
argument off.*

### V8 · Language, lexicon & grammar (*lughah · iʿrāb · balāghah*)

**Al-Alūsī.** The full third research, *"on its meaning"*, with the lexical and grammatical apparatus:

* **The *bāʾ***: "**either for *istiʿānah*, or *muṣāḥabah*, or adhesion (*ilṣāq*), or elevation
  (*istiʿlā*), or redundant, or for an oath — and the last four are nothing**" (their refutation and the
  preference for *istiʿānah* at V7). "**And they differed over the *jār*'s governor**: **Ibn Jarīr → *atlū*…
  the grammarians → *abtadiʾ* / *ashraʿ*… **al-Farrāʾ** chose the imperative estimate, reported from **Ibn
  ʿAbbās**… **and some went to *ibtidāʾī*** — in it a surplus suppression… **and al-Shaykh al-Akbar made this
  *jār* the predicate of an omitted subject**…"
* **Whether the *ẓarf* is *mustaqirr* or *laghw*** — with the three definitions of the grammarians
  (*laghw* = whose governor is mentioned; *mustaqirr* = whose governor is omitted or general like
  *ḥuṣūl/istiqrār*) and **his choice of the first, "so the *ẓarf* here is *mustaqirr* however the governor is
  estimated."**
* **The *kasrah* of the *bāʾ***: "**the solitary particles' right is *fatḥah*, because they are built [on
  *sukūn*], and the base in building is *sukūn* for its lightness… except that they, being words on their own,
  are the expected place of beginning — which by the *sukūn* is impossible or hard — **so its right was
  *fatḥah*, since it is the sibling of *sukūn* in the lightness sought**… **and it is distinguished among the
  particles by its being confined to particle-hood and *jarr*, and each of the two suits *kasrah***…" — with
  the counter-cases (*wāw* of conjunction, *kāf* of comparison, and the answer that in them the *jarr* is not
  their own effect) and **his verdict: "these are grammatical causes extracted after the fact, to display a
  suitability — so they do not bear discussion, for their weakness"**, with the versified sneer he quotes:
  *"My beloved's covenant and compact is weaker than a grammarian's proof"* — "**so we shall not keep the eye of
  thought awake over what is for it and against it.**"
* ***Ism* — its derivation:** "**with the Baṣrans it is one of the ten nouns whose beginnings were built on
  *sukūn*: *ibn, ibnah, ibnūm/ibnum, ism, ist, ithnān, ithnatān, imruʾ, imraʾah, aymanu llāh* and *aymu llāh*
  from it — and by the count of *ibnim*, eleven.** When they utter them they add a *hamzah*, for the ugliness
  of beginning with a *sukūn*… **and its root is *sumuww*, the *wāw* dropped for lightness and frequency of
  use** — "**and its conjugation to *asmāʾ*, *sumayy*, *summiyat*, against *awsām*, *wasīm*, *wasamtu* bears
  witness for it; and vitiation by the heart is not accepted.** And its derivation from *al-sumuww* is like
  *al-ʿuluww*, **because by its indicating what it names it raises it from the lowland of hiddenness to the
  summit of appearance and clarity**." **And the Kūfans**: "**it is from *al-simah*, because it is a mark on
  what it names; its root *wasm*, the *wāw* dropped and the *hamzah* of connection replacing it**… — **and
  they are refuted**: the *hamzah* is not known to enter what has had its head dropped; and the increase of
  *iʿlāl* is more analogically sound than the absence of a parallel; **and its being a compensation entails
  its being intended for itself, while connection entails its being intended secondarily** — compensation being
  like part of the root, comparison being the stronger."
* **The eighteen languages of *ism*** — with the versified mnemonic: "*For *al-ism* ten languages with eight —
  by the transmission of my grandfather, the sheikh of the people, the most complete of them: **smun, simāt,
  samā, ism**, and add **simah**, likewise **samāʾ** with [permitted] three [vowellings] of its first.*"
* **(His *tadabbur* on the *bāʾ*'s *kasrah*, filed at V14 below.)**

**Al-Baghawī.**

* **The *bāʾ* and its omitted verb.** "*Bismi llāh*: the *bāʾ* is a particle that makes what follows it
  genitive, like *min* and *ʿan*; **the verb to which the *bāʾ* attaches is omitted, the speech indicating
  it — its sense being *I begin with the name of Allah*, or *say: in the name of Allah***."
* **The dropped *alif* and the lengthened *bāʾ*.** "The *alif* was dropped from *ism* **seeking lightness
  and because of its frequency of use**, and the *bāʾ* was lengthened. **Al-Qutaybī** said: **so that the
  opening of the speech of Allah's Book be with a magnified letter** — and **ʿUmar b. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz** used
  to say to his scribes: **lengthen the *bāʾ*, make the *sīn* plain, leave space between them, and round the
  *mīm*** — for the magnifying of the Book of Allah. And it is said: when they dropped the *alif*, they
  returned the length of the *alif* to the *bāʾ* so that it be a sign of the dropping of the *alif* — do you
  not see that when the *alif* was written in *iqraʾ bi-smi rabbika* (96:1) the *bāʾ* was returned to its
  form; **and the *alif* is not dropped when the name is annexed to other than Allah, nor with other than
  the *bāʾ*.**"
* **The name and the named.** "**The name is the named, its very self and essence** (*al-ismu huwa
  l-musammā wa-ʿaynuhu wa-dhātuh*). Allah said *innā nubashshiruka bi-ghulāmin ismuhu Yaḥyā* (19:7) —
  He reported that his name is Yaḥyā, then called the name, saying *yā Yaḥyā*; and *mā taʿbudūna min dūnihi
  illā asmāʾan sammaytumūhā* (12:40) — **and He meant the persons worshipped**, because they were
  worshipping the named; and *sabbiḥi sma rabbika* (87:1), and *tabāraka smu rabbika* (55:78). **Then the
  *tasmiyah* itself is also called a *name***; and its use for the *tasmiyah* is more frequent than for the
  named." And: "if it is said, **what is the meaning of Allah's naming Himself**? — it is said: **it is
  teaching the servants how they should open the recitation**."
* **The derivation of *ism*.** "They differed over its derivation. **Al-Mubarrid of the Baṣrans**: it is
  derived from *al-sumuww*, which is height — as though it rose above its meaning and appeared over it, and
  its meaning became beneath it. **Thaʿlab of the Kūfans**: it is from *al-wasm* and *al-simah*, which is
  **the mark** — as though it is a mark for its meaning. **And the first is sounder, because its diminutive
  is *sumayy*; were it from *al-simah* its diminutive would be *wusaym***, as *al-waʿd* gives *wuʿayd*; and
  in its conjugation one says *sammaytu* — were it from *al-wasm* one would say *wasamtu*."
* **The name *Allāh*.** "**Al-Khalīl and a group**: it is a **proper name peculiar to Allah, Mighty and
  Exalted, with no derivation**, like the proper names of servants such as Zayd and ʿAmr. **And a group**:
  it is derived, then differed over its derivation: it is said from *alaha ilāhatan*, meaning *he worshipped
  worship* — and **Ibn ʿAbbās read** *wa-yadharaka wa-ilāhata-ka* (7:127), meaning *your worship* — "the
  sense being that He is worthy of worship to the exclusion of others"; and it is said its root is *ilāh*,
  by His word *wa-mā kāna maʿahu min ilāhin idhan la-dhahaba kullu ilāhin bimā khalaqa* (23:91). **Al-Mubarrid**:
  it is from the Arabs' *alahtu ilā fulān* — *I settled to him*, as the poet: *alahtu ilayhā wa-l-ḥawādithu
  jammah* — **as though creation settles to Him and is at rest in His mention**; and one says *alahtu ilayhi*
  — *I took refuge in him*, as the poet: *alahtu ilayhā wa-l-rakāʾibu wuqqaf*. And it is said the root of
  *ilāh* is *wilāh*, and the *wāw* was changed to a *hamzah* like *wishāḥ* and *ishāḥ* — **its derivation
  from *al-walah*, because the servants yearn (*yawlahūna*) to Him**, that is, they take refuge with Him in
  hardships and betake themselves to Him in needs, as every child yearns to its mother; and it is said it is
  from *al-walah*, **which is the going of the mind at the loss of one who is dear.**"
* **The two names.** "*Al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm*: **Ibn ʿAbbās** said: **they are two tender names, one tenderer
  than the other.**"

**Al-Jalālayn.** The lexicon of *ism*: a name is that by which its bearer is known; the divine names are
“the archetypal forms (*ṣuwar nawʿiyya*) whose specificities and ipseities (*hūwiyya*) indicate the
attributes of God and His essence, and which indicate by their very existence His countenance and by
their individuation His oneness — for these are the outward manifestations through which He can be
known.” The pair *al-Raḥmān / al-Raḥīm* is distinguished by scope: the first is the name by which the
flow of existence and perfection begins, the second the name of the perfection granted at the end; hence
the first is used of this world and the Hereafter together, the second of the Hereafter alone.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The philological *masāʾil*:

* **The verb-noun *basmala*.** "al-Māwardī said: one who says *bismi llāh* is called *mubasmil*; and it is a
  coined word (*lughah muwalladah*) — it has come in poetry, as ʿUmar b. Abī Rabīʿah: *laqad basmalat Laylā
  ghudhayyata laqītuhā / fa-yā ḥabba dhāka l-ḥabību l-mubasmili*." "I say: what is well known with the people
  of language is *basmala*: Yaʿqūb b. al-Sikkīt, al-Muṭarriz, al-Thaʿālibī and others say: *basmala l-rajul*
  = he said *bismi llāh*, and one says *you have done much *basmalah*** — "and like it *ḥawqala* for *lā ḥawla
  wa-lā quwwata illā bi-llāh*, *hallala* for *lā ilāha illā llāh*, *sabḥala* for *subḥāna llāh*, *ḥamdala*
  for *al-ḥamdu li-llāh*, *ḥayṣala* for *ḥayya ʿalā l-ṣalāh*, *jaʿfala* for *juʿiltu fidāka*, *ṭabqala* for
  *aṭāla llāhu baqāʾak*, *damʿaza* for *adāma llāhu ʿizzak*."
* **The *alif* of *bismi*.** "**The thirteenth** — *bismi llāh* is written without an *alif*, being dispensed
  of by the *bāʾ* of conjunction in pronunciation and writing, for the frequency of use — unlike *iqraʾ
  bi-smi rabbika* (96:1), where it is dropped for the rarity of use. They differed over dropping it with
  *al-Raḥmān* and *al-Qāhir*: al-Kisāʾī and Saʿīd al-Akhfash said the *alif* is dropped; Yaḥyā b. Waththāb
  said it is not dropped except with *bismi llāh* alone, because with it use was frequent."
* **The *bāʾ*'s *kasrah*.** "**The fourteenth** — the *bāʾ* was singled out for *kasrah* for three meanings: it
  was said, that its form match its function; and, that since the *bāʾ* only enters on nouns, it was singled
  out with the *khafḍ* that is only in nouns; and third, to distinguish it from what may be a noun among the
  particles, like the *kāf* in the poet's *wa-ruḥnā bi-kābni l-māʾi yujnabu wasṭanā* — that is, *with the like
  of the son of the water*."
* **The derivation of *ism*.** "**The fifteenth** — *ism*, its weight is *ifʿ*, and what is missing from it is
  the *wāw*, for it is from *samawtu*; its plural is *asmāʾ* and its diminutive *sumayy*." Its four dialects:
  *ism*, *usm*, *sim*, *sum* — with the verse *wa-llāhu asmāka suman mubārakan*. "**The sixteenth** — the
  *nisbah* to *ism* is *sumawiyy* (and one may say *ismiyy*), plural *asmāʾ*, and the plural of *asmāʾ* is
  *asāmī*." "**The seventeenth** — disagreement over *ism*'s derivation: **the Baṣrans: from *al-sumuww*, which
  is height and elevation** … and **the Kūfans: from *al-simah*, which is the mark, because the name is a mark
  for the one it is set for** — and the first is sounder, because the diminutive is *sumayy* and the plural
  *asmāʾ*, and diminutive and plural return things to their roots." And his doctrinal use of that: "whoever
  says the name is from height says: **Allah has not ceased to be described before the existence of creation
  and after it and at their passing, and they have no effect on His names or His attributes — and this is the
  saying of *Ahl al-Sunnah***; and whoever says it is from *simah* says: *Allah was in eternity with no name
  and no attribute, and when He created creation they made names and attributes for Him* — **and this is the
  saying of the Muʿtazilah, and it is contrary to what the community has agreed on, and greater in error than
  their saying that His speech is created**." "**The eighteenth** — on this disagreement falls the question
  of the name and the named: **the people of truth — Abū Bakr b. al-Ṭayyib al-Qāḍī, approved by Ibn Fūrak, and
  it is the saying of Abū ʿUbaydah and Sībawayh — held that the name is the named**", while Ibn al-Ḥaṣṣār:
  "whoever denies the attributes among the innovators claims that there is no meaning for the namings except
  the Essence, and therefore says the name is other than the named; and whoever affirms the attributes affirms
  for the namings meanings which are the descriptions of the Essence, other than the expressions."
* **The name *Allāh*.** "**The twentieth** — *Allāh* is the greatest of His names and the most comprehensive
  of them, to the point that some scholars said it is **the greatest name of Allah**, and no other is named by
  it (and therefore it is not dualized nor pluralized) — and this is one of the two readings of His word *hal
  taʿlamu lahu samiyyā* (19:65), that is, who is named by His name *Allāh*." And "**the twenty-first** —
  disagreement over whether it is derived or coined for the Essence as a proper name: Sībawayh from al-Khalīl
  that its root is *ilāh*, like *fiʿāl*, and the *alif* and *lām* were put in place of the *hamzah*, as
  al-Sībawayh said *al-nās* is originaly *unās*; and it is said the root is *lāh*, on which the *alif* and
  *lām* entered for magnification (Sībawayh's choice, with the verse *lāhi bnu ʿammika lā afḍalta fī ḥasabin*);
  and al-Kisāʾī and al-Farrāʾ: the sense is *bismi l-Ilāh*, so they took away the *hamzah* and merged the
  first *lām* into the second, as in *lākinna huwa llāhu rabbī* — al-Ḥasan read it so". And: "it is said it is
  derived from *waliha*, when he is bewildered — *al-walah* is the going of the mind: *so Allah, the minds are
  bewildered and go astray in the realities of His attributes and in thought about knowing Him*"; and from
  al-Ḍaḥḥāk: "it was named *ilāh* because **creation betakes itself to Him in their needs and humbles
  themselves to Him in their hardships**" (al-Khalīl: *yaʾlahūna ilayhi* with the *lām* in *naṣb*, and with
  *kasrah* — two dialects); and from *al-Rabb*/height ("the Arabs said of everything raised, *lāhā*; when the
  sun rose they said *lāhat*"); and from *alaha l-rajul* when he became a devotee — "and from that His word
  *wa-yadharaka wa-ilāhata-ka* (7:127), for Ibn ʿAbbās and others said: *and your worship* — so the meaning of
  *Allāh* is **the One aimed at in worship**; and from it the monotheists' word *lā ilāha illā llāh*, meaning
  *there is no worshipped but Allah*, and the *illā* in it is in the sense of *ghayr*, not of exception."
  And "some claimed the root is the *hāʾ* which is the pronoun of the absent … then the *lām* of ownership was
  added … then the *alif* and *lām* for magnification and veneration."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** Three grammatical matters are argued at length.

* **The ellipsis (*ḥadhf*) of the governing verb.** The *bāʾ* requires a verb; the verb is dropped because
  the situation supplies it — as in the poets' ellipses: *“w-aʿlamu annanī sa-akūnu ramsan — idhā sāra
  l-nawāʿiju lā yasīru / fa-qāla l-sāʾilūna li-man ḥafartum? — fa-qāla l-mukhbirūna lahum: wazīru”*
  (“…they said: a *wazīr*” — the word “dead man” being dropped), and *“wa-raʾayti zawjaki fī l-waghā
  mutaqallidan sayfan wa-rumḥā”* (“girded with a sword and a spear” — for “carrying a spear”, since a spear
  is not girded).
* **“Name” standing for “naming”.** The Arabs put a noun in place of a *maṣdar*: “*akramtu fulānan
  karāmatan*”, “*kallamtuhu kalāman*” (for *taklīman*), “*aʿṭāhu l-miʾata l-ritāʿā*” for *iʿṭāʾaka* — with
  the Lakhmid/Ṭāʾī verses adduced. On that model, *ism* in *bismi llāh* stands for the *maṣdar* *tasmiyah*
  (“naming”): the sense is “I begin with the naming of Allah before my act or my speech.”
* **The derivation of *Allāh*.** “*Allāh* is, in the speech of the Arabs, originally *al-Ilāh*”: the *hamzah*
  which is the *fāʾ* of the word is dropped, the *lām* which is the *ʿayn* meets the added *lām*, and the two
  are merged — “just as ”*lākin huwa llāhu rabbī*“ (18:38) is originally ”*lākin anā*“”, with the verse
  *“wa-tarmīnanī bi-l-ṭarfi ay anta mudhnibun / wa-taqḷīnanī lākinna iyyāki lā aqlī”* as the parallel. The
  **qirāʾah** of 7:127 is adduced for the root: “Ibn ʿAbbās read ”*wa-yadharaka wa-ilāhata-ka*“ — *your
  worship*” (no. 142), and “it is related that he said: *he was worshipped and did not worship*” (no. 143);
  Mujāhid likewise glossed it *wa-ʿibādata-ka*, “your worship” (no. 144). Al-Ṭabarī concludes: “*ilāhah* is
  the *maṣdar* of ”*alaha llāha fulānun ilāhatan*“, as one says ”*ʿabada llāha ʿibādatan*“”. _(Corpus
  editor's appraisal: nos. 142–143 are weak through the shaykh of al-Ṭabarī in them, Sufyān b. Wakīʿ; and
  the reading *wa-ilāhata-ka* is a shādhdh reading, the well-known reading being_ wa-ālihataka _.)_

He declines the wider question: “this is not the place to expand on whether the name is the named or other
than it, or an attribute of Him.”

**As-Saʿdī.** The generality of the opening is carried by the grammar: the word *ism* is a **singular
in construct** (*mufrad muḍāf*), which is a form that is general in meaning — hence "with every name
belonging to Allah", not one name only. *(Text: «لفظ «اسم» مفرد مضاف، فيعم جميع الأسماء الحسنى».)* The
two names *al-Raḥmān* and *al-Raḥīm* are both built on the pattern of intensity and both indicate the
attribute of mercy, one being more emphatic than the other.

**Ibn Kathīr.** On the derivation (*ishtiqāq*): “*Ar-Raḥmān* and *Ar-Raḥīm* are two names derived from
*Ar-Raḥmah* (the mercy)”, and the ḥadīth of the womb is adduced as “a text that indicates the derivation”.
Al-Qurṭubī is quoted: “It was said that both *Ar-Raḥmān* and *Ar-Raḥīm* have the same meaning, such as
the words *Nadman* and *Nadim*, as Abū ʿUbayd has stated. Abū ʿAlī al-Fārisī said: ‘*Ar-Raḥmān*, which is
exclusively for Allah, is a name that encompasses every type of mercy that Allah has. *Ar-Raḥīm* is what
effects the believers’”; and Ibn ʿAbbās: “They are two soft names, one of them is softer than the other
(meaning it carries more implications of mercy).”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** the phrase “is composed of three words — the letter *Bāʾ*, *Ism* and *Allāh*. The
preposition *Bāʾ* has several connotations in Arabic, three of which are appropriate to the occasion…
(1) Contiguity… (2) Seeking the aid of someone… (3) To seek the blessings of someone.” The three senses
give: “*With the name of Allah*; *With the help of the name of Allah*; *With the barakah of the name of
Allah*.” Since the phrase is incomplete without the act intended, “according to the rules of grammar, some
verb is taken to be understood here which should be suitable for the occasion — e.g., ‘I begin or recite
with the name of Allah,’” and “propriety demands that even this verb should be understood to occur after
the phrase, so that one does actually begin with the name of Allah and the verb does not precede His
name”. The ʿUthmānic script: “The regular Arabic script requires the letter *Bāʾ* here to be joined with
the letter *Alif*… But the ʿUthmāni manuscript has dropped the *Alif*, and joined the letter *Bāʾ* with the
letter *Sīn*, making the *Bāʾ* look like a part of the word *Ism*, so that the beginning is made, in
effect, with the name of Allah.” On the divine names: “The word *Allāh* is the greatest and the most
comprehensive of the divine names… the word ‘Allāh’ refers to the Essence, and hence this name cannot be
given to anyone except Allah. That is why this word has neither a plural nor a dual, for Allah is One and
has no associate.” On the pair: “*Raḥmān* signifies one whose mercy is common to all, and extends to the
whole universe… *Raḥīm* signifies one whose mercy is perfect in all possible ways. That is why *Raḥmān* is
the exclusive attribute of Allah… It is not permissible to qualify any created being as *Raḥmān*… Just
like the word *Allāh*, there is no dual or plural for the word *Raḥmān* too.” And: “The signification of
the word *Raḥīm*, on the contrary, does not contain anything which it should be impossible to find in a
created being… so the word *Raḥīm* may justifiably be employed in the case of a human being”, as the
Qurʾān uses it of the Prophet ﷺ (9:128). Both names “have been derived from the root *Raḥmah* (mercy),
indicating the all-pervasiveness and perfection of divine mercy”.

**The Study Quran.** The grammatical and lexical matter of this verse — the preposition *bi* and its
implied verb or noun (R, Ṭb, My); the derivation of *Allāh* (Ṭb); and the distinction of the three Names by
level (K) — is given above under V2.

### V10 · Juristic rulings (*aḥkām*)

**Al-Alūsī.** From the corpus's basmalah section — the Ḥanafī position argued:

> "**The sound position of our madhhab is that *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* is a separate verse, of the
> Qurʾān, though not of al-Fātiḥah itself; and many of us have made its recitation in prayer obligatory.**
> **Al-Zaylaʿī** in *Sharḥ al-Kanz*: **the soundest is that it is *wājib***; **al-Zāhidī** from *al-Mujtabā*:
> **the sound position is that it is obligatory in every *rakʿah* in which recitation is obligatory, and this
> is the sound report from Abū Ḥanīfah**" — and **Ibn Wahbān**'s verse: *"If one omits the basmalah in a
> *rakʿah* out of forgetfulness, he prostrates [in forgetfulness] since its obligatoriness the majority
> said"*; and *Ghunyat al-Mutamallī*: "**it is the more cautious and I hold it — contrary to Qāḍī Khān, the
> author of *al-Khulāṣah* and others**"; and: "**many of our scholars made the prostration of forgetfulness
> incumbent on one who omits it**" — hence the counting of *takrār* in the reports.
> **On the Fātiḥah itself in prayer**: "**it is obligatory, or a required part, in it** — the recommended-ness
> being a madhhab of some *mujtahids*, and a report from some on supererogatory prayer"; the name *Sūrat
> al-Ṣalāh* "because it is obligatory or a required part in it"; and the ḥadīth *'I have divided the prayer
> between Me and My servant'* "**and He meant the sūrah**" (with the juristic note on *al-Ṣalāh*'s being a
> name of the Fātiḥah by *majāz* of whole-and-part or of concomitance).
> **On the *basmalah*'s being a verse**: the ten views (S3) and the *tawqīf* principle: "**the sound position
> is that a verse is known only by *tawqīf* from the Lawgiver, as is a sūrah's [delimitation] — which is why
> they counted *Alif Lām Mīm* a verse where it occurs and did not count *Alif Lām Mīm Rā***." And the ten
> views' legal consequences — whether the *muḥdith* may touch it, [whether] its recitation is obligatory — are
> what al-Rāzī's sixteenth proof tried to turn on "the safer course", and **al-Alūsī's answer: the question
> between us is not theirs to settle** (see V5/V7).
> **And on saying it aloud** (V5): the reports' weighting; the ruling he adopts **is concealment**, with the
> reports of al-Thawrī, Ibn al-Mubārak, Ibn Masʿūd, Ibn al-Zubayr, ʿAmmār, al-Ḥasan b. Abī al-Ḥusayn,
> al-Shaʿbī, al-Nakhaʿī, Qatādah, ʿUmar b. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, al-Aʿmash, al-Zuhrī, Mujāhid and Aḥmad; **and
> al-Dāraquṭnī's verdict that no *jahr* report is sound**; and the Shīʿī claim of *tawātur* from ʿAlī —
> **"rejected by Ahl al-Sunnah; the Shīʿah claimed it and went to *jahr* in both loud and quiet prayers; and
> if one acted on all they allege as *mutawātir* from the Commander he would disbelieve — so there is only
> faith in part and disbelief in part"** — with his note that ʿAlī's madhhab is a matter of what is *sabt*
> [established], and that with Ahl al-Sunnah the prevalent is what the Two Sheikhs are upon, then Abū Bakr
> when they differ — quoting the four ḥadīths on following the Companions and Abū Bakr and ʿUmar.
> **And the *takbīr*'s history in the same debate**: al-Rāzī's report that Muʿāwiya failed both the basmalah
> and the *takbīr* at the inclinations, "and this does not harm us" — the point being **jahr** — "*yes,* ***jahr*
> remains, and the discussion of it is hidden now**."

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The basmalah's ruling, in his own *tarjīḥ*:

* "**The whole of Mālik's and his companions' doctrine is that it is not, with them, a verse of Fātiḥat
  al-Kitāb nor of others: the one praying does not recite it in the obligatory prayer nor in other than it,
  silently nor aloud; and it is permitted to recite it in supererogatory prayers.** This is the well known of
  his doctrine. Another report from him: it is recited at the head of sūrahs in the supererogatory and not at
  the head of Umm al-Qurʾān; and Ibn Nāfiʿ related from him the beginning of recitation with it in the
  obligatory and the supererogatory, not to be left in any case. And some of the people of Madinah say that
  the basmalah is indispensable in it — among them **Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn Shihāb**; and al-Shāfiʿī, Aḥmad,
  Isḥāq, Abū Thawr and Abū ʿUbayd said it. **And this shows that the question is one of *ijtihād*, not
  decisive** — as some of the ignorant jurists supposed, whose saying would entail declaring Muslims
  unbelievers. And a group of scholars held that it is recited silently with the Fātiḥah: among them **Abū
  Ḥanīfah and al-Thawrī**, and it is related from ʿUmar, ʿAlī, Ibn Masʿūd, ʿAmmār and Ibn al-Zubayr, and it is
  the saying of al-Ḥakam, Ḥammād and Aḥmad b. Ḥanbal and Abū ʿUbayd, and the like is related from al-Awzāʿī
  (cited by Abū ʿUmar b. ʿAbd al-Barr in *al-Istidhkār*)." Their proof: Anas's "the Messenger of Allah ﷺ led
  us in prayer and did not make us hear the recitation of *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*"; ʿAmmār b. Ruzayq
  from al-Aʿmash from Shuʿbah from Thābit from Anas: "I prayed behind the Prophet ﷺ, Abū Bakr and ʿUmar, and I
  did not hear any of them recite *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* aloud."
* His own preference: "**I say: this is a good saying, and on it the reports from Anas agree and do not
  contradict, and by it one leaves the disagreement over reciting the basmalah.**" And the reason given by
  Saʿīd b. Jubayr for the silence: "the idolaters used to attend the mosque, and when the Messenger of Allah
  ﷺ recited *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* they said: *This is Muḥammad mentioning the Raḥmān of
  al-Yamāmah* — meaning Musaylimah — **so he was commanded to lower his voice with the basmalah, and** *wa-lā
  tajhar bi-ṣalātika wa-lā tukhāfit bihā* (17:110) **came down**"; and Tirmidhī al-Ḥakīm: "that remained to
  our day on that script even if the cause has gone, as the *ramal* in *ṭawāf* remained though the cause had
  gone."
* **The obligation of the Fātiḥah in prayer** (second *bāb*, fifth to ninth *masāʾil*): **Mālik**: it is
  required of the imam and the one praying alone in every *rakʿah*; Ibn Khuwayz Mindād: "Mālik's saying did
  not differ that one who forgets it in a *rakʿah* of a two-*rakʿah* prayer, his prayer is void"; when
  forgotten in one *rakʿah* of a four- or three-*rakʿah* prayer, Mālik once said repeat the prayer and once
  said prostrate the two prostrations of forgetfulness; Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr: "the sound saying is to cancel that
  *rakʿah* and bring another in its place, like one who has omitted a prostration by forgetfulness." **Al-Ḥasan
  al-Baṣrī and most of the people of Baṣrah** and al-Mughīrah b. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān: "if one recited Umm al-Qurʾān
  once in the prayer it suffices him, since it is a prayer in which one has recited Umm al-Qurʾān, and it is
  complete — by his saying: *No prayer for one who does not recite Umm al-Qurʾān* — and this one has recited
  it." **Abū Ḥanīfah, al-Thawrī and al-Awzāʿī**: if one leaves it deliberately in the whole prayer and
  recites other than it, it suffices him (with a difference related from al-Awzāʿī); **Abū Yūsuf and
  Muḥammad b. al-Ḥasan**: the least is three verses, or one long verse like the verse of debt — and from
  Muḥammad b. al-Ḥasan: "I allow *ijtihād* in the measure of a verse and a comprehensible word, like *al-ḥamdu
  li-llāh*, and I do not allow it in a letter that is not speech"; **al-Ṭabarī**: the one praying recites Umm
  al-Qurʾān in every *rakʿah*, and if he does not, nothing suffices him but its like from the Qurʾān, in the
  number of its verses and letters — Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr: "this has no meaning; the designation of it and the
  text for it has singled it out for this ruling".
* **The *maʾmūm*.** The imam carries the recitation from him if he joins him bowing (consensus); if he joins
  him standing, he recites. In the silent prayer "one should not leave off recitation behind his imam, and if
  he does he has done ill and owes nothing (Mālik and his companions)"; when the imam prays aloud, "there is
  no recitation of Fātiḥat al-Kitāb nor of other than it in the well-known of Mālik's doctrine", by 7:204 and
  the Prophet's ﷺ "*Why am I disputed over the Qurʾān?*", "*When he recites, be silent*", and "*Whoever has
  an imam, the imam's recitation is his recitation*." And the dispute over the last: "it is a **weak ḥadīth**,
  from al-Ḥasan b. ʿUmārah (abandoned) and Abū Ḥanīfah (weak), both from Mūsā b. Abī ʿĀʾishah from ʿAbd Allāh
  b. Shaddād from Jābir — Dāraqutnī said: Sufyān, Shuʿbah, Isrāʾīl and others related it from Mūsā from Ibn
  Shaddād **as an interrupted report from the Prophet ﷺ, and that is the correct form**." Al-Shāfiʿī (as
  related by al-Buwayṭī and Aḥmad b. Ḥanbal): "no one's prayer is valid until he recites Fātiḥat al-Kitāb in
  every *rakʿah*, imam or follower, whether his imam prays aloud or silently" — in Iraq he said the follower
  recites when the imam is silent and does not when aloud; in Egypt he had two sayings. And Ibn Wahb, Ashhab,
  Ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, Ibn Ḥabīb and the Kūfans: "the follower recites nothing, whether the imam is aloud or
  silent."
* **Al-Qurṭubī's conclusion** (ninth *masʾalah*): "**the sound of these sayings is the saying of al-Shāfiʿī,
  Aḥmad and Mālik in the other saying: that the Fātiḥah is required in every *rakʿah* for every one,
  generally**, by his saying ﷺ *No prayer for one who does not recite in it Fātiḥat al-Kitāb*, and *Whoever
  prays a prayer in which he does not recite Umm al-Qurʾān, it is deficient* — three times; and Abū Hurayrah:
the Messenger of Allah ﷺ commanded me to announce that *there is no prayer except with the recitation of
Fātiḥat al-Kitāb, and then what is added* (Abū Dāwūd). As a prostration of one *rakʿah* does not substitute
for another's, nor its bowing, so the recitation of one *rakʿah* does not substitute for another" — and he
lists the Companions who said "no prayer except with Fātiḥat al-Kitāb": ʿUmar, Ibn ʿAbbās, Abū Hurayrah,
Ubayy b. Kaʿb, Abū Ayyūb al-Anṣārī, ʿAbd Allāh b. ʿAmr, ʿUbādah b. al-Ṣāmit, Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, ʿUthmān b.
Abī al-ʿĀṣ, Khawwāt b. Jubayr; and Ibn Mājah's ḥadīth of Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī: "*No prayer for one who does
not recite in every* rakʿah *al-Ḥamdu li-llāh and a sūrah, in an obligatory prayer or other.*" And **ʿUbādah
b. al-Ṣāmit's** ḥadīth (Abū Dāwūd), on which he relies: "the Messenger of Allah ﷺ led us in one of the
prayers in which he recited aloud, and it became confused for him; when he finished he turned his face to us
and said: *Do you recite when I recite aloud?* We said: Yes. He said: *Do not — for I say, why am I disputed
over the Qurʾān? Do not recite anything of the Qurʾān when I recite aloud, except Umm al-Qurʾān.*" And on
7:204, "it came down at Makkah, and the prohibition of speech in prayer came down at Madinah, as Zayd b.
Arqam said, so it is no proof — its object was the idolaters, as Saʿīd b. al-Musayyab said." And ʿAṭāʾ's
practice: "**ʿAṭāʾ said: *Āmīn* is a supplication; Ibn al-Zubayr would say *Āmīn* and those behind him until
the mosque surged with it.**"

**Ibn Kathīr.** On reciting the basmalah aloud in the prayer, he sets out the positions and their proofs:
ash-Shāfiʿī and many of the Companions, the Tābiʿīn and the imams held that it is recited aloud with
al-Fātiḥah — with the report that Abū Hurayrah recited it aloud and said, “Among you, I perform the prayer
that is the closest to the prayer of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ”, and the ḥadīth of Umm Salamah that the
Prophet ﷺ “used to distinguish each Ayah during his recitation”. Against that, “other scholars stated that
the Bismillah should not be recited aloud in the prayer, and this is the established practice of the Four
Khalifahs… It is also the Madhhab (view) of Abū Ḥanīfah, Ath-Thawrī and Aḥmad bin Ḥanbal. Imam Mālik
stated that the Bismillah is not recited aloud or silently”, their proof being ʿĀʾishah's report that the
Prophet ﷺ “used to start the prayer by reciting the Takbir and then recite *Al-Ḥamdu lillāhi
Rabbil-ʿālamīn*”, and Anas's report that the Prophet ﷺ, Abū Bakr, ʿUmar and ʿUthmān “used to start their
prayer with *Al-Ḥamdu lillāhi Rabbil-ʿālamīn*”. He closes: “These are the opinions held by the respected
Imams, and their statements are similar in that they agree that the prayer of those who recite Al-Fatihah
aloud or in secret is correct.”

On the **istiʿādhah**: “The majority of the scholars state that reciting the *Istiʿādhah* (in the prayer
and when reciting the Qur'an) is recommended and not required, and therefore, not reciting it does not
constitute a sin. However, Ar-Rāzī recorded that ʿAṭāʾ bin Abī Rabāḥ said that the *Istiʿādhah* is required
in the prayer and when one reads the Qur'an.”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** On recitation: “According to the consensus of Ummah, it is a Sunnah to say
*taʿawwudh* — *Aʿūdhu billāhi mina-sh-Shayṭāni-r-rajīm* — before the recitation of the Holy Qur'an whether
in *ṣalāh* or out of *ṣalāh*”, with 16:98: *When you recite the Qur'an, seek the protection of Allah
against Satan, the rejected one*. “Saying *taʿawwudh* is peculiar to the recitation of the Holy Qur'an.
Therefore, with the exception of *tilāwah*, all other chores should be taken up by first saying
‘Bismillah’ only.” During recitation, one repeats the basmalah at each new sūrah but not the taʿawwudh,
“with the sole exception of the Surah al-Barāʾah”. On handling: “*Bismillah al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm* is a verse
of the Holy Qur'an and a part of the verse in Surah al-Naml; it is also a regular verse when it occurs
between two Surahs. It must, therefore, be treated with as much respect as the Holy Qur'an itself, and it
is not permissible to touch it without having performed *wuḍūʾ*. In the state of major ritual impurity… it
is not allowed to even read this verse as recitation of the Holy Qur'an before having taken a ritual bath.
One may, however, recite it as a form of prayer before beginning a work.”

On *ṣalāh*: “It is a Sunnah to recite ‘Bismillah’ after ‘Aʿūdhu-billāh’ at the very beginning of the first
*rakʿah* in the *ṣalāh*. But views differ as to whether it should be recited in a loud or a low voice.
Imam Abū Ḥanīfah and certain other Imams prefer it to be done in a low voice. There is a consensus on the
point that ‘Bismillah’ should be recited at the beginning of all the succeeding *rakʿahs* too… however, in
some narrations, the reciting of ‘Bismillah’ at the beginning of every *rakʿah* has been identified as
*wājib*.” And on recitation after al-Fātiḥah: “In the course of *ṣalāh*, whether one is reciting the Holy
Qur'an loudly or silently, one should not recite ‘Bismillah’ before beginning a Surah just after the Surah
Fatihah. Such a practice has not been reported either from the Holy Prophet ﷺ or from any of the first four
Khulafāʾ” — the view of Abū Ḥanīfah and Abū Yūsuf preferred in *Sharḥ al-Munyah*, *al-Durr al-Mukhtār*
and *al-Burhān*; “But Imam Muḥammad considers it to be preferable that one should recite ‘Bismillah’ if
one is reciting the Holy Qur'an in a *ṣalāh* offered silently.”

**The Study Quran.** “Whether or not it is considered a numbered verse, in most schools of Islamic Law,
with the exception of the Shāfiʿī and Jaʿfarī schools, the *basmalah* is not recited with any of the
sūrahs during the daily prayers.” Shiite scholars, on the strength of Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq's saying, “all
maintain that the *basmalah* is a verse of the Fātiḥah and of every other sūrah that begins with it (Ṭb,
Ṭs).”

### V11 · Creed & theology (*ʿaqīdah*)

**Al-Alūsī.** The doctrinal nodes of the section: (1) **the name and the named** — "**the Ashʿarīs on the
first** [the name is the very named], **the Muʿtazilah on the second** [other than it]", with al-Rāzī's
"course of futility" verdict, al-Shihāb's and al-Suhaylī's and Ibn al-Sayyid's contributions, and **al-Alūsī's
own promised resolution** — the corpus's page ends before the argument is given, and the editor notes the
abridgement; (2) **the two Names** as the frame of the *bāʾ*'s secrets: "**the attributes are either of beauty
or of majesty, and the first has precedence**, as the ḥadīth *'My mercy precedes My wrath'* indicates; **and
the *bāʾ* of *jarr* is a sign of that**, since it is the medium of annexation and effusion — **so its *kasrah*
and the lowering of the wing suit it**" — with the ḥadīth *'None of you will enter the Garden by his deed —
not even you, Messenger of Allah? — Not even I, unless Allah should cover me with His mercy'*; (3) **the
refutation of the Muʿtazilī "worship is known by reason"** in the maqāṣid section (V/S5 above): "**perhaps he
made praise a partner to worship as a hint that the gratitude of a benefactor is obligatory by reason,
observing the Muʿtazilī doctrine**"; (4) **the *qasamtu al-ṣalāh*'s doctrinal frame** (V5).

**Al-Baghawī.** Gathering the positions he reports on the two names (his condensed *ʿaqīdah* of them):

* **One meaning:** "some said: they are one meaning, like *nadmān* and *nadīm*, and their meaning is
  *Possessor of mercy*; and the mention of one after the other is **to give relish to the hearts of the
  desirous**." **Al-Mubarrid:** "it is **beneficence after beneficence and favour after favour**."
* **Two:** "some distinguished between them: ***al-Raḥmān* is in the sense of generality and *al-Raḥīm* in
  the sense of particularity: *al-Raḥmān* in the sense of the Provider in this world, universal to all
  creation; and *al-Raḥīm* in the sense of the Healer in the next world and the pardon in the next world
  for the believers in particular** — and for that it is said in supplication: ***O Raḥmān of this world and
  Raḥīm of the next***. *Al-Raḥmān* is the one whose mercy reaches creation universally, *al-Raḥīm* the one
  whose mercy reaches them particularly; and for that **other than Allah may be called *raḥīm* but other
  than Allah may not be called *raḥmān***: so **al-Raḥmān is general in meaning, specific in word, and
  al-Raḥīm general in word, specific in meaning**."
* **The attribute of mercy:** "*Al-raḥmah* is **Allah's willing of good for its people**; and it is said:
  **the leaving of punishment for one who deserves it, and the doing of good to one who does not deserve
  it** — so on the first it is **an attribute of the Essence**, and on the second **an attribute of act**."
* And on *the name being the named*, above (V8): the doctrine al-Baghawī states flatly as the sūrah's text
  requires.

**Al-Jalālayn.** The commentary states the identity of the divine names with the essence and its
attributes: *Allāh* names the essence without consideration of the attributes; the names are the
manifestations by which He is known; and *Allāh* is “the Greatest Name of God”.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The basis of the above: "**the nineteenth** — as a consequence of this disagreement falls the
speech about the name and the named"; and "**the eighteenth** … this is the saying of *Ahl al-Sunnah* … and
the saying of the Muʿtazilah is contrary to what the community agreed on". And on the two mercy-names (which
are treated with the basmalah here since the corpus file treats the basmalah by itself): "**the twenty-second**
— disagreement over the derivation of the name *al-Raḥmān*: some said it has no derivation because it is one
of the names peculiar to Him … and because if it is derived from mercy it would not be joined to the mention
of the one shown mercy … and also, **had it been derived from mercy the Arabs would not have denied it when
they heard it**, since they did not deny their Lord's mercy — and Allah said: *wa-idhā qīla lahumu sjudū
li-l-Raḥmāni qālū wa-mā l-Raḥmān* (25:60). And when **ʿAlī** wrote at Ḥudaybiyah by the Prophet's command
*bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*, **Suhayl b. ʿAmr** said: *As for bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm, we do not
know what bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm is; but write what we know: bi-smika llāhumma.*" — "**Ibn al-ʿArabī
said: they were ignorant of the attribute, not of the One described**, proving it by their saying *and what
is al-Raḥmān?* and not *and who is al-Raḥmān?*; **Ibn al-Ḥaṣṣār said: it is as though he had not read the
other verse, *wa-hum yakfurūna bi-l-Raḥmān*** (13:30)." And: "**the majority hold that al-Raḥmān is derived
from mercy, built on intensification, meaning the Possessor of mercy who has no like in it; and therefore it
is neither dualized nor pluralized, as al-Raḥīm is dualized and pluralized**"; Ibn al-Ḥaṣṣār: "of what shows
the derivation is what Tirmidhī narrated, and graded sound, from **ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. ʿAwf** — he heard the
Messenger of Allah ﷺ say: **Allah, Mighty and Exalted, said: I am al-Raḥmān; I created the womb and split
for it a name from My name: whoever joins it I join him, and whoever cuts it off I cut him off** — and this
is a text on the derivation; **so there is no meaning to disagreement**; and the Arabs' denial of it was
their ignorance of Allah and of what is due to Him." "**The twenty-third** — **al-Mubarrid claimed that
al-Raḥmān is a Hebrew (*ʿibrānī*) name**, and therefore *al-Raḥīm* came with it (with the verse of the poem:
*aw tatrukūna ilā l-Qasṣayni hijratakum / wa-masḥakum ṣulbahum raḥmāna qurbānā*) — and Abū Isḥāq al-Zajjāj
said: **this saying is to be turned away from**; and Abū al-ʿAbbās: the attribute may occur for praise; and
Quṭrub: it may be that He joined the two for **emphasis**, and in emphasis is the greatest benefit: 'it is
favour after favour, and beneficence after beneficence, strengthening the ambitions of the desirous, and a
promise whose hoper is not disappointed'" (Muḥammad b. Yazīd). And "**the twenty-fifth** — most scholars hold
that *al-Raḥmān* is **peculiar to Allah, not permitted for any other to be named by it**, by *quli dʿū llāha
awi dʿū l-Raḥmān* (17:110), matching it with the name no other shares… and *wa-sʾal man arsalnā min qablika
min rusulinā a-jaʿalnā min dūni l-Raḥmāni ālihatan yuʿbadūn* (43:45)" — "and **Musaylimah the liar**, may
Allah curse him, dared to be called **Raḥmān al-Yamāmah** … **and Allah made that description (*the liar*) a
mark by which he is known**." And: "**it has been said of His name al-Raḥmān that it is the greatest name of
Allah** — mentioned by Ibn al-ʿArabī." And "**the twenty-fourth** — are they one meaning or two? Abū ʿUbaydah:
one, like *nadmān* and *nadīm*; and it was said: **the pattern *faʿlān* is not like *faʿīl*, for *faʿlān*
falls only on the intensification of the act** — *a man ghaḍbān*, filled with anger — while *faʿīl* may be in
the sense of the doer and the done-to (ʿAmallas: *fa-ammā idhā ʿaḍḍat bika l-ḥarbu ʿaḍḍatan / fa-innaka
maʿṭūfun ʿalayka raḥīm*): **so *al-Raḥmān* is specific in name, general in act, and *al-Raḥīm* is general in
name, specific in act — this is the saying of the majority.**" Abū ʿAlī al-Fārisī: "*al-Raḥmān* is a name
general in all the kinds of mercy, peculiar to Allah; *al-Raḥīm* is only in the direction of the believers,
as He said *wa-kāna bi-l-muʾminīna raḥīmā*." **Al-ʿArzamī**: "*al-Raḥmān* to all His creation in the
provinces and the gifts of the senses and the general blessings; *al-Raḥīm* to the believers in guiding them
and being gentle with them." **Ibn al-Mubārak**: "*al-Raḥmān*, if asked He gives; *al-Raḥīm*, if not asked He
is angry" — and Ibn Mājah and Tirmidhī from Abū Ṣāliḥ from Abū Hurayrah: "*Whoever does not ask Allah, He is
angry with him*" (and the poet: *Allāhu yaghḍabu in tarakta suʾalahū / wa-bunayyu Ādama ḥīna yusʾalu
yaghḍab*). And **Ibn ʿAbbās**: "they are two names, **tender** (*raqīqān*), one tenderer than the other,
i.e. more merciful" — al-Khaṭṭābī: "this is problematic, for tenderness has no entry into any of Allah's
attributes"; al-Ḥusayn b. al-Faḍl al-Bajalī: "this is a confusion of the transmitter: they are two names
**gentle** (*rafīqān*), one gentler than the other, and gentleness is of Allah's attributes" — the Prophet ﷺ:
"*Allah is Gentle and loves gentleness, and gives for gentleness what He does not give for harshness.*" And
"**the twenty-sixth** — *al-Raḥīm* is a description for the created; and because of the generality in
*al-Raḥmān* it was placed before *al-Raḥīm* in our speech, in agreement with the revelation (al-Mahdawī)"
— and: "it was said: the meaning is **by the Raḥīm you reached Allah — so *al-Raḥīm* is the description of
Muḥammad ﷺ**, His description *la-raʾūfun raḥīm* (9:128); as though the meaning were: *by the name of Allah,
al-Raḥmān, and by al-Raḥīm — that is, by Muḥammad ﷺ — you have reached Me*, through following him and what
he brought you reach My reward, My honour and the sight of My face."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** Under “the meaning of *Allāh*”: “it is on the sense of what is related to us from ʿAbd
Allāh b. ʿAbbās — *He whom everything worships and serves* (no. 141: ”*Allāh* is the Possessor of divinity
and of being-worshipped by His creation, all of them“; chain weak, as above). And the doctrine he draws from
the name: “*al-ulūhiyyah* is not for other than Him, in any respect, neither in being-named by it nor in
meaning… and the taking of the name has been forbidden by Allah: so naught is worshipped but He.”

*(The exposition of “al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm” belongs to the same verse in this work; it is recorded under 1:3,
where al-Ṭabarī's own text treats the two names — he explains them once and refers back.)*

**As-Saʿdī.** Faith in Allah's names, His attributes and the rulings of the attributes is one of the
principles on which the salaf of the community and its imams agreed. They believe, for example, that He
is *Raḥmān Raḥīm*, possessing mercy that is an attribute of His and that relates to the one shown
mercy. All blessings are a trace of His mercy. And so it is with all the names: He is *al-ʿAlīm*,
knowledgeable, possessing knowledge by which He knows everything; *al-Qadīr*, capable, possessing power
by which He is able over everything.

**Ibn Kathīr.** The exclusivity of the names: “there are several of Allah's Names that are used as names
for others besides Allah. Further, some of Allah's Names are exclusive for Allah alone, such as *Allah*,
*Ar-Raḥmān*, *Al-Khāliq* (the Creator), *Ar-Rāziq* (the Sustainer), and so forth.” And the doctrinal rule
he draws: “Hence, Allah started the *Tasmiyah* with His Name, *Allah*, and described Himself as *Ar-Raḥmān*,
which is softer and more general than *Ar-Raḥīm*. The most honorable Names are mentioned first, just as
Allah did here.” On *Allāh* he notes it is said to be “the Greatest Name of Allah, because it is referred
to when describing Allah by the various attributes”. He also reports the fate of Musaylimah: “when
Musaylimah the Liar called himself the *Raḥmān* of Yamāmah, Allah made him known by the name ‘Liar’ and
exposed him… Only Musaylimah and those who followed his misguided ways described Musaylimah by
*Ar-Raḥmān*.”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “The word ‘Allāh’ is… the *al-Ism al-Aʿẓam* (the Great Name)… Allah is the name of
that Ultimate Reality which comprehends in Itself all the attributes of perfection, which is the creator
and sustainer, unique and peerless.” And the mercy of the two names is the motive of creation: “It points
to the fact that the creation of the heavens and the earth and the sustenance of the whole universe have
no other motivation than making manifest Allah's quality of mercy. He Himself had no need of these things,
nor could anyone compel Him to create them.” The Persian verse of Rūmī is quoted: “There was nothing —
neither our being nor our claim to be; it was Thy mercy that heard our unsaid.”

### V13 · History, sīrah & parables (*qiṣaṣ · sīrah*)

**Al-Alūsī.** The historical material of the basmalah section: **the writing-phases of the Prophet ﷺ** (from
*bi-smika Allāhummā* to *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*, at 11:41, 17:110 and 27:30 — the report he quotes in
the first research, V2); **Sulaymān's letter** (*innahu min Sulaymāna…*, 27:30) as the model of the completed
formula; **Muʿāwiya's arrival at Madinah and his loud prayer**: "**Anas** — Muʿāwiya came to Madinah and led
the people in a loud prayer, reciting Umm al-Qurʾān **and not reciting the basmalah**; and when he finished,
**the Emigrants and the Helpers called out from every side: 'Did you forget it? Where is *bismi llāhi
l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* when you opened the recitation?' — and Muʿāwiya repeated the prayer and said it aloud.**"
— **al-Alūsī's reading**: "**the utmost its outward indicates — granting it — is that Muʿāwiya, when he did
not recite the basmalah and left the obligatory [act] and did not make the prostration of forgetfulness,
repeated the prayer so that it fall sound from defect; and for this they gave him time until he finished, to
see whether he would repair the defect with the prostration of forgetfulness or not**" — **and his retort**:
"their objecting to him for leaving an obligation repaired by prostration is not stranger than their
objecting to him in that same prayer **for leaving a mode** — as **al-Shāfiʿī himself transmitted, and al-Fakhr
himself carried**, that Muʿāwiya came to Madinah and prayed with them, **not reciting *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni
l-Raḥīm* and not saying the *takbīr* at the inclinations to bowing and prostration**; and when he gave the
salutation, the Emigrants and Helpers called: 'O Muʿāwiya, you have stolen from the prayer — where is *bismi
llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* and where is the *takbīr* at bowing and prostration?' Then he repeated the prayer
with the naming and the *takbīr* — **and this does not harm us**." And: **the era of the *jahr* controversy**
— "the claim that the reports from Anas are six and contradictory, sometimes *jahr* and sometimes concealment,
**because of fear of the Umayyads who opposed ʿAlī**" — his answer (the stronger *isnād* is preferred; the
charge against a transmitter lighter than against Anas); **al-Dāraquṭnī's book on *jahr* and the Mālikīs'
adjuration** (V5); and **the report about Caesar asking Muʿāwiya and then ʿAlī** about the seven dropped
letters — "**it has no basis**" (S3).

**Al-Baghawī.** The historical traces used here: **ʿUmar b. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz**'s instruction to his scribes
(below); and the naming of Makkah "*Umm al-Qurā* … because it is the origin of the lands, the earth being
spread out from beneath it" (cited as the analogue to the sūrah's name *Umm al-Qurʾān*).

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The historical episodes recorded with the basmalah: **the treaty of Ḥudaybiyah** — "when ʿAlī
wrote at Ḥudaybiyah by the Prophet's ﷺ command *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*, Suhayl b. ʿAmr said: *As for
bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm, we do not know what it is; but write what we know: bi-smika llāhumma*"; the
**Musaylimah** episode — "he named himself **Raḥmān al-Yamāmah**, and Allah put on him the description of the
liar by which he is known"; and the story of **Warqah b. Nawfal** and the first call (above, S2); and the
report that **Iblīs wailed four times**; and the incident behind the silent basmalah — "the idolaters used to
attend the mosque, and when the Prophet ﷺ recited the basmalah they said: *this is Muḥammad mentioning the
Raḥmān of al-Yamāmah*" (above, V10).

**Ibn Kathīr.** Two narrations are carried in this section: the report of ʿUmar bin al-Khaṭṭāb, who rode a
*Berthawn* (huge camel) “which started to proceed arrogantly… ʿUmar dismounted the animal and said, ‘By
Allah! You have carried me on a Shaytan’”; and the Qurʾānic history of the devil's enmity — Adam and
Ḥawwāʾ driven from Paradise (7:27), *Surely, Shaytan is an enemy to you, so take him as an enemy* (35:6),
18:50, and Iblīs's vow, *By Your might, then I will surely mislead them all, except Your chosen servants
among them* (38:82–83).

### V14 · Spiritual purification & etiquette (*tazkiyah · ādāb*)

**Al-Alūsī.** The mystical reading of the basmalah — for which he explicitly disclaims completeness: "**and
behind it a great secret, and Allah guides to the right path.**"

> **On the *kasrah* of the *bāʾ***: "**and one of them said, from the way of allusion: the *bāʾ* was given
> *kasrah* in the basmalah to teach [the servant] the means of reaching Allah and attaching to His Names by
> the breaking of one's side and the submission and abasement of servitude — for one reaches no kind of
> knowledge except by a kind of abasement and breaking.**" — and **Ibn al-Fāriḍ**'s lines as al-Alūsī quotes
> them: *"Had you for me been a lowering dot of the *bāʾ*, you would be raised to what no stratagem attains;
> so that you see that you do not see what you counted, and that what you prepared is not a preparation"* —
> "**for lowering corresponds to raising: whoever the regard lowers in the abasement of servitude, destiny
> raises to the witnessing of the might of lordship; and this raising is not attained by stratagem but by the
> sheer divine grant. And whoever lowers himself in order to rise — his lowering is caused and his effort is
> unaccepted.**"
> **On the dot and the *bāʾ***: "**this is a matter peculiar to the *bāʾ* of the basmalah; it cannot run in the
> *bāʾ* of *jarr* absolutely**… **and with me the secret of that is that the *bāʾ* is the second rank in
> relation to the simple *alif*, stripped, preceding all ranks — so it is the sign of the Real Existence; and
> the *bāʾ* is a sign of His attributes, which the dot of the [letter's] being displays.**" — and: "when
> **al-Shiblī** was asked, 'Are you al-Shiblī?' he said: '**I am the dot beneath the *bāʾ*.'**" — and
> **al-Shaykh al-Akbar**'s verses as al-Alūsī quotes them: *"The *bāʾ* for the gnostic al-Shiblī is a
> consideration, and in its small dot for the heart a reminder; the secret of the lofty servitude blended into
> it — therefore it stood in the station of the Real, so consider; is not its reality dropped from *bismi*?
> because it is a substitute for it, and that is a burden"* — and his account that "**the attributes are of
> beauty or majesty, and the first has precedence**, as the ḥadīth *'My mercy precedes My wrath'* indicates,
> **and the *bāʾ* of *jarr* is a sign of it**… so the *kasrah* suited it and the lowering of the wing, **that
> the matter be complete and the secret appear**; and in beginning with it here is a hastening of the good
> news and a symbol that **the pivot is mercy**… **and He graduated in showing it: He symbolised with the
> *bāʾ*, pointed with *Allāh*, and made the fullest declaration with *al-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm***." And: "**and
> without exception every sūrah — the Lord opened it with a symbol to His state ﷺ, out of magnifying him and
> as good news for him who gives ear while he is a witness**; and when majesty was outward in the Sūrat
> al-Barāʾah, He left the basmalah's sign and brought **an opened *bāʾ*** — for the changed state — and drew
> the curtain over the brides of beauty; **and He did not leave the symbol altogether to the Muḥammadan
> Reality**… **and we cannot be more explicit than this in this chapter for fear of the people of the veil;
> and behind it is a great secret.**"
> **And the ascension of the meanings** (S5 above): "**the meanings of the Books in the Qurʾān, of the Qurʾān
> in the Fātiḥah, of the Fātiḥah in the basmalah, of the basmalah in the *bāʾ***" — with: "**and its being
> dropped in *bismi*: because it is a substitute for it**."
> **And the etiquette of beginning**: "**and in the beginning with it here is a hastening of the good news**
>… **and the first of the rain is a drizzle, then it pours**."

**Al-Jalālayn.** The basmalah is read as the entry into the human perfection: the Prophet ﷺ was sent “to
complete the noble character traits”. “For words constitute the realities (*ḥaqāʾiq*) of existents
(*mawjūdāt*) and their concrete substances (*aʿyān*), just as Jesus — peace be upon him — was called a
Word from God [cf. 3:39; 4:171].” The noble character traits “are the perfections of these existents and
represent their specificities, constituting the sources of their actions in their entirety, contained as
they are in the comprehensive human microcosm (*kawn insānī*).” A subtlety is noted: “the prophets —
peace be upon them — have made the letters of the alphabet analogous to the ranks of existents (*marātib
al-mawjūdāt*)”.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The twenty-seventh** — related from ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭālib, may Allah honour his face, that he
said of His word *bismi llāh*: **it is a cure from every disease and a help to every remedy**; and as for
*al-Raḥmān*, **it is a help for everyone who believes in Him, and it is a name by which no other is named**;
and as for *al-Raḥīm*, **it is for the one who repents, believes and does righteous deeds**." And the
letter-by-letter report: "some explained it by the letters — related from **ʿUthmān b. ʿAffān** that he asked
the Messenger of Allah ﷺ about the explanation of *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* and he said: *As for the
bāʾ, it is the affliction of Allah and His spirit and His radiance and His splendour; as for the sīn, the
eminence of Allah; as for the mīm, the dominion of Allah; as for Allāh, there is no god but He; as for
al-Raḥmān, **the One who inclines to the righteous and the sinful of His creation**; as for al-Raḥīm, **the
Gentle with the believers in particular***." And **Kaʿb al-Aḥbār**: "*the bāʾ is His splendour (bahāʾuhu),
the sīn His eminence than which nothing is higher, and the mīm His dominion, and He is over all things
powerful so that nothing rivals Him*." And: "it has been said that every letter is the opening of one of His
names: the *bāʾ* the key of His name *Baṣīr*, the *sīn* of *Samīʿ*, the *mīm* of *Malīk*, the *alif* of
*Allāh*, the *lām* of *Laṭīf*, the *hāʾ* of *Hādī*, the *rāʾ* of *Razzāq*, the *ḥāʾ* of *Ḥalīm*, the *nūn*
of *Nūr* — **and the meaning of all this is calling upon Allah, Mighty and Exalted, at the opening of
everything**."

And the etiquette *masāʾilah* of the basmalah — "**the eighth** — the Sharīʿah's urging to mention the
basmalah at the beginning of every act, such as eating, drinking, slaughtering, marital relations, purity and
riding the sea, and other acts than that": *fa-kulū mimmā dhukira smu llāhi ʿalayh* (6:118) and *wa-qāla
irkabū fīhā bismi llāhi majrāhā wa-mursāhā* (11:41); and the Prophet's ﷺ: "*Lock your door and mention the
name of Allah, put out your lamp and mention the name of Allah, cover your vessel and mention the name of
Allah, tie your waterskin and mention the name of Allah*"; and "*If one of you, when he intends to come to his
wife, says:* bismi llāhi, allāhumma jannibnā l-shayṭān wa-jannibi l-shayṭāna mā razaqtanā, *then if a child
is decreed between them, no Satan will ever harm him*"; and to ʿUmar b. Abī Salamah: "*O boy, name Allah, eat
with your right hand, and eat from what is next to you*"; and "*Satan makes lawful the food over which the
name of Allah is not mentioned*"; and "*Whoever does not slaughter, let him slaughter with the name of
Allah*"; and to ʿUthmān b. Abī al-ʿĀṣ, who complained of a pain in his body since he became Muslim: "*Place
your hand on the part of your body that hurts and say* bismi llāh *three times, and say seven times:* aʿūdhu
bi-ʿizzati llāhi wa-qudratihi min sharri mā ajidu wa-uḥādhir*; then he said: "all of this is established in
the *Ṣaḥīḥ*, and al-Qurṭubī says: *I hold the well-known view of the people of language is* basmala." And
the rulings (commands/rulings on touching)? He adds: "**the sixth** — the community agreed on the
permissibility of writing *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* at the head of every book of knowledge and letters;
if the book is a *dīwān* of poetry, Mujālid from al-Shaʿbī related: **they agreed not to write *bismi llāhi
l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* before poetry**; al-Zuhrī: **the Sunnah has passed that they not write it in poetry**;
Saʿīd b. Jubayr held to writing the basmalah at the head of books of poetry and most later scholars followed
him — Abū Bakr al-Khaṭīb: *that is what we choose and prefer*." And the ḥadīths of the Sunan: "*the screen
between the jinn and the private parts of the sons of Adam, when one enters the privy, is his saying* bismi
llāh" (Ibn Mājah, Tirmidhī); and Dāraqutnī from ʿĀʾishah: "*when the Messenger of Allah ﷺ touched his
purification water he named Allah, Mighty and Exalted, then poured the water over his hands*."

**Ibn Kathīr.** The section on *istiʿādhah* — recorded here because it belongs to the etiquette of
recitation, not to the words of 1:1 — establishes its meaning and merits: “*Istiʿādhah* means, ‘I seek
refuge with Allah from the cursed Satan so that he is prevented from affecting my religious or worldly
affairs, or hindering me from adhering to what I was commanded, or luring me into what I was prohibited
from.’” Its virtues: “The *Istiʿādhah* cleanses the mouth from the foul speech that it has indulged in. It
also purifies the mouth and prepares it to recite the speech of Allah. Further, the *Istiʿādhah* entails
seeking Allah's help and acknowledging His ability to do everything. The *Istiʿādhah* also affirms the
servant's meekness, weakness and inability to face the enemy of his inner evil, whom Allah alone, Who
created this enemy, is able to repel and defeat.” It is said “before you recite the Qur'an”, with the
proofs of 16:98–100 and 5:6; and when one is angry, as in the two men who disputed before the Prophet ﷺ
and the words “*I know of a statement which if he said it, will make what he feels disappear: I seek
