refuge with Allah from the cursed Satan.*”

The basmalah is likewise an etiquette of every act: “Basmalah is recommended before starting any action or
deed” — before the *khutbah*, before entering the place of relieving oneself, before ablution (*There is
no valid ablution for he who did not mention Allah's Name in it*), before eating (*Say Bismillah, eat with
your right hand and eat from whatever is next to you*), and before marital relations (*In the Name of
Allah. O Allah! Protect us from Satan and also protect what you grant us…*).

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** As above: the sacralisation of the whole of daily life by the basmalah — “his
economic and worldly needs and activities acquire the nature of the remembrance of Allah, and are counted
as acts of worship”. And: “*Bismillah* is the legendary philosopher's stone which transmutes, not copper,
but mere dust into the purest of gold.”

**The Study Quran.** “The *basmalah*, or its shortened form, *In the Name of God*, is employed by pious
Muslims to consecrate all licit actions, since from a Quranic perspective all things should be performed
for the sake of God and in His Name.”

### V15 · Wisdom, counsel & admonition (*ḥikam · tadabbur*)

**Al-Alūsī.** His counsels within the section: on **the ordering of the *bāʾ*'s meanings**: "**in it is a
hint from the first glance of the dropping of one's might and power, and the negation of the creature's
independence and influence; it is the opening of the gate of mercy**"; on **the estimate of the governor**:
"**the estimate postponed is distanced from the field of verification**"; and, in the great refutation,
**the ethical frame he repeats**: "**the fair-mindedness is half of religion, and submission to the truth is
of the believers' character**"; "**and do not let the saying of a great one (*al-Fakhr*) be taken as
authority in the ḥadīths — he is not one of their memorisers**"; and against the **fanaticism of the pen**:
"**the saying of some on this is the utmost tyranny of the pen and a limit of partisanship without
mastery**"; and the traveller-of-thought image: "**so we shall not keep awake the eyelid of thought over what
is for it and against it**"; and his closing image from the poems of the debate: *"The son of the she-camel,
when bound in a neck-rope, cannot [match] the attack of the full-grown stallions"*.

**Al-Baghawī.** From the sūrah's own sense of *basmalah*: everything is opened **"for seeking blessing"**;
and the report he cites of **ʿUmar b. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz** ordering his scribes to **lengthen the *bāʾ*, make
plain the *sīn*, space them, and round the *mīm*, out of magnifying the Book of Allah** — the reverent
script-*ādāb* of the name.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** In the fourth *bāb*'s frame — "on what al-Fātiḥah contains of meanings, readings, *iʿrāb*,
and the virtue of those who praise" — he opens with the ḥadīth report of Abū Muhammad ʿAbd al-Ghanī b. Saʿīd
al-Ḥāfiẓ from Abū Hurayrah and Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī: the Prophet ﷺ said: "*When the servant says* al-ḥamdu
li-llāh, *Allah says: My servant has spoken the truth; praise is Mine*"; and Muslim from Anas: "*Allah is
pleased with the servant who eats a meal and praises Him for it, or drinks a drink and praises Him for it*";
and **al-Ḥasan**: "there is no blessing but that *al-ḥamdu li-llāh* is better than it"; and Ibn Mājah from
Anas: "*Allah has not bestowed a blessing on a servant and he says* al-ḥamdu li-llāh, *but that what He gave
is better than what he took*"; and in *Nawādir al-Uṣūl*: "*Were the whole world, with all its furnishings, in
the hand of a man of my community, and then he said* al-ḥamdu li-llāh, *al-ḥamdu li-llāh would be better than
that*" — "the meaning: he has been given the world and then given this word after it, so the word is better,
because the world perishes and the word remains — it is of the *bāqiyāt al-ṣāliḥāt*, *wa-l-bāqiyātu
l-ṣāliḥātu khayrun ʿinda rabbika thawāban wa-khayrun amalā* (19:76)"; and the ḥadīth of the servant who said
"*O Lord, to You belongs praise as befits the majesty of Your face and the greatness of Your sovereignty*" —
"and it was too much for the two angels, so they did not know how to write it, and they went up to heaven and
said: *O our Lord, Your servant has said a saying we do not know how to write*; and Allah, who knows best
what His servant said, said: *What did My servant say?* They said:… He said: *Write it as My servant said it,
until he meets Me and I repay him for it*"; and Muslim from Abū Mālik al-Ashʿarī: "*Purity is half of faith,
and* al-ḥamdu li-llāh *fills the scale, and* subḥāna llāh wa-l-ḥamdu li-llāh *fill, or fill what is between,
the heaven and the earth*."

### V16 · Contemporary issues & practical application

**Al-Alūsī.** In this 19th-century *Rūḥ al-Maʿānī* section, the contemporary application is the polemical
scene itself: (1) **the community's practice**: the obligation to **name Allah over the prayer and to
prostrate for its omission** (V10), and the reminder "**so let the servant consider where he stands**"; (2)
**the intra-school contestation** — al-Alūsī's self-description (a Shāfiʿī-formed scholar who became Ḥanafī)
and his ruling on the debate: "**the more cautious is that it is obligatory, and I hold it, contrary to Qāḍī
Khān, the author of *al-Khulāṣah* and others — and the truth is worthier of being followed**"; (3) **the
cross-confessional challenge**: on the Shīʿī claim of *tawātur* from ʿAlī — "**rejected with Ahl al-Sunnah**,
the Shīʿah claimed it and applied it to *jahr* in both prayers; **and were one to act on all they allege as
*mutawātir* from the Commander he would disbelieve — so there is only faith in part and disbelief in part**";
(4) **the charge of lying and the standing of the Companions**: "**the charge of lying against a transmitter
who opposes [the majority] on Anas is lighter with me than the charge of lying on Anas — the companion of the
Messenger of Allah — against the Messenger of Allah and the foremost of his Companions**."

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The reports that set the basmalah in the history of the earlier revelations: "**the third** —
al-Shaʿbī and al-Aʿmash related that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ used to write *bi-smika llāhumma* until he was
commanded to write *bismi llāh* and wrote it; then when *quli dʿū llāha awi dʿū l-Raḥmān* came down he wrote
*bismi llāhi l-Raḥmān*; and when *innahu min Sulaymāna wa-innahu bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* (27:30) came
down he wrote it." And in Abū Dāwūd's *Muṣannaf*: "al-Shaʿbī, Abū Mālik, Qatādah and Thābit b. ʿUmārah said:
**the Prophet ﷺ did not write *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* until Sūrat al-Naml came down**." And al-Ḥasan:
"*bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* did not come down in anything of the Qurʾān except in *Ṭā Sīn*, *innahu min
Sulaymāna wa-innahu bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*" — and al-Jurayrī said al-Ḥasan was asked about the
basmalah and said: "**in the openings of letters**". And **Ibn ʿAbbās's** words on the *Āmīn*: "the Jews
envied you for nothing as they envied you for *al-salām* and *al-taʾmīn*" (the ḥadīth of ʿĀʾishah and of Ibn
ʿAbbās in Ibn Mājah: "*The Jews envied you for nothing so much as for* Āmīn, *so say* Āmīn *much*") — "and the
people of the Book envied us because its beginning is praise of Allah and extolling of Him, then submission
to Him and humbling before Him, then a supplication for us for guidance to the straight path, then a
supplication against them, with our saying *Āmīn*." And of Moses and Aaron (in the *Āmīn* chapter): "*the
word* Āmīn *was not before us except for Mūsā and Hārūn*" (Tirmidhī al-Ḥakīm's report from Anas: "*Allah gave
my community three things not given to anyone before them: the salām — which is the greeting of the people of
the Garden, the rows of the angels, and* Āmīn — *except what was from Mūsā and Hārūn*"), explained by 10:89,
*qad ujībat daʿwatukumā*, "where Mūsā supplicated against Pharaoh and Hārūn said *Āmīn*, and Allah named
Hārūn a suppliant although it was from him only the *Āmīn*, since He made that from him a supplication."

### V17 · Specific virtues & supplications (*faḍāʾil · duʿāʾ*)

**Al-Alūsī.** The basmalah's specific merits in this section: **the report of Ubayy b. Kaʿb** — "*What is the
greatest verse in the Qurʾān?* — *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* — and the Prophet confirmed him" — with
al-Alūsī's use of it (V7's tenth proof) and his note; **the four-witness standing of the *taqṭīʿ* reports**;
the **Umm Salamah**, **Abū Hurayrah**, **ʿAlī's** and **Ibn ʿAbbās's** testimonies to its being *one of its
verses* (V5); **the Fātiḥah's own faḍāʾil** (*"equal to two-thirds of the Qurʾān"*, the Abū al-Dardāʾ
report, *al-Ruqyah / al-Shifāʾ / al-Shāfiyah*) — **filed at S4 above**, since the sūrah-level faḍāʾil of the
corpus belong to the sūrah introduction, not to a verse; and **the *qasamtu al-ṣalāh* ḥadīth**, a *ḥadīth
qudsī* of the Fātiḥah as dialogue in prayer (V5).

**Al-Baghawī.** For the *fāʾil* of the basmalah, the author's own summary belongs to the sūrah's opening: as
the sūrah's three names, its Makkan descent and the seven-verse count are given under S1–S3, so the
*basmalah*'s reward-claims come from the faḍāʾil section (S4) — the "two lights" ḥadīth and the promise
"**you will not recite a letter of them but you are given it**".

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The *basmalah*'s protection-reports: **Ibn Masʿūd's** "whoever wishes Allah to save him from
the nineteen *zabāniyah*, let him recite the basmalah, so that Allah makes for him a shield with every letter
of it" (nineteen letters by 74:30, *ʿalayhā tisʿata ʿashar*); and Ibn ʿAṭiyyah's caveat. And ʿAlī's: *bismi
llāh* "a cure from every disease and a help to every remedy; *al-Raḥmān* a help for everyone who believes in
Him; *al-Raḥīm* for the one who repents, believes and acts righteously"; and the report of "the four verses
suspended by the Throne" (S1).

**Ibn Kathīr.** The merit of saying the basmalah over any act: “This is the blessing of reciting
Bismillah.” And the supplication with which the Prophet ﷺ opened the night prayer, recorded by Imam
Aḥmad from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī — “*All praise is due to You, O Allah, and also the thanks. Blessed be
Your Name, Exalted be Your sovereignty, and there is no deity worthy of worship except You*” — followed by
“*I seek refuge with Allah, the Hearing, the Knowing, from the cursed Satan, from his coercion, lures to
arrogance and poems.*”


## 1:2 — الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ رَبِّ الْعَالَمِينَ

*[context]* All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

### V2 · Running exposition (*bayān*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "*Al-ḥamdu li-llāh* — **its wording is a report (*khabar*), as though He reports that the one
deserving of praise is Allah, Mighty and Exalted; and in it is the teaching of creation, its sense being
"say: *al-ḥamdu li-llāh*".** And *al-ḥamd* is **in the sense of thanks for a blessing, and in the sense of
praise of Him for the praiseworthy traits in Him**: one says *ḥamidtu fulānan ʿalā mā asdā ilayya mina
l-niʿmah* (I praised so-and-so for the blessing he conferred on me) **and** *ḥamidtuhu ʿalā ʿilmihi wa
shajāʿatihi* (I praised him for his knowledge and his courage) — **while *al-shukr* is only for a blessing;
so *al-ḥamd* is more general than *al-shukr*, since one does not say *shakartu fulānan ʿalā ʿilmihi*; every
praiser is a thank-er and not every thank-er is a praiser.** And it is said: **al-ḥamd is with the tongue in
speech, and al-shukr is with the limbs in deed** — Allah said *wa-quli l-ḥamdu li-llāhi lladhī lam yattakhidh
waladā* (17:111), and said *iʿmalū āla Dāwūda shukrā* (34:13)."
"*Lillāh* — **the *lām* in it is for entitlement, as one says *al-dāru li-Zayd* (the house is Zayd's).**"
And the exposition of *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* to *al-Raḥīm*: **the *Rabb* is in the sense of the owner**, as one
says to the owner of the house, *rabb al-dār*; and one says *rabb al-shayʾ* when he owns it. **And it is in
the sense of nurture and setting right**, as one says *rabba fulānun al-ḍayʿah yarubbuhā* when he completes
it and sets it right — so he is *rabb*, like *ṭabb* and *barr*." "**So Allah, Mighty and Exalted, is the
owner of the worlds and their nurturer**; and the creature is not called *al-rabb* with the definite
article — one says only *rabb kadhā*, annexed, **because the *alif* and *lām* are for comprehension and he
does not own all.**"

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The third** — the Muslims are agreed that **Allah is praised for all His blessings, and
that of what Allah has blessed with is *īmān*; and this proves that *īmān* is His act and His creation**, and
the proof is His word *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* — and *al-ʿālamūn* is the totality of created things, and of that
totality is *īmān* — **not as the *Qadariyyah* say, that it is a creation belonging to them**." And "**the
sixth** — Allah praised Himself with *al-ḥamd* and opened His Book with His praise, and He did not permit
others to do that, but forbade them in His Book and on the tongue of His Prophet ﷺ: *fa-lā tuzakkū anfusakum,
huwa aʿlamu bi-mani ttqā* (53:32); and he ﷺ said: *Throw dust in the faces of the flatterers* (related by
al-Miqdād)." And its sense: "**the meaning of** *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn* **is: praise has preceded
from Me to Myself before any of the worlds might praise Me — My praise of Myself to Myself in eternity was not
for a cause, while My praise of creation is mixed with causes**… and when He knew His servants' inability to
praise Him, **He praised Himself by Himself for Himself in eternity**; and the exhaustion of the servants'
capacity is itself the place of their inability before His praise — do you not see the master of the
messengers how he showed inability, saying: *I cannot count Your praise* — and the poet: *if we praise You
with good, You are as we praise, and above what we praise*." And "it was said: He praised Himself in eternity
because He knew the abundance of His blessings on His servants and their inability to fulfil the duty of His
praise, so He praised Himself on their behalf, that the blessing may be more delightful to them, since He
dropped from them by that the burden of obligation."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The sense: “*al-ḥamdu li-llāh* is thanks rendered purely to Allah, without any of the things
worshipped besides Him and without any of what He created, for the blessings He has bestowed on His
servants — blessings that no number can count, in fitting the instruments for His obedience, enabling the
limbs of those under obligation to perform His ordinances, and with the provision He spread for them in
their world and the ease of living He fed them with, without their deserving it of Him, and with what He
alerted them to and called them to of the means leading to lasting immortality in the abode of enduring
bliss. So to our Lord is all praise for that, first and last.” The reports he cites for it: **Ibn ʿAbbās**
(no. 151, weak chain): “*al-ḥamdu li-llāh* is thanks to Allah, abasement before Allah, and the
acknowledgement of His blessing and His guidance and His beginning”; **the Prophet ﷺ** through al-Ḥakam b.
ʿUmayr (no. 152, chain declared by the corpus editor as not standing at all): “*When you say,* al-ḥamdu
lillāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn, *you have thanked Allah and He will grant you more*”; **Kaʿb al-Aḥbār** (no. 153,
sound chain but reaching Kaʿb): “*Whoever says* al-ḥamdu lillāh, *that is praise of Allah*”; and
**al-Aswad b. Sarīʿ** (no. 154, sound chain, and Aḥmad's *Musnad* carries it with a sound chain):
“*Nothing is more beloved to Him than praise, from Allah; and therefore He praised Himself, saying* al-ḥamdu
lillāh.” The distinction he reports: “it has been said that the speaker's words *al-ḥamdu li-llāh* are
praise of Allah by His names and His most beautiful attributes, while his words *al-shukru li-llāh* are
praise of Him by His blessings and His favours” — and Kaʿb is said to have said *al-ḥamdu li-llāh* is praise
of Allah, without making clear which of the two senses.

**As-Saʿdī.** *Al-ḥamd* is praise of Allah with the attributes of perfection and with His acts, which
move between grace and justice; all praise is His, complete, in every respect. *Rabb al-ʿālamīn*: the
*Rabb* is the one who nurtures all the worlds — and they are all that is besides Allah — by creating
them, preparing for them their means, and favouring them with great blessings which, if they lost them,
they could not continue. Whatever blessing they have is from Him.

**Ibn ʿAbbās** — *on his authority it is related that Ibn ʿAbbās said, regarding the saying of Allah,
Exalted is He, "Praise be to Allah":*

*On "Praise be to Allah" three reports are given:*

1. "He says: laudation is Allah's in that He is kind towards His created beings and, hence, they praise Him."
2. "It is also said that laudation is Allah's because of His excessive blessings upon His servants whom He has guided to believe in Him."
3. "It is also said: laudation, absolute unity and godhood all belong to Allah Who has neither progeny, partner, helper nor an assistant."

*On "Lord of the worlds" three reports are given:*

1. "Lord of any that has a spirit and walks about on the face of the earth and also of the dwellers of heaven."
2. "It is also said that this means: the Master of the jinn and human beings."
3. "It is also said that (Lord of the worlds) means the Creator of all created beings, their Provider and the One Who changes them from one state into another."

_Cross-heads: V11 — the third report names the exclusive divine prerogatives (absolute unity and godhood,
no progeny, partner, helper or assistant)._

**Ibn Kathīr.** Abū Jaʿfar bin Jarīr said: “The meaning of *Al-Ḥamdu Lillāh* (all praise and thanks be to
Allah) is: all thanks are due purely to Allah, alone, not any of the objects that are being worshipped
instead of Him, nor any of His creation. These thanks are due to Allah's innumerable favors and bounties,
that only He knows the amount of… All thanks and praise are due to Allah for these favors from beginning
to end.” Ibn Jarīr also said it means “a praise that Allah praised Himself with, indicating to His
servants that they too should praise Him, as if Allah had said, ‘Say: All thanks and praise is due to
Allah’”, and that the statement “entails praising Allah by mentioning His most beautiful Names and most
honorable Attributes”.

On *Ar-Rabb*: “the owner who has full authority over his property… linguistically means the master or the
one who has the authority to lead. All of these meanings are correct for Allah. When it is alone, the word
*Rabb* is used only for Allah”, and “it was reported that *Ar-Rabb* is Allah's Greatest Name”. On
*al-ʿālamīn*: “*Al-ʿālamīn* is plural for *ʿĀlam*, which encompasses everything in existence except
Allah… The *ʿĀlamīn* are different creations that exist in the heavens and the earth, on land and at sea.
Every generation of creation is called an *ʿĀlam*.”

*The difference between praise and thanks:* “*Ḥamd* is more general, in that it is a statement of praise
for one's characteristics, or for what he has done. Thanks are given for what was done, not merely for
characteristics.”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** On *al-ḥamd*: “The Surah begins with the words *Al-ḥamdu lillāh*, signifying that
all praise essentially belongs to Allah. Whosoever praises anything anywhere in the world is ultimately
praising Allah… Admiring anything that exists in the created world is no more than showing one's
admiration for a work of art or craft, which in fact is a praise of the artist or the craftsman.” On
*Rabb*: “Lexically, the word *Rabb* signifies ‘one who nurtures’. And ‘nurture’ implies developing a
thing by gradual stages in a manner which is conducive to its own good till it attains perfection. The
word *Rabb* is exclusive to the sacred Being of Allah, and cannot be employed in the case of any created
being without adding some qualification.” On *al-ʿālamīn*: “*Al-ʿālamīn* is the plural of *ʿālam* (world,
universe, kingdom). ‘The worlds’ include all possible forms of existence: the sky, the earth, the sun, the
moon, stars, wind and rain, the angels, the jinns, animals, plants, minerals, and, of course, men. So ‘the
Lord of all the worlds’ means that Allah alone gives nurture to all the forms of existents that are to be
found in this universe, or in the millions of universes that may lie beyond our own.” Imām Rāzī's argument
is given at length: “the existence of an indefinite space beyond our universe can be proved on the basis of
rational argument, and it is also certain that Allah is All-Powerful, so it should not be at all difficult
for Him to have created millions of other universes in this endless space.” The reports: Abū Saʿīd
al-Khudrī — “there are forty thousand worlds; our world, stretching from the East to the West, is only one
of them”; Muqātil — eighty thousand. And Rāzī's answer to the objection from the conditions of life in
space: “the inhabitants of the worlds in the outer space need not necessarily have the same physical
make-up as that of the inhabitants of our world”. The argument closes: “When Allah alone is the ultimate
cause for the nurture of the whole universe, He alone can, in reality, be worthy of praise. Thus, the
first verse of the Surah… combines in itself the praise of Allah and a subtle indication of the first and
basic principle of the Islamic creed — the oneness of God.”

**The Study Quran.** “*Praise* translates *al-ḥamd*, which indicates extolling the Praiseworthy (*maḥmūd*)
and giving thanks to Him for all of the favors He has bestowed in this world and for the reward that will
be given in the next world.” The distinction: “whereas thanks (*shukr*) is given for what one has already
received, praise is given for the qualities the One Who is praised possesses prior to having bestowed
anything and is thus more universal (Q). Praise (*al-ḥamd*) is rendered in the definite rather than the
indefinite to indicate that all forms of praise and all gratitude belong to God (Ṭ).” And: “It is said that
God has praised Himself in this opening address so that human beings can praise God in the speech of God,
since God knows that they cannot praise Him fully in their own words (Qu)” — with the Prophet's words,
*“There is no way to enumerate the praise due to Thee; Thou art as Thou hast praised Thyself”* (Qu). The
authority of *Rabb*: “*Lord* renders *rabb*, which refers to a master who is obeyed (*sayyid*), to one who
puts matters in their proper order, and to one who possesses something. In reference to God it thus means
that He is the Master without peer, Who arranges the affairs of all His creatures and to Whom all of
creation belongs (Ṭ). Some also relate *Lord* (*rabb*) to ‘cultivation’ (*tarbiyah*), since God is the
Caretaker (*murabbī*) of all things as well as the Trainer and Caretaker of our souls, hearts, and spirits
(Qu).” The scope of *al-ʿālamīn*: “*The worlds* refers to various levels of cosmic existence and the
communities of beings within each level. Some say it refers to four communities: human beings, jinn,
angels, and satans (Q), while others say it refers only to human beings and jinn, since the Prophet is
referred to as a warner unto the worlds (25:1), and only jinn and human beings are in need of a warner
(Q)… Thus some say that in the most universal sense *the worlds* refers to all existent things other than
God (IK, Q).” And: “the commentator Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī notes that there is infinite space beyond this
world and that God can actualize all possibilities, even worlds and universes of which we have no
knowledge… there is no thing, save that it hymns His praise (17:44).” On etiquette: “whereas the *basmalah*
is employed to consecrate a deed at its beginning, *Praise be to God* is employed to thank God for an act
or event upon its completion. According to traditional Islamic etiquette, whenever one is asked how one is
feeling, the correct response should be *Praise be to God*, no matter one's condition.”

### V4 · Qurʾān cross-references (*tafsīr al-Qurʾān bi-l-Qurʾān*)

**Al-Baghawī.** The verse-senses of *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* are fixed by: 25:1 (*li-yakūna li-l-ʿālamīna nadhīrā*
— so the *ʿālamīn* are those addressed); 26:23–24 (*rabbu l-samāwāti wa-l-arḍi wa-mā baynahumā* — so they
are all created things); and 74:31 (*wa-mā yaʿlamu junūda rabbika illā huwa* — so their number is unknown).

**Al-Qurṭubī.** On His word *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* as it bears on what follows: "**God described Himself after
*Rabb al-ʿālamīn* as *al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm*** (recorded under S6), and the *Rabb* is what makes the later
*māliki yawmi l-dīn* a **specific after a general** — see under 1:4, where Abū ʿAlī's answer to the claim of
repetition is given."

**Ibn Kathīr.** On *al-ʿālamīn*, 26:23–24: *Firʿawn said: “And what is the Lord of the ʿālamīn?” Mūsā
said: “The Lord of the heavens and the earth, and all that is between them, if you seek to be convinced
with certainty”* — showing the scope of the term.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** 38:27: *We have not created in vain the heavens and the earth and what lies
between them. That is the fancy of the disbelievers*; and 51:56: *I have not created the jinn and mankind
except to worship Me* — the universe is not absurd, and man in it is not purposeless.

**The Study Quran.** 25:1 for the Prophet as “a warner unto the worlds”; 13:16; 17:102; 18:14; 19:65;
21:56; 26:24; 37:5; 38:66; 43:82; 44:7; 78:37 for *Lord of the heavens and the earth*; and 17:44 — *there
is no thing, save that it hymns His praise*.

### V5 · Prophetic traditions (ḥadīth)

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The ḥadīths of the *ḥamd* (first *masʾalah* of the fourth *bāb*, the *ḥamd* chapter): the
report of Abū Muhammad ʿAbd al-Ghanī al-Ḥāfiẓ from Abū Hurayrah **and** Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī — the Prophet ﷺ:
"*When the servant says* al-ḥamdu li-llāh, *Allah says: My servant has spoken the truth — praise is Mine*";
Muslim from Anas: "*Allah is pleased with the servant who eats a meal and praises Him for it, or drinks a
drink and praises Him for it*"; Ibn Mājah from Anas: "*Allah has not bestowed a blessing on a servant and he
says* al-ḥamdu li-llāh *but that what He gave is better than what he took*"; *Nawādir al-Uṣūl*: "*Were the
whole world, with all its furnishings, in the hand of a man of my community and then he said* al-ḥamdu
li-llāh, *al-ḥamdu li-llāh would be better than that*"; Ibn Mājah from Ibn ʿUmar: the two angels and the
servant's "*O Lord, to You belongs praise as befits the majesty of Your face and the greatness of Your
sovereignty*" — "write it as My servant said, until he meets Me and I repay him for it"; Muslim from Abū Mālik
al-Ashʿarī: "*Purity is half of faith, and* al-ḥamdu li-llāh *fills the scale, and* subḥāna llāh wa-l-ḥamdu
li-llāh *fill what is between the heaven and the earth*."

**Ibn Kathīr.** The virtues of *al-ḥamd*, recorded in this section:

* Imam Aḥmad from al-Aswad bin Sarīʿ: “I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah! Should I recite to you words of
  praise for My Lord that I have collected?’ He said: *Verily, your Lord likes Al-Hamd.*”
* At-Tirmidhī, an-Nasāʾī and Ibn Mājah from Jābir bin ʿAbdullāh: “*The best Dhikr is Lā ilāha illallāh
  and the best supplication is Al-Ḥamdu lillāh.*”
* Ibn Mājah from Anas bin Mālik: “*No servant is blessed by Allah and says ‘Al-Ḥamdu Lillāh’, except that
  what he was given is better than that which he has himself acquired.*”
* Ibn Mājah from Ibn ʿUmar: the servant who said, “*O Allah! Yours is the Ḥamd that is suitable for the
  grace of Your Face and the greatness of Your Supreme Authority*” — and the two angels were taught to
  “*Write it as My servant has said it, until he meets Me and then I shall reward him for it.*”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** The ḥadīth of the division of the prayer, from the *Ṣaḥīḥ* of Muslim on the
authority of Abū Hurayrah: “Allah has said, ‘The *ṣalāh* (i.e., the Surah al-Fatihah) is equally divided
between Me and My servant. And My servant shall be given what he prays for.’” The responses are then given
verse by verse: on *Praise belongs to Allah, the Lord of all the worlds* — “My servant has paid his homage
to Me”; on *The All-Merciful, the Very-Merciful* — “My servant has praised Me”; on *The Master of the Day
of Judgment* — “My servant has proclaimed my greatness”; on *You alone we worship, and to You alone we
pray for help* — “This verse is common to Me and My servant”; on *Guide us in the straight path* — “All
this is there for My servant. He shall be given what he prays for.” *(Mazharī.)*

**The Study Quran.** “In this vein, the Prophet is reported to have said, *‘When you say, “Praise be to
God, Lord of the worlds,” you will have thanked God and He will increase your bounty’* (Ṭ).” And the
Prophet's address: *“There is no way to enumerate the praise due to Thee; Thou art as Thou hast praised
Thyself”* (Qu).

### V6 · Companions & Successors (āthār)

**Al-Baghawī.** The authorities he names for the two senses of the verse: **Ibn ʿAbbās**, **Qatādah,
Mujāhid, al-Ḥasan**, **Abū ʿUbaydah**, **Saʿīd b. al-Musayyib**, **Muqātil b. Ḥayyān**, **Wahb**, **Kaʿb
al-Aḥbār** — as above.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** **Ibn ʿAbbās**: "*al-ḥamdu li-llāh* is **the word of every grateful one**; Ādam said it when
he sneezed; Allah said to Nūḥ *fa-quli l-ḥamdu li-llāhi lladhī najjānā mina l-qawmi l-ẓālimīn* (23:28);
Ibrāhīm said *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi lladhī wahaba lī ʿalā l-kibari Ismāʿīl wa-Isḥāq* (14:39); of Dāwūd and
Sulaymān *wa-qālā l-ḥamdu li-llāhi lladhī faḍḍalanā ʿalā kathīrin min ʿibādihi l-muʾminīn* (27:15); to His
Prophet *wa-quli l-ḥamdu li-llāhi lladhī lam yattakhidh waladā* (17:111); and the people of the Garden *al-ḥamdu
li-llāhi lladhī adhhaba ʿannā l-ḥazan* (35:34), *wa-ākhiru daʿwāhum ani l-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn*
(10:10)" — "so it is the word of every grateful one." And **al-Ḥasan**: "there is no blessing but that
*al-ḥamdu li-llāh* is better than it." And **Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq** on *al-ḥamdu li-llāh*: "**whoever praises Him
by His attributes as He described Himself has praised Him** — for ḥamd is *ḥāʾ*, *mīm*, *dāl*: the *ḥāʾ* from
oneness (*waḥdāniyyah*), the *mīm* from dominion (*mulk*), the *dāl* from everlastingness (*daymūmiyyah*);
whoever knows Him by oneness, everlastingness and dominion has known Him, and this is the reality of *al-ḥamdu
li-llāh*." And **Shaqīq b. Ibrāhīm**: "*al-ḥamdu li-llāh* is on three faces: that when Allah gives you a thing
you know who gave it; that you be pleased with what He gave; and that so long as His strength is in your body
you not disobey Him — these are the conditions of praise." And **al-Junayd** to a man who said *al-ḥamdu
li-llāh* before him: "Complete it as Allah said: say *Rabb al-ʿālamīn*" — the man said: "and who are the
worlds, that they should be mentioned with the Truth?" He said: "Say it, my brother — for **the created, when
it is joined with the Eternal, no trace of it remains**."

**Ibn Kathīr.** “The Statements of the Salaf about Al-Ḥamd”: Ḥafṣ mentioned that ʿUmar said to ʿAlī, “We
know *Lā ilāha illallāh*, *Subḥān Allāh* and *Allāhu Akbar*. What about *Al-Ḥamdu Lillāh*?” ʿAlī said:
“A statement that Allah liked for Himself, was pleased with for Himself and He likes that it be repeated.”
Ibn ʿAbbās said: “*Al-Ḥamdu Lillāh* is the statement of appreciation. When the servant says *Al-Ḥamdu
Lillāh*, Allah says, ‘My servant has praised Me.’” Ibn Abī Ḥātim recorded it. And on *al-ʿālamīn*, the
Successors: al-Farrāʾ and Abū ʿUbayd — “*ʿĀlam* includes all that has a mind, the Jinns, mankind, the
angels and the devils, but not the animals”; Zayd bin Aslam and Abū Muḥaysin — “all that Allah has created
with a soul”; Qatādah — “Every type of creation is an *ʿĀlam*”; az-Zajjāj — “*ʿĀlam* encompasses
everything that Allah created, in this life and in the Hereafter”; and al-Qurṭubī: “This is the correct
meaning”.

### V7 · Exegesis of the classical mufassirūn (*aqwāl al-mufassirīn*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "***Wa-l-ʿālamīn* is the plural of *ʿālam*, having no singular from its own form.** They
differed over the *ʿālamīn*: **Ibn ʿAbbās** said: **they are the jinn and mankind, because they are the
ones addressed** — Allah said *li-yakūna li-l-ʿālamīna nadhīrā* (25:1); and **Qatādah, Mujāhid and al-Ḥasan**:
**they are all created things** — Allah said *wa-qāla firʿawnu wa-mā rabbu l-ʿālamīn? qāla rabbu l-samāwāti
wa-l-arḍi wa-mā baynahumā* (26:23–24). **And its derivation is from *al-ʿilm* and *al-ʿalāmah*; they were
named by it because the trace of the making is manifest in them.** **Abū ʿUbaydah**: **they are four
communities — the angels, mankind, the jinn and the devils** — derived from *al-ʿilm*; **and the beasts are
not called an *ʿālam* because they do not understand.**" And their number: "**Saʿīd b. al-Musayyib**: Allah
has **a thousand worlds — six hundred in the sea and four hundred on land**; **Muqātil b. Ḥayyān**: Allah
has **eighty thousand worlds — forty thousand in the sea and forty thousand on land**; **Wahb**: Allah has
**eighteen thousand worlds, this world being one of them — and what are the inhabited places among the
desolate but like a tent in a desert**; **Kaʿb al-Aḥbār**: **no one can count the number of the worlds but
Allah** — Allah said *wa-mā yaʿlamu junūda rabbika illā huwa* (74:31)."

**Al-Qurṭubī.** On *al-ʿālamīn* (the eleventh *masʾalah*): "the people of explanation differed greatly over
*al-ʿālamīn*": **Qatādah**: "the worlds are the plural of *ʿālam*, and it is **every existent other than
Allah**, having no singular from its own form, like *rahṭ* and *qawm*"; and it was said: "the people of every
age are an *ʿālam* (al-Ḥusayn b. al-Faḍl, by 26:165, *ataʾtūna l-dhukrāna mina l-ʿālamīn* — that is, of
mankind)" — with al-ʿAjjāj's *fa-Khindifun hāmatu hādhā l-ʿālami* and Jarīr's *tanṣufuhu l-bariyyatu wa-huwa
sāmin / wa-yuḍḥī l-ʿālamūna lahu ʿiyālā*; **Ibn ʿAbbās**: "the *ʿālamūn* are **the jinn and mankind**, its
proof His word *li-yakūna li-l-ʿālamīna nadhīrā* (25:1) — and he was not a warner to the beasts"; **al-Farrāʾ
and Abū ʿUbaydah**: "*al-ʿālam* is an expression for those who understand, and they are **four communities:
mankind, the jinn, the angels and the devils** — the beasts are not called an *ʿālam*, because this plural is
only the plural of those who understand" (al-Aʿshā: *mā in samiʿtu bi-mithlihim fī l-ʿālamīnā*); **Zayd b.
Aslam**: "those provided for (*al-murtaziqūn*)", and the like is **Abū ʿAmr b. al-ʿAlāʾ**: "the spiritual
beings"; and it is the sense of **Ibn ʿAbbās** too: "**every living being that crawls on the face of the
earth**"; **Wahb b. Munabbih**: "Allah has **eighteen thousand worlds**, and this world is one of them";
**Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī**: "Allah has **forty thousand worlds**, and this world from its east to its west is one
world"; **Muqātil**: "the worlds are **eighty thousand worlds** — forty thousand on land and forty thousand
in the sea"; and **al-Rabīʿ b. Anas from Abū al-ʿĀliyah**: "the jinn are a world and mankind are a world, and
besides that the earth has **four corners, in each corner a thousand five hundred worlds**, created for His
worship."
Al-Qurṭubī's own *tarjīḥ*: "**the first of these sayings is the soundest, because it comprises every created
and existing thing** — its proof His word *qāla firʿawnu wa-mā rabbu l-ʿālamīn? qāla rabbu l-samāwāti
wa-l-arḍi wa-mā baynahumā* (26:23–24); then it is taken **from *al-ʿilm* and *al-ʿalāmah*, because it points
to its Maker** (as al-Zajjāj said: *al-ʿālam* is all that Allah created in this world and the next; and
al-Khalīl: *al-ʿilm*, *al-ʿalāmah* and *al-maʿlam* are what points to a thing — so the world points to its
having a Creator and a Director) — and this is clear."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** On *al-ʿālamīn*, he sets out the term and then the reports. “*Al-ʿālamīn* is the plural of
*ʿālam*; and *ʿālam* is a plural which has no singular from its own form, like *al-anām*, *al-rahṭ* and
*al-jaysh*… It is a name for the kinds of peoples: every kind of them is an *ʿālam*, and the people of every
generation of every kind is the *ʿālam* of that generation and that time. So mankind is an *ʿālam*, and the
jinn are an *ʿālam*, and likewise all the races of creation.” The reports:

* **Ibn ʿAbbās** (no. 156, weak chain): “All praise belongs to Allah, to whom belongs all creation — the
  heavens and the earths and those in them and what is between them, of what is known and what is not.”
* **ʿIkrimah from Ibn ʿAbbās** (no. 157): “the jinn and mankind”. **Saʿīd b. Jubayr** (no. 159): “the jinn
  and mankind”. **Ibn ʿAbbās** through Saʿīd b. Jubayr (no. 158): “the Lord of the jinn and mankind”.
  **Saʿīd b. Jubayr** (no. 160): “the son of Adam, and the jinn and mankind — every community of them an
  *ʿālam* on its own”. **Mujāhid** (nos. 161–162): “mankind and the jinn”. **Qatādah** (no. 163): “every
  species is an *ʿālam*”. **Ibn Jurayj** (no. 165): “the jinn and mankind”.
* **Abū al-ʿĀliyah** (no. 164): “Mankind is an *ʿālam*, and the jinn are an *ʿālam*, and besides that
  eighteen thousand *ʿālam* — or fourteen thousand, he was in doubt — of the angels on the earth; and the
  earth has four corners, in each corner three thousand five hundred *ʿālam*, created for His worship.”

Al-Ṭabarī's judgement: “this is the saying maintained; it is the saying of Ibn ʿAbbās and Saʿīd b. Jubayr,
and it is the sense of the generality of the exegetes.”

### V8 · Language, lexicon & grammar (*lughah · iʿrāb · balāghah*)

**Al-Baghawī.** *Rabb*'s two senses (owner; nurturer-and-setter-right) with the morphological parallel
*rabb* → *ṭabb*, *barr*; *ʿālamīn*'s having no singular from its own form; the rule that the creature is not
called *al-rabb* with the article "because the *alif* and *lām* are for comprehension and he does not own
all"; **the *lām* of *li-llāh* for entitlement** (*al-dāru li-Zayd*); and **the *ḥamd*/*shukr* distinction —
*al-ḥamd* more general, since praise may be for a trait (*ʿilm*, *shajāʿah*) while *shukr* is only for a
blessing, so "every praiser is a thank-er and not every thank-er is a praiser"**; and, it is said, **praise
with the tongue, thanks with the limbs** (17:111; 34:13). His grammatical point on the opening: **"its
wording is a report (*khabar*) … and in it is the teaching of creation, its sense being 'say: *al-ḥamdu
li-llāh*'"** — the *khabar* carrying an imperative sense.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The fourth** — *al-ḥamd* in the speech of the Arabs means **complete praise** (*al-thanāʾ
al-kāmil*), and the *alif* and *lām* are **for the comprehension of the genus** of praises, so He deserves
praise in its entirety, since His are the most beautiful names and the highest attributes" — and he brings
the poets (*wa-ablahu maḥmūdi l-thanāʾi khaṣaṣtuhu / bi-afḍali aqwālī wa-afḍali aḥmadī*) and: "al-ḥamd is the
contrary of blame; *ḥamidtu l-rajul* — *aḥmaduhu ḥamdan* — and he is *ḥamīd* and *maḥmūd*; *al-taḥmīd* is
more emphatic than *al-ḥamd*; *al-ḥamd* is more general than *al-shukr*; *al-maḥmadah* is the contrary of
*al-madhmamah*; *aḥmada l-rajul*: his affair came to praise; *aḥmadtuhu*: I found him praiseworthy; a man
*ḥumadah*, like *humazah*: one who praises things more than they deserve; and *ḥamadah* of the fire is the
sound of its blazing."
"The fifth — **Abū Jaʿfar al-Ṭabarī and Abū al-ʿAbbās al-Mubarrid held that *al-ḥamd* and *al-shukr* are one
meaning, and it is not acceptable** — and Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Sulamī related it in *al-Ḥaqāʾiq* from
Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq and Ibn ʿAṭāʾ (Ibn ʿAṭāʾ: the meaning is thanks to Allah, since from Him is the favour of
teaching us it until we praised Him). **Al-Ṭabarī argued for their being one meaning by the correctness of
your saying** *al-ḥamdu li-llāh shukran* … **Ibn ʿAṭiyyah said: in truth it is a proof against what he held**,
because your saying *shukran* you have attached to *al-ḥamd* only because it is for a blessing among
blessings. And some scholars said: **thanking is more general than praising**, since it is with tongue, limbs
and heart, while praise is with the tongue only; and it was said: **praise is more general**, because in it
is the meaning of thanks and the meaning of eulogy, and it may be put in place of thanks but thanks not in
place of praise." His own conclusion: "**the sound view is that *al-ḥamd* is praise of the praised for His
attributes without any prior beneficence, and *al-shukr* is praise of the thanked for what beneficence he has
brought** — and on this limit our scholars said *al-ḥamd* is more general than *al-shukr*, because praise
falls on eulogy, on extolling and on thanks, while recompense is specific, being only a requital for one who
has done you a kindness; so *al-ḥamd* in the verse is more general since it exceeds *al-shukr*."
And: "*al-ḥamd* is also mentioned with the sense of pleasure: *balaṭuhu fa-ḥamadtuhu* — I was pleased with
him; from it *maqāman maḥmūdan* (17:79); and he ﷺ said *aḥmadu ilaykum ghasla l-iḥlīl* — I approve it for
you."
"**The seventh** — the seven reciters and the majority are agreed on **raising** the *dāl* of *al-ḥamd*; and
from **Sufyān b. ʿUyaynah and Ruʾbah b. al-ʿAjjāj** it is related with the *naṣb* of the *dāl*, and that is
on the implied *faʿala*." And Sībawayh's answer to the question what the benefit is in the nominative
sentence: "when a man says *al-ḥamdu li-llāh* with the nominative, in it is the same meaning as your saying
*ḥamidtu llāha ḥamdan*, except that **the one who raises *al-ḥamd* reports that the praise is from him and
from all creation to Allah, while the one who puts it in the accusative reports that the praise is from him
alone**"; and "another said: this is spoken as an exposure to Allah's pardon and forgiveness, magnifying and
glorifying Him, so it is the opposite of the sense of a report and in it is the sense of a request — and in
the ḥadīth: *whoever is occupied with My mention from asking Me, I give him better than I give the askers*."
And the *iltifāt*-reading of the raised form: "it was said: His praising Himself and extolling Himself is to
teach that to His servants; the meaning therefore is: **say *al-ḥamdu li-llāh*** — and this is the sense of
his saying of *iyyāka*: *say*." And the transmitted *iʿrāb*-variants: "it is related from **Ibn Abī ʿAblah**
— *al-ḥamdu li-llāh* with *ḍammah* of both *dāl* and *lām*, making the second follow the first and matching
the sounds"; and from **al-Ḥasan b. Abī al-Ḥasan and Zayd b. ʿAlī** — with *kasrah* of the *dāl*, making the
first follow the second." And on *al-Rabb*: "**the eighth** — *Rabb al-ʿālamīn*, that is, **their Owner**: everyone
who owns a thing is its *rabb*, so *al-Rabb* is the owner. And *al-Rabb* is one of Allah's names; it is not
said of other than Him except by annexation — and they said it in the Jāhiliyyah of a king: al-Ḥārith b.
Ḥilizah: *wa-huwa l-rabbu wa-l-shahīdu ʿalā yaw / mi l-ḥiyārīna wa-l-balāʾu balāʾ*" — "*al-Rabb* is the
master (*al-sayyid*), as in *udhkurnī ʿinda rabbika* (12:42) and the ḥadīth *that the slave girl give birth
to her mistress*; and *al-Rabb* is **the one who sets right, the director, the mender, the maintainer** — as
al-Harawī: "of one who undertakes the setting right of a thing and its completion one says *rabba rabbahu*,
and from it the *rabbāniyyūn* are named for their undertaking of the Books"; and *al-Rabb* is **the
worshipped**, as the poet: *a-rabbun yabūlu l-thaʿlabāni bi-raʾsihi / laqad dhalla man bālat ʿalayhi
l-thaʿālibu*" — "and for frequency one says *rabbāhu*, *rabbabahu*, *rabbatahu* (al-Naḥḥās)."
And: "**the ninth** — some scholars said **this name is Allah's greatest name**, because of the frequency with
which suppliants call by it … and because of what this description gives rise to in the relation between
*rabb* and *marbūb*, with what it comprises of care, mercy and need at every state; and they differed over
its derivation: it is said from *al-tarbiyah* — so Allah is **director of His creation and their nurturer**,
as in *wa-rabāʾibukumu llātī fī ḥujūrikum* (4:23), where the wife's daughter is called *rabībah* for the
husband's nurturing of her. **On its being director of His creation and their nurturer it is an attribute of
act; and on *al-Rabb* being the owner and master it is an attribute of the Essence.**" And "**the tenth** —
when the *alif* and *lām* are put on *rabb*, Allah is singled out by it because it is for the covenant, while
without them it is shared between Allah and His servants — one says *Allah is the Rabb of the servants* and
*Zayd is the rabb of the house*; so **Allah is the Rabb of rabbs, owning the owner and the owned, and He is
the Creator of that and its Provider; and every rabb other than Him is neither creator nor provider, and every owned thing was caused to be owned after it was not, and that is taken from His hand**; and **His
attribute differs from these meanings — this is the difference between the attribute of the Creator and of
the created**." And "**the twelfth** — the nominative and accusative are both permissible in *Rabb*: the
accusative as praise, and the nominative as a fresh start — that is, *He is the Lord of the worlds*."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** Four arguments belong here:

* **“Praise” and “thanks” are interchangeable (*al-ḥamd* and *al-shukr*).** The proof: all the Arabs
  accept “*al-ḥamdu li-llāh shukran*”, which would be impossible “if praise were not of the meaning of
  thanks and thanks not of the meaning of praise”.
* **The definite article.** “There is a meaning in entering the *alif* and *lām* on *al-ḥamd* that is not
  conveyed by ‘*ḥamdan*’”: the *al-* tells “all praise and complete thanks belong to Allah”; without it the
  word would indicate only the speaker's own praise of Allah. Hence “the reading of the reciters and the
  scholars of the community has followed one another in raising *al-ḥamd* — *al-ḥamdu li-llāh* — and not in
  putting it in the accusative”. And on that: “were a reciter to read it in the accusative, the sense would,
  in my view, be absurd, and he would deserve punishment for reciting it so, if he did it deliberately,
  knowing his error and the corruption of its sense.”
* **The implied “say”.** To the objection — God praised Himself, so how is the rest His speech, and how does
  *iyyāka naʿbudu* belong to Him? — al-Ṭabarī answers: “it is all the speech of Allah; but He praised
  Himself with what He is worthy of, then taught that to His servants and made its recitation obligatory on
  them, as a test and a trial, saying to them: *Say* *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn*, and *Say* *iyyāka
  naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn*.” The omitted word “*qūlū*” (“say”) is supplied by the same principle of
  ellipsis he established at 1:1, with the poets' lines again as evidence.
* **The meaning and scope of *al-Rabb*.** “Concerning *rabb*: in the speech of the Arabs it is applied to
  meanings — *the obeyed master* (as Labīd: *wa-ahlakna yawman rabba Kindata wa-bnahu / wa-rabba Maʿaddin
  bayna khabtin wa-ʿarʿar*), *the man who sets a thing right* (as al-Farazdaq: *kānū ka-sāliʾatin
  ḥamqāʾa idh ḥaqanat / silāʾahā fī adīmin ghayri marbūb* — “in a hide not put right”), and *the owner of a
  thing* (as ʿAlqamah: *fa-kunta mraʾan afḍat ilayka ribābatī / wa-qablaka rabbatnī fa-ḍiʿtu rubūb*).” And
  with these three, “our Lord is the Master who has no like or peer in His mastery, the One who sets the
  affair of His creation right by what He has lavished on them, and the Owner to whom belong creation and
  command”. The report of **Ibn ʿAbbās** (no. 155, weak chain, through Jibrīl): “*Say*: praise be to Allah,
  to whom belongs all creation — the heavens, all of them and all that is in them, and the earths, all of
  them and all that is in them and what is between them, of what is known and what is not known. Know, O
  Muḥammad, that this your Lord is One whom nothing resembles.*”

**Ibn ʿAbbās (as rendered here).** The phrase is glossed through the lexical range of *al-ḥamd*: in the
three reports it is rendered *laudation* — praise called out of kindness shown (report 1), praise called
out of abundant blessing and guidance (report 2), and praise bound together with *absolute unity* and
*godhood* (report 3). "Lord of the worlds" is likewise glossed through the range of *al-Rabb*: owner of
every living, walking creature and of the dwellers of heaven; master of jinn and humankind; and creator,
provider and transformer of states.

**Ibn Kathīr.** “*Al* before *Ḥamd* encompasses all Types of Thanks and Appreciation for Allah”, with the
ḥadīth: “*O Allah! All of Al-Hamd is due to You, You own all the ownership, all types of good are in Your
Hand and all affairs belong to You.*” On *ʿālam*: “*ʿĀlam* is derived from *ʿAlāmah* (sign), because it is
a sign testifying to the existence of its Creator and to His Oneness.”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** The lexical matter of this verse is the sense of *Rabb* and the scope of
*al-ʿālamīn*, given above: nurture as gradual development to perfection; and *ʿālam* as a term taking in
all possible forms of existence, so that “the plural ‘worlds’ covers the millions of universes that may
lie beyond our own”.

**The Study Quran.** The definite article in *al-ḥamd* as indicating comprehensiveness (Ṭ); *rabb* as
*sayyid*, orderer, possessor (Ṭ), and as *murabbī* (Qu); the term *al-ʿālamīn* and its referents (Q, IK);
the note that God is called “*Lord of the worlds* some forty-two times” in the Quran.

### V9 · Variant readings (*qirāʾāt*)

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The *ḥamd*-readings of the seventh *masʾalah* (above, V8): the consensus *rafʿ* of the seven
reciters; the *naṣb* attributed to Sufyān b. ʿUyaynah and Ruʾbah; Ibn Abī ʿAblah's double *ḍammah*; al-Ḥasan
b. Abī al-Ḥasan's and Zayd b. ʿAlī's *kasrah* of the *dāl*; with the parallels *ajūʾuka*, *al-sāqānu*,
*muraddifīn*/*muqattilīn*, and al-Nuʿmān b. Bashīr's *wa-yli immihā*.

### V11 · Creed & theology (*ʿaqīdah*)

**Al-Qurṭubī.** (i) "The Muslims are agreed that Allah is praised for all His blessings, **and that of His
blessings is *īmān* — and this proves *īmān* is His act and His creation, not as the *Qadariyyah* say that it
is a creation belonging to them**" (the third *masʾalah*). (ii) Against the *Qadariyyah*'s claim that
"Allah chose for them *al-ḥamd* and made it their own doing"? — see under 1:5. (iii) His praise of Himself
"was not for a cause, while His praise of creation is mixed with causes" — the created's self-praise is
"foul, for it seeks by it to attract benefits and repel harms" (the sixth *masʾalah*).

**As-Saʿdī.** His nurture of creation is of two kinds:

* **General** — His creating them, providing for them, and guiding them to what serves their interests
  and preserves them in this world.
* **Special** — His nurture of His friends: He nurtures them with faith, grants them success in it,
  perfects them, and repels from them the hindrances that stand between them and Him. Its reality is
  the nurture of success in every good and protection from every evil.

This is likely the secret of why most of the prophets' supplications use the name *al-Rabb*: all their
requests fall under His special lordship. Thus "Lord of the worlds" proves His singleness in creation,
in governance and in blessing, the perfection of His self-sufficiency, and the total dependence of the
worlds on Him in every respect and consideration.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “If there is only one Being in the whole universe who inherently deserves all
praise, it necessarily follows from it that this Being alone should be Worthy of adoration and worship.
Thus… although the phrase *Al-ḥamdu lillāh* has been used to signify praise, yet, by implication, it cuts
the very root of polytheism or the worship of created beings, and at the same time brings out in a
self-evident manner the first and the basic principle of the Islamic creed — Oneness of God.”

**The Study Quran.** “Praise (*al-ḥamd*) is rendered in the definite rather than the indefinite to indicate
that all forms of praise and all gratitude belong to God”; and the verse conveys “God being the Lord of all
that can be seen or imagined and of all that cannot be seen or imagined by human beings… God is Lord of all
‘space’, not only physical space, and therefore of all that exists, no matter what the nature of that
existence may be.”

### V17 · Specific virtues & supplications (*faḍāʾil · duʿāʾ*)

**Ibn Kathīr.** *Al-Ḥamdu lillāh* is stated to be “the best supplication”, and the servant's praise is
answered by Allah's “My servant has praised Me” — the ḥamd functioning as the servant's address to his Lord.

**The Study Quran.** *Praise be to God* as “a frequently repeated formula recited by Muslims on many
occasions throughout their daily lives”, and the report that “when you say, ‘Praise be to God, Lord of the
worlds,’ you will have thanked God and He will increase your bounty”.


## 1:3 — الرَّحْمَٰنِ الرَّحِيمِ

*[context]* The Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

### V2 · Running exposition (*bayān*)

**Al-Baghawī.** The verse is treated together with 1:2 in the source (the comment runs *Rabb al-ʿālamīn,
al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm* as one passage of names): "the owner of the worlds and their nurturer"; and for the two
names his exposition of generality/particularity and of mercy as **"Allah's willing of good for its people"
/ "the leaving of punishment for one who deserves it and the doing of good to one who does not"** — the
first making mercy **an attribute of the Essence**, the second **an attribute of act** — which is filed at
1:1, V11 above, since the source itself comments on the two names once, in the basmalah.

**Al-Jalālayn.** “The Compassionate, the Merciful — that is to say, the One who possesses ‘mercy’ which
means *to want what is good for those who deserve it*.”

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The thirteenth** — He described Himself, after *Rabb al-ʿālamīn*, as *al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm*,
because when His being described as *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* contains intimidation, He joined it to *al-Raḥmān
al-Raḥīm* for what it contains of encouragement, so as to join in His attributes fear of Him and hope in Him,
and so be a greater aid to His obedience and a stronger restraint" — with 15:49–50, *nabbiʾ ʿibādī annī anā
l-ghafūru l-raḥīm, wa-anna ʿadhābī huwa l-ʿadhābu l-alīm*, and 40:3, *ghāfiri l-dhanbi wa-qābili l-tawbi
shadīdi l-ʿiqāb*, and the Muslim ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah: "*Were the believer to know what is with Allah of
punishment, no one would hope for His Garden; and were the unbeliever to know what is with Allah of mercy, no
one would despair of His Garden.*" And: "what is in these two names of meanings has already passed" — with
the cross-reference to the basmalah's twenty-second to twenty-sixth *masāʾil* (given under 1:1, V11).

**Al-Ṭabarī.** Two matters: the two names are expounded once, in the basmalah, and referred to here — “the
explanation of *al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm* has already passed, in the explanation of *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni
l-Raḥīm*, and that suffices from repeating it in this place.” And the second, which is a *naẓm* argument
that carries his ruling: “we had no need to explain the reason for its repetition here, **because we do not
hold that *bismi llāhi l-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* is a verse of al-Fātiḥah** — for then a questioner could ask:
what is the reason for repeating it here, when Allah had already described Himself by it in the basmalah,
so near in place? Rather, that is for us a proof against the claim of the one who claims the basmalah is a
verse of Fātiḥat al-Kitāb: for were it so, it would be a repetition of one verse, with one wording and one
meaning, twice with no separator between them — **and there is not found in any part of the Book of Allah
two adjacent verses repeated with one wording and one meaning with no separating words.**” Then his report
of a further position: “a group of the people of explanation denied that, and said the phrase is of the
**postponed which has the sense of the placed-first** (*al-muʾakhkhar alladhī maʿnāhu l-taqdīm*), and that
the order is: *Praise be to Allah, the Compassionate, the Merciful, Lord of the worlds, Master of the Day
of Judgement*” — their argument being that *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* goes with *Malik yawm al-dīn* (the two
statements of ownership), while the two names of mercy go with the praise; and they supported it with the
Qurʾānic parallel of 18:1, *al-ḥamdu li-llāhī lladhī anzala ʿalā ʿabdihi l-kitāba wa-lam yajʿal lahu
ʿiwajan qayyiman*, where *qayyiman* belongs with *anzala* although placed at the end.

_(This is the record's clearest case of an element carried by another: al-Ṭabarī's explanation of_
al-Raḥmān _and_ al-Raḥīm _is his basmalah exposition, and is therefore filed with 1:1 below.)_

**Ibn ʿAbbās (as rendered here).** "(The Beneficent) the Gentle. (The Merciful) the Gentle." — the two
divine names are both expounded as gentleness shown to creation.

**Ibn Kathīr.** “We explained these Names in the Basmalah. Al-Qurṭubī said: ‘Allah has described Himself by
*Ar-Raḥmān, Ar-Raḥīm* after saying *the Lord of the ʿālamīn*, so His statement here includes a warning,
and then an encouragement.’” Ibn Kathīr draws the rule: “Hence, *Rabb* contains a warning while *Ar-Raḥmān
Ar-Raḥīm* encourages.”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “The second verse speaks of the Divine quality of mercy, employing two adjectives,
*Raḥmān* and *Raḥīm*, which are hyperbolic terms in Arabic, and respectively connote the superabundance
and perfection of Divine mercy. The reference to this particular attribute in this situation is perhaps
intended to be a reminder of the fact that it is not through any external compulsion or inner need or any
kind of necessity whatsoever that Allah has assumed the responsibility of nurturing the whole of His
creation, but in response to the demand of His own quality of mercy. If this whole universe did not exist,
He would suffer no loss; if it does exist, it is no burden to Him.”

**The Study Quran.** “Both Names are intensifications of the word *raḥmah*, meaning ‘Mercy’ or
‘Loving-Mercy’. *Al-Raḥmān*, which is also the title of Sūrah 55, is considered to be more emphatic,
embracing, and encompassing than *al-Raḥīm* (IK, Qu, Ṭ). It is one of the Divine Names that cannot be
applied to anything other than God, either literally or figuratively, since it connotes the Loving-Mercy by
which God brings forth existence. *Al-Raḥīm* indicates the blessing of nourishment (*rizq*) by which God
sustains each particular existent thing. Thus it may apply figuratively to creatures, and the adjective
*raḥīm* is in fact used to describe the Prophet in 9:128… The relationship between them is thus presented
as that of different levels or degrees of light: *al-Raḥmān* is like the light of the sun that illuminates
the whole sky, and *al-Raḥīm* is like the particular ray of sunlight that touches a creature.” The
metaphysical reading: “it was by God breathing ‘the Breath of the Compassionate’ (*Nafas al-Raḥmān*) upon
the immutable essences (*al-aʿyān al-thābitah*)… that the world was brought into being. From this
perspective, the very existence of the world is in essence nothing but the breath of Divine Compassion.”
And: “Together these two Names refer to two aspects of the Divine Mercy (*raḥmah*): one essential and
universal, the other attributive and particular… God is thus said to be Compassionate toward all of
creation and Merciful toward the believers (Ṭb).” On the Names of rigor: “in Divine Names of rigor, such as
‘the Powerful’ (*al-Qādir*), ‘the Avenger’ (*al-Muntaqim*), and ‘the Abaser’ (*al-Mudhill*), the
manifestation of Divine Mercy is veiled by the inseparability of God's Kindness from His Majesty and
determinative power (*qadar*).”

### V4 · Qurʾān cross-references (*tafsīr al-Qurʾān bi-l-Qurʾān*)

**Ibn Kathīr.** 15:49–50: *Declare unto My servants, that truly, I am the Oft-Forgiving, the Most Merciful.
And that My torment is indeed the most painful torment*; and 6:165: *Surely, your Lord is swift in
retribution, and certainly He is Oft-Forgiving, Most Merciful.*

**The Study Quran.** 9:128 for *raḥīm* used of the Prophet; 17:110 — *Call upon God, or call upon the
Compassionate. Whichever you call upon, to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names*; 33:43 — *And He is
Merciful unto the believers*; 20:5 and 25:59 — *The Compassionate mounted the Throne*; and 7:156 — *My
Mercy encompasses all things*.

### V5 · Prophetic traditions (ḥadīth)

**Ibn Kathīr.** Muslim recorded in his *Ṣaḥīḥ* that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: “*If the believer knew
what punishment Allah has, none would have hope in acquiring His Paradise, and if the disbeliever knew
what mercy Allah has, none will lose hope of earning His mercy.*”

### V6 · Companions & Successors (āthār)

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The transmitted explanations of the pair:

* **Al-ʿArzamī** (no. 146; the corpus editor notes him as very weak, and that Aḥmad said “his ḥadīth is
  worth nothing”): “*al-Raḥmān* to all creation, *al-Raḥīm* to the believers.”
* **The Prophet ﷺ**, through the chain of Ismāʿīl b. Yaḥyā (no. 147; editor: weak, indeed two weak chains):
  “ʿĪsā ibn Maryam said: *al-Raḥmān* is the Raḥmān of the next world and this world, and *al-Raḥīm* is the
  Raḥīm of the next world.”
* **Ibn ʿAbbās** (no. 148, weak chain): “*al-Raḥmān*, *al-Raḥīm*: the tender, the gentle to the one he loves
  to show mercy to, and the far, the severe to the one he loves to treat harshly — and so all His names.”
* **ʿAṭāʾ al-Khurāsānī** (no. 149, chain sound to ʿAṭāʾ): “He was *al-Raḥmān*; and when *al-Raḥmān* was
  cut off from His name, He was *al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm*.” Al-Ṭabarī explains: “what ʿAṭāʾ meant is that
  *al-Raḥmān* was one of Allah's names by which no one of His creation named himself — and when the liar
  Musaylimah named himself by it (that is his cutting it off from His names for himself), Allah informed His
  servants that His name is *al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm*, so as to distinguish His name from the name of him who had
  named himself with His names… for no one is named *Raḥmān Raḥīm*, joining the two names, but He.”
* **Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī** (no. 150, sound chain): “*al-Raḥmān* is a **forbidden name** (*ismun mamnūʿ*)” —
  that is, forbidden to creatures.

### V8 · Language, lexicon & grammar (*lughah · iʿrāb · balāghah*)

**Al-Jalālayn.** The lexical definition of the root: *mercy* is defined as willing the good for those who
deserve it (*irādat al-khayr li-man yastaḥiqq*), which is the sense the two names carry.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** For the derivation and senses of *al-Raḥmān* / *al-Raḥīm* (the intensification pattern,
the "peculiar to Allah" ruling, one-meaning-or-two) the record is at 1:1, V11 above; the author defers to it
here: "the explanation of what is in these two names of meanings has already passed, so there is no meaning
in repeating it."

**Al-Ṭabarī.**

* **The patterns.** “*Al-Raḥmān* is *faʿlān* from *raḥima*, and *al-Raḥīm* is *faʿīl* from it. The Arabs very
  often build names from *faʿila yafʿalu* on *faʿlān* — as from *ghaḍiba* → *ghaḍbān*, from *sakira* →
  *sakrān*, from *ʿaṭisha* → *ʿaṭshān*. So *Raḥmān* from *raḥima*… And *Raḥīm* was said, although the *ʿayn*
  of *faʿila* is vowelled with *kasrah*, because it is praise; for the Arabs carry the patterns of names, when
  there is praise or blame in them, to *faʿīl*, as from *ʿalima* → *ʿālim* and *ʿalīm*, from *qadara* →
  *qādir* and *qadīr*.” Had the two names departed from the pattern of their verbs, “their form would have
  been *al-Rāḥim*”.
* **Which is the stronger.** “There is no disagreement among those who know the languages of the Arabs that
  the speaker's word *al-Raḥmān* — among the patterns of names from *faʿila yafʿalu* — is further removed
  from its origin (*ashaddu ʿudūlan*) than his word *al-Raḥīm*; and that every name which has an origin in
  *faʿila yafʿalu* and is then further removed from it denotes a stronger praise in its bearer than one built
  on its origin, when the naming is praise or blame. This, then, is what *al-Raḥmān* has over *al-Raḥīm* in
  the language.”
* **What each name carries.** After the reports (below), his own conclusion: “the meaning in naming Allah
  *al-Raḥmān*, beyond that in naming Him *al-Raḥīm*, is that by *al-Raḥmān* He is described with the
  **generality** of mercy to all His creation, and by *al-Raḥīm* with the **particularity** of mercy to some
  of His creation… Our Lord is therefore *Raḥmān* to all His creation in this world and the next, and *Raḥīm*
  to the believers in particular in this world and the next.” The general mercy of this world he lists as
  “the spreading of provision, the subjection of the clouds with rain, the bringing forth of plants from the
  earth, the soundness of bodies and minds, and the rest of the blessings that cannot be counted, in which
  believers and unbelievers share” — with 14:34 and 16:18, *wa-in taʿuddū niʿmata llāhi lā tuḥṣūhā*. The
  general mercy of the next world he states as “His levelling between them all in His justice and His
  judgement, so that He wrongs none of them by the weight of an atom” — with *wa-tuwaffā kullu nafsin mā
  kasabat*. The particular mercy is 33:43, *wa-kāna bi-l-muʾminīna raḥīman*.
* **The order of the three names.** “It is the habit of the Arabs, when they intend to report of one of whom
  they speak, to put his name first and then follow it with his attributes and descriptions… And since Allah
  has names He has forbidden His creation to take — namely *Allāh*, *al-Raḥmān*, *al-Khāliq* — and names He
  has permitted them to use of one another, such as *al-Raḥīm*, *al-Samīʿ*, *al-Baṣīr*, *al-Karīm* — the
  due order was that His names peculiar to Him be placed first, so that the hearer knows to whom the praise
  is directed; then the names by which others may be named follow.” The proof: 17:110, *quli dʿū llāha awi
  dʿū l-Raḥmāna ayyamā tadʿū fa-lahu l-asmāʾu l-ḥusnā*, and 25:60, the idolaters' *wa-mā l-Raḥmānu
  a-nasjudu limā taʾmurunā*.

**Ibn ʿAbbās (as rendered here).** The rendering glosses both *al-Raḥmān* and *al-Raḥīm* by the same
English word, *the Gentle* — the distinction carried by the Arabic pair is not brought out in this
rendering. *(Quoted as given: "(The Beneficent) the Gentle. (The Merciful) the Gentle.")*

**Ibn Kathīr.** The two names are stated to have been fully treated “in the Basmalah” — the distinction of
scope between *Ar-Raḥmān* and *Ar-Raḥīm* (1:1, V8 above) is the lexical matter of this verse.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** The two names are “hyperbolic terms in Arabic” (*ṣīghat al-mubālaghah*) — “the
superabundance and perfection of Divine mercy” — with the lexical distinction of *Raḥmān* and *Raḥīm*
already set out at 1:1, V8.

**The Study Quran.** The morphological point: “Both Names are intensifications of the word *raḥmah*” —
*al-Raḥmān* “more emphatic, embracing, and encompassing than *al-Raḥīm*”; the one indicating “the
Loving-Mercy by which God brings forth existence”, the other “the blessing of nourishment (*rizq*) by which
God sustains each particular existent thing.”

### V11 · Creed & theology (*ʿaqīdah*)

**Al-Baghawī.** See 1:1, V11: the two names' distinction, the ruling that **other than Allah may be called
*raḥīm* but may not be called *raḥmān***, and the two descriptions of mercy (will of good; and pardon plus
beneficence) with their consequence for the attribute being **of the Essence** or **of act**.

**Al-Ṭabarī.** Two polemics are joined here, both of them creedal:

* **Against those who claim the Arabs did not know *al-Raḥmān*.** “Certain people of dullness claimed that
  the Arabs did not know *al-Raḥmān* and that it was not in their language, and for that the idolaters said
  to the Prophet ﷺ, *wa-mā l-Raḥmānu a-nasjudu limā taʾmurunā* (25:60), rejecting the name.” His answer
  turns on 2:146, *alladhīna ātaynāhumu l-kitāba yaʿrifūnahu kamā yaʿrifūna abnāʾahum*, and on pre-Islamic
  poetry that uses the name: *“a-lā ḍarabat tilka l-fatātu hajīnahā / a-lā qaḍaba l-Raḥmānu rabbī
  yamīnahā”*, and Salāmah b. Jandal: *“ʿajiltum ʿalaynā ʿajlataynā ʿalaykumū / wa-mā yashaʾi l-Raḥmānu
  yaʿqid wa-yuṭliq”*. _(The corpus editor adds a long note contesting the attribution of the first line.)_
* **Against the claim that *al-Raḥmān* is “of the same meaning” as *al-Raḥīm* in a loose way** — “*dhu
  l-raḥmah*” and “*al-rāḥim*” — as one says *nadmān* and *nadīm* (with Burj b. Mushir's line *wa-nadmānin
  yazīdu l-kaʾsa ṭīban*). Al-Ṭabarī: “there is no doubt that *dhū l-raḥmah* is the one for whom mercy is
  established as an attribute, while *al-rāḥim* is the one described as about to be merciful, or as having
  shown it and finished, or as in the act of it” — the two are not interchangeable, and the analogy of
  *nadmān/nadīm* does not carry.

**The Study Quran.** *Al-Raḥmān* as a name that “cannot be applied to anything other than God, either
literally or figuratively”; “As *al-Raḥmān* is more encompassing, it is closer to the highest Name of God,
*Allāh*”; and the two aspects of Mercy: “one essential and universal, the other attributive and
particular. The first is that by which creation is brought forth, while the second is by which God shows
Mercy to those whom He will.”

### V13 · History, sīrah & parables (*qiṣaṣ · sīrah*)

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The historical anchor of the third report above: “when the liar Musaylimah named himself the
*Raḥmān* of Yamāmah” — the incident by which ʿAṭāʾ's report is explained, and which al-Ṭabarī also uses in
1:1's creedal discussion.

### V17 · Specific virtues & supplications (*faḍāʾil · duʿāʾ*)

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The names are opened to the servant's use as invocation: “*al-Raḥmān*” is a name no creature
may take, so that “no one is named *Raḥmān Raḥīm* — the two together — but He”; and ʿAṭāʾ's report is read
as the reason the two are joined in the basmalah, “so that His servants may know by the two together that He
is the One intended by them, apart from any other”.


## 1:4 — مَالِكِ يَوْمِ الدِّينِ

*[context]* Master of the Day of Judgement.

### V2 · Running exposition (*bayān*)

**Al-Jalālayn.** “Master of the Day of Judgement — that is, the day of requite, the Day of Resurrection.
The reason for the specific mention of the Day of Judgement is that the mastery of none shall appear on
that Day except that of God, may He be exalted, as is indicated by God's words *Whose is the Kingdom
today?* — *God's*,” and the commentary notes the reason for singling out the day: on it His mastery —
and no other — appears.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The fifteenth** — the scholars differed which is more emphatic, *malik* or *mālik*; **the
two readings are related from the Prophet ﷺ, Abū Bakr and ʿUmar** (Tirmidhī mentioned them). It was said:
***malik* is more general and more emphatic than *mālik*, since every king is an owner and not every owner is
a king**, and because the king's command runs over the owner in his property, so that he does not dispose of
it except by the king's direction (Abū ʿUbaydah, al-Mubarrid). And it was said: ***mālik* is more emphatic,
because he is owner of people and other than them, and the owner is more emphatic in disposal and greater**,
since to him belongs the execution of the ordinances of the law, and then with him is the increase of
ownership" (al-Fārisī's report of Ibn al-Sarrāj's citation). **Al-Fārisī's answer to the claim that *mālik*
is mere repetition of *Rabb al-ʿālamīn***: "there is no proof in this, for in the revelation there are things
of this form — the general first, then the specific: *huwa llāhu l-khāliqu l-bāriʾu l-muṣawwiru* (59:24),
where *al-Khāliq* is general and *al-Muṣawwir* is mentioned for what it contains of pointing to the making
and the existence of wisdom; and *wa-bi-l-ākhirati hum yūqinūn* after *alladhīna yuʾminūna bi-l-ghayb*
(2:3–4), where *al-ghayb* comprises the Hereafter and other than it, but it was mentioned for its greatness
… and *al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm*, where *al-Raḥmān* is general and *al-Raḥīm* is mentioned after it to single out
the believers, by *wa-kāna bi-l-muʾminīna raḥīmā*." **Abū Ḥātim**: "**mālik is more emphatic in praise of the
Creator than *malik*, and *malik* is more emphatic in praise of the created than *mālik***; the difference
between them is that the owner among creatures may be other than a king, while **when Allah is an owner He is
a king**" — and **al-Qāḍī Abū Bakr b. al-ʿArabī** chose this, giving three faces: (i) you annex it to the
specific and the general — *mālik al-dār wa-l-arḍ wa-l-thawb*, as you say *mālik al-mulūk*; (ii)
it applies to the owner of the little and the much; (iii) you say *mālik al-mulk* and not *malik al-mulk*.
**Ibn al-Ḥaṣṣār**: "'*mālik*' indicates ownership (*milk*) with the *kasrah*, which does not comprise
sovereignty (*mulk*) with the *ḍammah*, while *milk* comprises both — so it is worthier of emphasis" — and he
gives the further inclusions: **perfection, and therefore the sovereignty over what is below him**; 2:247,
*inna llāha iṣṭafāhu ʿalaykum wa-zādahu basṭatan fī l-ʿilmi wa-l-jism*; the ḥadīth *al-imāmah fī Quraysh*;
**capability and choice** — "necessary in a king: if he is not capable, choosing, executing his judgement and
command, his enemy overcomes him, another defeats him and his subjects despise him"; and **the seizing,
command, prohibition, promise and threat** — as Solomon's *mā liya lā arā l-hudhuda am kāna mina
l-ghāʾibīn… la-uʿadhdhibannahu ʿadhāban shadīdā* (27:20–21) — "other than that of wonderful matters and noble
meanings that are not found in *mālik*." And al-Qurṭubī adds: "some argued that *mālik* is more emphatic
**because in it is an extra letter, so its reciter has ten good deeds more than the one who recites *malik***
— **I say: this is a view of the form and not of the meaning; and the reading with *malik* is established,
and in it is meaning not in *mālik***, as we have shown."

And "**the eighteenth** — if one says: how did He say *māliki yawmi l-dīn* when the Day of Judgement does not
yet exist? — it is said: know that *mālik* is the active participle of *malaka yamliku*, and the active
participle in the speech of the Arabs may be annexed to what follows it while it has the sense of the future
act, and that is sound, rational speech with them — as you say *this is the striker of Zayd tomorrow*, i.e.
*he will strike Zayd*, and *this is the pilgrim of the House of Allah next year*, its sense *he will
perform the pilgrimage next year*. So *māliki yawmi l-dīn* is on the sense of the future: **He will own the
Day of Judgement, or in the Day of Judgement, when it comes.** And a second face: the sense of *mālik*
returns to power — **He is powerful on the Day of Judgement, or over the Day of Judgement and its bringing
into being.**" And on *yawm al-dīn*: "**the nineteenth** — Allah's word *yawm al-dīn*: *al-dīn* here is the
reckoning and the requital of deeds" — the poets *idhā mā ramawnā ramaynāhum / wa-dinnāhumū mithla mā
yuqriḍūnā* and *wa-ʿlam yaqīnan anna mulkaka zāʾilun / wa-ʿlam bi-annaka mā tadīnu tudānu*; and "**the
twenty-first** — the people of language related: *dintuhu bi-fiʿlihi daynan* (with *fatḥ*) and *dīnan* (with
*kasrah*): I requited him — and from it *al-Dayyān* in the description of the Lord, that is, the Requiter;
and in the ḥadīth: *the shrewd one is he who takes account of himself (dāna nafsahu)*, that is, he
reckons" — with the further senses: **the decree (al-qaḍāʾ)** (also related from Ibn ʿAbbās), with Ṭarafah's
*la-ʿamruka mā kānat ḥamūlatu Maʿbadin / ʿalā juddihā ḥarban li-dīnika min Muḍar*; and **obedience**, with
ʿAmr b. Kulthūm's *wa-ayyāmin lanā ghurrin ṭiwālin / ʿaṣaynā l-malka fīhā an nadīnā* — "so on this it is a
homonym". And "**the twenty-second** — **Thaʿlab said: *dāna* the man when he obeyed, and *dāna* when he
disobeyed, and *dāna* when he was mighty, and *dāna* when he was humbled, and *dāna* when he overpowered —
so it is among the** ***aḍdād*** (*the words with opposite senses*)" — and *al-dīn* is said of custom
(*al-ʿādah*) and of one's affair, and of a king's way of life (*sīrat al-malik*: Zuhayr's *lāʾin ḥalalta bi-jawwin
fī Banī Asadin / fī dīni ʿAmrin wa-ḥālat baynanā Fadak* — "he meant, in the place of ʿAmr's obedience"), and
*al-dīn* is "the disease", from al-Liḥyānī.

**Al-Ṭabarī.** “The more fitting of the two interpretations to the verse, and the sounder of the two
readings in recitation in my view, is the first — the reading of the one who reads *malik*, in the sense of
*al-mulk*. For in the acknowledgement of His exclusivity in sovereignty there is necessarily His
exclusivity in ownership, and the excellence of the addition of ownership over the owner, since it is known
that there is no king but an owner, while there may be an owner who is not a king.” And the *naẓm* argument
for it: “Allah had informed His servants in the verse before — *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn,
al-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm* — that He is the owner and master and reformer and caretaker of all the worlds; so the
worthiest attribute to follow that is what is not contained in *Rabb al-ʿālamīn al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm*: namely
His being described as the **King**.” He then refutes at length the view that *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* speaks only
of this world — “had that been so, all the worlds of the age of the Prophet ﷺ would be included and no
other; and 45:16, *wa-faḍḍalnāhum ʿalā l-ʿālamīn*, with 3:110, *kuntum khayra ummatin ukhrijat li-l-nās*,
shows that the *ʿālam* of every age is other than the *ʿālam* of the age before and after it.”

**As-Saʿdī.** *Al-Mālik* is the one characterised by the attribute of kingship, whose effects are that
He commands and forbids, rewards and punishes, and disposes of His possessions in every manner of
disposal. He attached kingship to the Day of Judgement — the day on which people are recompensed for
their deeds, the good and the evil.

**Ibn ʿAbbās (as rendered here).** "Owner of the Day of Judgement" is "the Arbitrator on the Day of
Judgement which is the Day of Reckoning and settlement of disputes between created beings, i.e. the Day
when people are judged according to their deeds; there is no judge save Him."

_Cross-heads: V11 — the closing clause, "there is no judge save Him", is the exclusive divine prerogative
of judgement on that day._

**Ibn Kathīr.** On the singling out of the Day: “Allah mentioned His sovereignty of the Day of
Resurrection, but this does not negate His sovereignty over all other things… Allah only mentioned the Day
of Recompense here because on that Day, no one except Him will be able to claim ownership of anything
whatsoever. On that Day, no one will be allowed to speak without His permission.” Ad-Daḥḥāk said that Ibn
ʿAbbās commented: “Allah says, ‘On that Day, no one owns anything that they used to own in the world.’”

*The meaning of Yawm Ad-Dīn:* Ibn ʿAbbās said: “*Yawm Ad-Dīn* is the Day of Recompense for the creatures,
meaning the Day of Judgment. On that Day, Allah will reckon the creation for their deeds, evil for evil,
good for good, except for those whom He pardons.” And *al-Dīn*: “*Ad-Dīn* means the reckoning, the reward
or punishment.”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “The third verse pays homage to Allah as ‘the Master of the Day of Judgment or
Requital’. The word *Mālik* has been derived from the root *milk*, which signifies possessing a thing in
such a manner that one has the right and power to dispose of it as one likes. The word *Dīn* signifies
‘Requital’. So the phrase ‘Master of the Day of Requital’ implies total mastery on the Day of Requital…
According to the commentary *al-Kashshāf*, the phrase makes a general reference to cover everything. That
is to say, on the Day of Requital the mastery over everything that exists will belong to Allah alone.”
The two questions are put and answered: “Firstly, what is this Day of Requital? Secondly, Allah being the
Master of everything even today as much as on the Day of Requital, why does this verse specifically
mention the Day of Requital? The Day of Requital or the Day of Judgment is the Day appointed by Allah to
