recompense good or evil deeds.” And the reason for the specification: “The real and complete mastery over
everything, no doubt, belongs to Allah alone even in this world. Yet Allah Himself, in His beneficence and
wisdom, has granted a kind of imperfect, temporary and apparent mastery to man as well… But today, in
possessing lands or money or power, which has been given to him by way of trial, man has always been prone
to get drunk with pride and vanity.”

**The Study Quran.** “*Master* renders *mālik*, which can also mean ‘owner.’ It is also sometimes read
*malik*, meaning ‘king’ or ‘sovereign,’ and both variants can be traced to the Prophet (Q). *Malik* (‘king’)
is understood to be more intensive than *mālik* (‘master’), since every king is a master, but not every
master is a king (Q, Ṭ, Z), though others maintain that *malik* is more comprehensive, since it indicates
God's Power of disposal over all things (Q).” And: “That God is Master of the Day of Judgment indicates
that He alone has sovereignty over the Day of Reckoning and that all those who seek to contend with His
Absolute Sovereignty will be brought low on that Day, as in 40:16… *Whose is the sovereignty this Day? It
is God's, the One, the Paramount* (IK, Ṭ).” On *dīn*: “*Judgment* renders *dīn*, which is etymologically
related to ‘obedience’ and to ‘custom,’ ‘way,’ or ‘affair.’ It is, however, most often rendered ‘religion.’
*Dīn* is also closely related to the word ‘debt’ (*dayn*), found in 2:282… While *dayn* refers to a debt,
*dīn* refers to the repayment of our debt to God, and God is also known as *al-Dayyān*, meaning ‘The One Who
Repays’ (Q). The Day of *dīn* can thus be understood as the day of the requital and reckoning for actions
performed in this world (IK, Q, Ṭ).” And on the accounting: “This day is also referred to as the Day of
Reckoning, since all of one's good and bad deeds will be weighed and one will be taken to account for all
that transpired during the life of this world, as in 7:8–9.”

### V4 · Qurʾān cross-references (*tafsīr al-Qurʾān bi-l-Qurʾān*)

**Al-Jalālayn.** The verse is read by the cross-reference Q 40:16: “as is indicated by God's words ‘Whose
is the Kingdom today?’ ‘God's’”.

**Ibn Kathīr.** 78:38: *The Day that Ar-Rūḥ and the angels will stand forth in rows, they will not speak
except him whom the Most Gracious allows*; 20:108: *And all voices will be humbled for the Most Gracious*;
11:105: *On the Day when it comes, no person shall speak except by His leave*; 59:23: *He is Allah, beside
Whom none has the right to be worshipped but He, the King, the Holy*; 40:16: *Whose is the kingdom this
Day? Allah's, the One, the Irresistible*; 24:25: *On that Day Allah will pay them the recompense of their
deeds in full*; 37:53: *Shall we indeed be raised up to receive reward or punishment according to our
deeds?* — with 2:247, 18:79 and 5:20 for “king” used of others in this life “as a figure of speech”.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “The verses of the Surah al-Muʾmin/Ghāfir (Chapter 40) serve as a commentary on the
phrase under discussion, and provide a clear account of the Day of Requital”: 40:17 — *The day they will
present themselves before Allah, and nothing of theirs will remain hidden from Allah… “Whose is the
kingdom today?” Of Allah alone, the One, the Mighty. Today everyone will be recompensed for what he has
done. Today no one will be wronged. Allah's reckoning is surely swift.*

**The Study Quran.** 40:16 and 40:17; 2:282 for *dayn*; 24:25 — *On that Day God will pay them their just
due in full*; cf. 45:28; 7:8–9 — *And the weighing on that Day is true…*; also 21:47c and 101:6–9c; and on
the return to God, “see, e.g., 2:156, 245, 281; 3:55, 83; 5:48, 105; 6:36, 61–62, 108, 164; 7:29; 21:93”.

### V5 · Prophetic traditions (ḥadīth)

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The sixteenth** — it is **not permitted for anyone to be named by this name nor called by
it except Allah, Mighty and Exalted**": Bukhārī and Muslim from Abū Hurayrah — the Prophet ﷺ: "*Allah will
take up the earth on the Day of Resurrection, and fold the heaven in His right hand, and then say: I am the
King — where are the kings of the earth?*"; and from him: "*The most lowly name with Allah is a man who
called himself **Malik al-Amlāk***" — Muslim adds "*no king but Allah, Mighty and Exalted*"; Sufyān said:
"like *Shāhān Shāh*"; and Aḥmad b. Ḥanbal asked Abū ʿAmr al-Shaybānī about *akhnaʿ* and he said: "the
lowest"; and from him: "*The most angering man to Allah on the Day of Resurrection and the most vicious is a
man who was called Malik al-Amlāk — no king but Allah, Glorified.*" **Ibn al-Ḥaṣṣār**: "likewise *māliki
yawmi l-dīn* and *mālika l-mulk* — it should not be disagreed that this is forbidden to all creatures as
Malik al-Amlāk is forbidden; as for being described by *mālik* and *malik* — see the next."

**Ibn Kathīr.** “The Two Sahihs recorded Abū Hurayrah saying that the Prophet ﷺ said: *The most despicable
name to Allah is a person who calls himself the king of kings, while there are no owners except Allah.*”
And: “The Two Sahihs recorded that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: *(On the Day of Judgement) Allah will
grasp the earth and fold up the heavens with His Right Hand and proclaim, ‘I Am the King! Where are the
kings of the earth? Where are the tyrants? Where are the arrogant?’*”

**The Study Quran.** “In this regard, the Prophet is reported to have said, *‘On the Day of Judgment, God
will grasp the earth, fold up the sky with His Right Hand [see 39:67], and proclaim, “I am the King, where
are the kings of the earth? Where are the tyrants?”’* (IK).” And: “when asked, ‘Who is the most
intelligent of believers?’ the Prophet replied, *‘The most frequent in recalling death, and the best
prepared for what follows it, they are the most intelligent.’*”

### V6 · Companions & Successors (āthār)

**Ibn Kathīr.** As above, Ibn ʿAbbās on 1:4 (“On that Day, no one owns anything…”), and his definition of
*Yawm Ad-Dīn*; “several other Companions, Tābiʿīn and scholars of the Salaf said similarly, for this
meaning is apparent and clear from the Ayah”.

**The Study Quran.** “Following upon the Prophet's advice, the second Caliph, ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb… is
reported to have said, ‘Bring yourself to account before you are brought to account. And weigh your deeds
before your deeds are weighed’ (IK).”

### V7 · Exegesis of the classical mufassirūn (*aqwāl al-mufassirīn*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "Some said: **the two readings have one meaning**, like *farihīn* and *fārihīn*, *ḥadhirīn*
and *ḥādhirīn* — and their meaning is **the Lord**: one says *rabb al-dār* and *mālikuhā*. And it is said:
**the *mālik* and the *malik* is the One able to bring the entities forth from non-existence into
existence, and none other than Allah is able to do that**."
"**Abū ʿUbaydah**: *mālik* is more comprehensive and wider, because one says *mālik al-ʿabd wa-l-ṭayr
wa-l-dawābb* and one does not say *malik* of these things; and because he is not an owner of a thing unless
he owns it, while one may be *malik* of a thing and not own it. **And a group said: *malik* is worthier,
because every owner of a kingdom is a *malik* and not every owner is a *malik*, and because it agrees with
the rest of the Qurʾān** — His word *fa-taʿālā llāhu l-maliku l-ḥaqq* (20:114), *al-maliku l-quddūs* (59:23)."
And on *yawm al-dīn*: "**Mujāhid**: *al-dīn* is the reckoning — Allah said *dhālika l-dīnu l-qayyim*
(9:36), that is, the upright reckoning; and *maliki l-nās* (114:2). **Ibn ʿAbbās, Muqātil and al-Suddī**:
*maliki yawmi l-dīn* — **the judge of the Day of Reckoning**; **Qatādah**: *al-dīn* is the requital, and it
falls on requital in good and evil together; one says: *as you requite, so are you requited*
(*kamā tadīnu tudānu*). **Muḥammad b. Kaʿb al-Quraẓī**: **King of a day in which nothing benefits but
religion**; **Yamān b. Rabāb**: *al-dīn* is **overpowering** — one says *dintuhu fa-dāna*, that is, I
overpowered him and he submitted; and it is said: *al-dīn* is **obedience**, that is, the day of obedience."
And the *munāsabah* of the specification: "**He specified the Day of Judgement for mention though He is
owner of all the days, because on that day the ownerships vanish and there is no sovereignty and no command
but His** — Allah said *al-mulku yawmaʾidhin al-ḥaqqu li-l-Raḥmān* (25:26), *li-mani l-mulku l-yawma?
li-llāhi l-wāḥidi l-qahhār* (40:16), *wa-l-amru yawmaʾidhin li-llāh* (82:19)."

**Al-Qurṭubī.** On the *dīn* of the verse: the transmitted senses and their authorities (above, V2): the
reckoning and requital; **al-qaḍāʾ** (related from Ibn ʿAbbās); **obedience**; and the lexical material of
Thaʿlab and al-Liḥyānī.

**Al-Ṭabarī.** On *yawmi l-dīn*: “*al-dīn* in this place has the sense of the reckoning and the requital for
deeds” — proof from the poets: Kaʿb b. Juʿayl, *idhā mā ramawnā ramaynāhum / wa-dinnāhumū mithla mā
yuqriḍūnā*, and *wa-ʿlam bi-annaka mā tadīnu tudānu* (“as you requite, so are you requited”); and from
82:9–10, *kallā bal tukadhdhibūna bi-l-dīn… wa-inna ʿalaykum la-ḥāfiẓīn*, and 56:86, *fa-lawlā in kuntum
ghayra madīnīn*. The reports: **Ibn ʿAbbās** (no. 167, weak chain): “the day of the reckoning of creation —
and it is the Day of Resurrection, on which He requites them for their deeds: if good, then good, and if
evil, then evil, except the one He pardons”; **Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd and Companions** (no. 168, the
Suddi chain): “the Day of Reckoning”; **Qatādah** (no. 169): “the day on which Allah requites the servants
for their deeds”; **Ibn Jurayj** (no. 170): “the day on which people are dealt with in reckoning”.

### V8 · Language, lexicon & grammar (*lughah · iʿrāb · balāghah*)

**Al-Baghawī.** The grammatical-and-phonetic material: *mālik*/*malik* as *fārihīn*/*farihīn* variants;
*mālik* vs *malik* scope; *al-dīn*'s senses (reckoning, requital, overpowering, obedience); and Abū ʿAmr's
*idghām* rule-set (above, V9).

**Al-Jalālayn.** On the nouns: “if one reads it *mālik* as opposed to *malik*, then this signifies that He
has possession of the entire affair on the Day of Resurrection; or else that He is ever described by this
expression, in the same way as He is described as ‘Forgiver of sin’ (*ghāfir al-dhanb*). Thus one can
validly take it as an adjective of a definite noun.”

**Al-Ṭabarī.** Besides the derivation of *malik*/*mālik* and the vocative construction (*al-naṣb bi-niyyati
l-nidāʾ*), the section contains his long treatment of the return from third-person description to direct
address at *iyyāka naʿbudu*: “had he known the explanation of the beginning of the sūrah — that *al-ḥamdu
li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn* is Allah's command to His servant to say these words… and had he grasped from the
Arabs that it is their habit, when reporting or commanding the reporting of a speech that follows a saying,
to address and then report of an absent one and report of an absent one and then return to address” — its
proof the poets' lines, and the Qurʾānic *ḥattā idhā kuntum fī l-fulki wa-jarayna bihim bi-rīḥin ṭayyibah*
(10:22), “where He addressed and then returned to reporting of the absent, and did not say *wa-jarayna
bikum*”.

**Ibn Kathīr.** *Al-Mālik*: “Allah is the True Owner (*Malik*) of everything and everyone.” *Al-Dīn*:
“means the reckoning, the reward or punishment”, with 24:25 and 37:53 as evidence. The ḥadīth “*The wise
person is he who reckons himself and works for his life after death*” is given as the sense of “he holds
himself accountable”.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** *Mālik* from *milk*: “possessing a thing in such a manner that one has the right and
power to dispose of it as one likes (see *Qāmūs*)”; *Dīn*: “signifies ‘Requital’”.

**The Study Quran.** The reading *mālik* / *malik* and the comparison of intensity between them (Q, Ṭ, Z);
the etymology of *dīn* (“obedience”, “custom”, “way”, “affair”; most often “religion”) and its relation to
*dayn* (“debt”) and to the divine name *al-Dayyān*.

### V9 · Variant readings (*qirāʾāt*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "**ʿĀṣim, al-Kisāʾī and Yaʿqūb read *mālik*; and the others read *malik*.**" And the
*idghām*: "**Abū ʿAmr read *al-Raḥīmi malik*, merging the *mīm* into the *mīm*; and thus he merges every two
letters of one kind, or one articulation point, or two close points, whether the letter is vowelless or
vowelled** — except where the first letter is doubled, or nunated, or defective, or vowelled *fatḥah*, or
the *tāʾ* of address with a vowelless letter before it of other than the two like ones — these he does not
merge; and the merging of the vowelled is in **the great *idghām***. **Ḥamzah agreed with him in merging the
vowelled** in *bayyata ṭāʾifatun* (4:81), *wa-l-ṣāffāti ṣaffā fa-l-zājirāti zajrā fa-l-tāliyāti dhikrā*
(37:1–3), *wa-l-dhāriyāti dharwā* (51:1) — he merged the *tāʾ* into what follows it of letters; **and
al-Kisāʾī and Ḥamzah agreed with him in the small *idghām***, which is the merging of the vowelless into the
vowelled — except in the *rāʾ* with the *lām*, and the *dāl* with the *jīm*; and likewise Ḥamzah — in the
report of Khallād and Khalaf — does not merge the *dāl* with the *sīn*, the *ṣād* and the *zāy*; and there is
no merging for the rest of the reciters except in counted letters."

**Al-Jalālayn.** The two canonical readings of the word are both weighed: *mālik* (possessor) and *malik*
(king), each with the meaning it adds.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The fourteenth** — **Muḥammad b. al-Samayqaʿ** recited *māliki* with the *naṣb*." And:
"**there are four dialects: *mālik*, *malik*, *malk* (lightened from *malik*), and *malīk***" — with the
poets: *wa-ayyāmin lanā ghurrin ṭiwālin / ʿaṣaynā l-malka fīhā an nadīnā*, and *fa-qnaʿ bimā qasama
l-malīku fa-innamā / qasama l-khalāʾiqa baynanā ʿallāmuhā*. And: "from **Nāfiʿ** is related the full
vowelling of the *kasrah* in *malik*, reading *malikī*, on the dialect of those who make the vowels full — a
dialect of the Arabs mentioned by al-Mahdawī and others."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** “The reciters differ in the recitation of *māliki yawmi l-dīn*: some recite *maliki yawmi
l-dīn*, some recite *māliki yawmi l-dīn*, and some recite *mālika yawmi l-dīn* with *naṣb* of the *kāf*.”
He refers the detail to his *Kitāb al-Qirāʾāt* and gives the meaning of each: “there is no disagreement
among those who know the languages of the Arabs that *malik* is derived from *al-mulk* (sovereignty) and
*mālik* is taken from *al-milk* (ownership).” The *malik* reading means “sovereignty on the Day of
Judgement belongs to Allah alone, without all His creation who had been kings and tyrants in this world,
contending with Him”; the *mālik* reading means “no one has any judgement with Him on that day as they had
in this world” (no. 166, Ibn ʿAbbās, weak chain), supported by 40:16, 78:38, 20:108, 21:28. On the *naṣb*
reading: it is read as a vocative — “*yā mālika yawmi l-dīn*, *iyyāka naʿbudu*”, the *kāf* put in the
accusative by the intention of calling, as in 12:29, *yūsufu aʿriḍ ʿan hādhā*, and as the poets address
*Jazʾ* and *Banī Shāb* with the vocative particle dropped. And his verdict: “the reading *mālika yawmi
l-dīn* is **forbidden, not permitted**, by the consensus of all the authoritative reciters and scholars of
the community upon rejecting recitation by it.”

### V10 · Juristic rulings (*aḥkām*)

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The seventeenth** — it is permitted for one who has the concept to be described by
*mālik* and *malik*: Allah, Mighty, said *inna llāha qad baʿatha lakum Ṭālūta malikā* (2:247); and the
Prophet ﷺ said: *people of my community were shown to me as raiders in the way of Allah, riding the back of
this sea, kings on thrones or like kings on thrones*." And the prohibition of the sixteenth *masʾalah* is
thus confined to the absolute and to *Malik al-Amlāk*.

### V11 · Creed & theology (*ʿaqīdah*)

**Al-Baghawī.** The exclusivity stated in the definition: "*al-mālik* and *al-malik* is **the One able to
bring the entities forth from non-existence into existence, and none other than Allah is able to do that**"
(V7); and, from the *munāsabah*, that on the Day of Judgement **"the ownerships vanish and there is no
sovereignty and no command but His"** (V7).

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The doctrine drawn from the *malik*/*mālik* preferment: "*malik* and *mālik* both witness to
Allah's sole ownership of the Day of Judgement; **the prohibition of the name to creatures** (above) shows
that it belongs to Him alone" — and note the ordering argument: "the *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* and *al-Raḥmān
al-Raḥīm* already established His ownership of the worlds, so *māliki yawmi l-dīn* adds what they do not
have" (al-Fārisī's answer, V2 above).

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The doctrinal weight of the preferred reading: in *malik* “the acknowledgement that
sovereignty on the Day of Judgement belongs to Him alone, without the kings of this world who on the Day of
Judgement pass from their sovereignty to humiliation and contempt” — the Qurʾānic statement of it being
40:16, *li-mani l-mulku l-yawma? li-llāhi l-wāḥidi l-qahhār*.

**As-Saʿdī.** On that day the perfection of His kingship, His justice and His wisdom appears to creation
in full, and the ownership of creatures is cut off, so that kings and subjects, slaves and free are
equal in it: all submissive to His greatness, humble before His might, awaiting His recompense, hoping
His reward, fearing His punishment. For that reason He singled it out; otherwise He is Master of the Day
of Judgement and of every other day as well.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** The ownership of the Day: “there shall come a Day when masters will no more be
masters and slaves no more slaves, when no one will own anything even in appearance, and the ownership and
mastery, apparent as well as real, of the whole universe will be seen to belong to none but Allah, the
Exalted.”

**The Study Quran.** “God's function as *al-Malik* or *al-Mālik* pertains to His management of all affairs
in the created order. It is in a sense a ‘lower’ Divine Name, because the existence of an ‘other’ is
necessary for this aspect of God to become fully manifest. Thus, like the name *al-Raḥīm*, it pertains to
the Divine acts.”

### V15 · Wisdom, counsel & admonition (*ḥikam · tadabbur*)

**Ibn Kathīr.** ʿUmar's saying, quoted for *ad-dīn* as self-reckoning: “Hold yourself accountable before
you are held accountable, weigh yourselves before you are weighed, and be prepared for the biggest
gathering before He Whose knowledge encompasses your deeds — *That Day shall you be brought to Judgement,
not a secret of yours will be hidden* (69:18).”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “The phrase ‘Master of the Day of Judgment’ is a warning to man reeling in his
forgetfulness and self-conceit, and an intimation that all his possessions, all his relationships with
things and men are only short-lived.”

**The Study Quran.** “Given the importance and finality of this accounting”, the Prophet's answer on the
most intelligent of believers and ʿUmar's saying on self-accounting (above) are given as the practical
counsel of the verse.


## 1:5 — إِيَّاكَ نَعْبُدُ وَإِيَّاكَ نَسْتَعِينُ

*[context]* You alone we worship, and You alone we seek help.

### V2 · Running exposition (*bayān*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "*Wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn*: **we ask Your help for Your worship and for all our affairs**." And
the order of the two clauses: "if it is said, **why was the mention of worship put before the seeking of
help, when seeking help comes before worship?** — **this is incumbent on one who puts *istiṭāʿah* before the
act; and we, by Allah's praise, put *tawfīq* and *istiʿānah* with the act, so there is no difference between
putting first and last.** And it is said: **seeking help is a kind of servitude**, so it is as though He
mentioned the whole of worship first and then mentioned what is of its details."

**Al-Jalālayn.** “You alone we worship and You alone we ask for help — that is to say, we reserve worship
for You alone by way of acknowledging Your Oneness (*tawḥīd*), and so on; and we ask for Your assistance
in worship and in other things.”

_Cross-heads: V11 — the exclusive reservation of worship is stated to be the acknowledging of God's
Oneness (*tawḥīd*)._

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The twenty-third** — *iyyāka naʿbudu*: **He returned from the absent to the address
(*iltifāt*), because from the beginning of the sūrah to here it is a report about Allah and praise of Him** —
like *wa-saqāhum rabbuhum sharāban ṭahūrā* (76:21), then *inna hādhā kāna lakum jazāʾan*; and its reverse
*ḥattā idhā kuntum fī l-fulki wa-jarayna bihim* (10:22)." And: "***naʿbudu* means we obey; *al-ʿibādah* is
obedience and humbling; a road is *muʿabbad* when it is trodden down for those who use it (al-Harawī)** —
and the obligation-bound one's saying it **is an acknowledgement of lordship and a verification of Allah's
worship**, since the rest of people worship other than Him of idols and other things. ***Wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn***:
**we seek help, support and success.**" And al-Sulamī in *Ḥaqāʾiq*: "I heard Muḥammad b. ʿAbd Allāh b.
Shādhān say: I heard Abū Ḥafṣ al-Farghānī say: **whoever acknowledges *iyyāka naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn* is
quit of *jabr* and *qadar*.**"

**Al-Ṭabarī.** “The explanation of *iyyāka naʿbudu* is: to You, O Allah, we humble ourselves and abase
ourselves and submit, in acknowledgement to You, our Lord, of lordship — not to other than You.” His reason
for choosing this over the report: “we preferred to explain it as *we humble ourselves and abase ourselves
and submit* rather than as *we hope and we fear*… because servitude (*al-ʿubūdiyyah*), with all the Arabs,
is in origin abasement — they call the trodden road, which feet have pressed down and travellers have
levelled, *muʿabbad*,” with Ṭarafah's verse *tubārī ʿitāqan nājiyātin wa-atbaʿat / waẓīfan waẓīfan fawqa
mawrin muʿabbad*. The transmitted reports: **Ibn ʿAbbās** through Jibrīl (no. 171, weak chain): “*To You we
worship* — You we make one, You we fear and You we hope in, our Lord, not other than You”; and on the
second clause, **Ibn ʿAbbās** (no. 172, weak chain): “*wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn* — from You we seek help in
obeying You and in all our affairs.”

**As-Saʿdī.** The sense is: we single You out alone for worship and for seeking help. Since fronting the
object (*taqdīm al-maʿmūl*) yields exclusivity (*ḥaṣr*), it is as though he says: *We worship You and we
do not worship other than You; we seek help from You and we do not seek help from other than You.*
Placing worship before seeking help is of the order of the general before the specific, and out of
concern to put Allah's right before the servant's right.

*Definitions given by the author:* **worship** (*al-ʿibādah*) is a comprehensive term for everything
Allah loves and is pleased with, of sayings and deeds, outward and inward. **Seeking help**
(*al-istiʿānah*) is reliance upon Allah in bringing benefit and repelling harm, together with trust in
Him to accomplish it.

**Ibn ʿAbbās (as rendered here).** "(Thee (alone) we worship), we turn to you as the only One God and we
obey you; ((Thee alone) we ask for help), we ask for your help in worshipping You and from You we obtain
confidence in obeying You."

_Cross-heads: V11 — "we turn to you as the only One God" states the singling out of Allah in worship
(tawḥīd). V14 — "from You we obtain confidence in obeying You" states reliance on Allah for the strength
to obey._

**Ibn Kathīr.** “*You* means, we worship You alone and none else, and rely on You alone and none else.
This is the perfect form of obedience and the entire religion is implied by these two ideas. Some of the
Salaf said, Al-Fatihah is the secret of the Qur'an, while these words are the secret of Al-Fatihah.” The
first part “is a declaration of innocence from Shirk (polytheism), while the second negates having any
power or strength, displaying the recognition that all affairs are controlled by Allah alone”. On the
order of the two: worship is mentioned before seeking help “because the objective here is the worship,
while Allah's help is the tool to implement this objective”. And on the transition of speech: “in this
Ayah, the type of speech here changes from the third person to direct speech by using the *Kāf* in the
statement *Iyyāka*… because after the servant praised and thanked Allah, he stands before Him, addressing
Him directly.” And: “Since the praise of Allah, Who is being sought for help, was mentioned, it was
appropriate that one follows the praise by asking for his need… This is the best method for seeking help,
by first praising the one whom help is sought from and then asking for His aid” — with the example of
Mūsā's *My Lord! Truly, I am in need of whatever good that You bestow on me* (28:24) and Dhun-Nūn's *Lā
ilāha illā Anta, glorified be You! Truly, I have been of the wrongdoers* (21:87).

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “The fourth verse… has a double aspect, one of praise and another of prayer”, and
the conclusion is drawn from the three preceding verses: “The three verses having made it clear that man
is totally and absolutely dependent on Allah in all the three states of his life, it logically and
naturally leads to the conclusion that Allah alone is worthy of being worshipped, for in Arabic the word
*ʿibādah* (worship) connotes showing the utmost humility and submissiveness out of an intense respect and
love for someone, and such an attitude of willing self-abasement cannot justly be adopted towards anyone
except Allah.” On seeking help: “It may also be noticed that the phrase *iyyāka nastaʿīn* does not mention
the purpose for which help is being sought. According to most of the commentators, it generalizes the idea
of the request to cover everything from acts of worship to all possible worldly or other-worldly
concerns.” And it “teaches man not to worship anyone except Allah, not to consider anyone else as being
really capable of satisfying his needs, and not to beg anyone else to satisfy these needs. It does not,
however, go against this principle if, in praying to Allah, one mentions the name of a prophet or a man of
Allah by way of a medium (*wasīlah*) for drawing the mercy of Allah upon oneself.”

**The Study Quran.** “In v. 5 begins the direct address of God in the second person, a marked change from
the praise and description of God in vv. 1–4 (IK, Q). Human beings speak here in the first-person plural,
hence not only as individuals, but also as vicegerents of God (see 6:165c), supplicating God on behalf of
all creation. To speak to God in the first-person plural rather than the singular also implies humility
before the Divine (Ṭb).” The verb: “*We worship* renders the verb *naʿbudu*, which also indicates
servitude, obedience, and adoration. The first phrase thus indicates standing before God as a humble
adoring servant or as a slave (*ʿabd*), the state that in reality all created beings are in before God, as
in 19:93.” The grammar: “this verse recognizes the state of complete servitude before God, as reflected by
the construction of the sentence in which the direct object is placed before the verb (Ṭb).” The address:
“Addressing God in the second person rather than the third person indicates a direct I-Thou relationship in
which God is worshipped as one who is present before the worshipper and whom the worshipper thus addresses
directly (Ṭb), a privilege given in Islam to all human beings, who are not in need of a distinct priestly
class to function as intermediaries between human beings and God. Correspondingly, worshippers should be
fully present before their Lord. Their attention should not be divided between God and something else,
either openly, as in idol worship, or secretly, as in the case of those who worship God distracted by
thoughts and subjective desires or intent upon personal reward. Rather, one should worship God, devoting
religion entirely to Him (39:2; Ṭb).” On the order of the two phrases: “The expression of servitude
necessarily precedes the statement that one ‘seeks help’ from God, since one does not seek help from a king
without first acknowledging the king's power and sovereignty (Aj). The request for God's help can thus be
seen as a request for further aid in worshipping and serving Him and in all of one's affairs (Aj, IK), a
request founded upon the acknowledgment that one is never independent of God (Ṭb) and that it is only
through His assistance that one can worship Him, not through one's own power and strength (Bq).”

### V4 · Qurʾān cross-references (*tafsīr al-Qurʾān bi-l-Qurʾān*)

**Ibn Kathīr.** 11:123: *So worship Him and put your trust in Him*; 67:29: *He is the Most Gracious, in Him
we believe, and in Him we put our trust*; 73:9: *The Lord of the east and the west; none has the right to
be worshipped but He. So take Him alone as Wakīl*; and 15:97–99, “worship your Lord until there comes unto
you the certainty” — encouraging “the performance of the acts of worship during times of distress”.

**The Study Quran.** 19:93 — *There is none in the heavens and on the earth, but that it comes unto the
Compassionate as a servant*; 39:2 — devoting religion entirely to Him; 47:38 — *God is the Rich, and you
are the poor*; and 6:165c on human beings as vicegerents.

### V5 · Prophetic traditions (ḥadīth)

**Ibn Kathīr.** “The prayer is not valid unless one recites Al-Fatihah, if he is able. The Two Sahihs
recorded that ʿUbādah bin aṣ-Ṣāmit said that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: *There is no valid prayer for
whoever does not recite Al-Fatihah of the Book.*” Also the ḥadīth qudsī of the division of the prayer,
from Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim on the authority of Abū Hurayrah: “*I divided the prayer into two halves between Myself
and My servant… When the servant says, ‘Al-Ḥamdu lillāhi Rabbil-ʿālamīn’, Allah says, ‘My servant has
praised Me’… If the servant says, ‘You we worship, and You we ask for help’, Allah says, ‘This is between
Me and My servant, and My servant shall have what he asked.’*”

### V6 · Companions & Successors (āthār)

**Ibn Kathīr.** Ad-Daḥḥāk narrated that Ibn ʿAbbās said: “*You we worship* means, ‘It is You whom we single
out, Whom we fear and Whom we hope in, You alone, our Lord, and none else’; *And You we ask for help from*,
‘to obey You and in all of our affairs.’” Qatādah said the verse “contains Allah's command to us to perform
sincere worship for Him and to seek His aid concerning all of our affairs”.

**The Study Quran.** “In this vein, Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq is reported to have said, *‘Worship is of three kinds:
some people worship God out of fear, and that is the worship of slaves; other people worship God seeking
reward, and that is the worship of hirelings; and some people worship God out of love, and that is the
worship of those who are free, and that is the most excellent worship’* (Ṭb).”

### V8 · Language, lexicon & grammar (*lughah · iʿrāb · balāghah*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "*Iyyāka naʿbudu*: **«iyyā» is a pronoun word, made specific to annexation to the pronoun,
and it is used placed before the verb** — one says *iyyāka aʿnī*, *iyyāka asʾalu* — **and it is not used
placed after the verb except detached**: one says *mā ʿanaytu illā iyyāka*." "*Naʿbudu* — that is, **we make
You one and obey You humbly**; *al-ʿibādah* is **obedience with abasement and humility**; and the slave
(*al-ʿabd*) is so named **for his abasement and his submission** — one says *ṭarīq muʿabbad*, that is,
trodden down."

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The twenty-fourth** — if it is said: why was the object placed before the verb? — it is
said: **it was placed first out of concern, and it is the way of the Arabs to place the most important
first**; it is mentioned that a bedouin reviled another and the reviled turned away from him, and the reviler
said *iyyāka aʿnī* (you I mean!), and the other said *wa-ʿanka uʿriḍu* (and from you I turn away) — both put
the most important first. And also **lest the mention of the servant and the worship precede the mention of
the Worshipped**: so *naʿbuduka wa-nastaʿīnuka* is not permitted, nor *naʿbudu iyyāka wa-nastaʿīnu iyyāka*,
which would place the verb before the pronoun of the object — rather the wording of the Qurʾān is followed"
— with al-ʿAjjāj: *iyyāka adʿū fa-taqabbal malqiya / waghfir khaṭāyāya wa-kaththir wariqī*; and "the poet's
*ilayka ḥattā balaghat iyyākā* is anomalous and not to be measured by; and **he repeated the name lest it be
imagined that *iyyāka naʿbudu wa-nastaʿīn* means other than You**."
"The twenty-fifth — **the majority of the reciters and scholars read *iyyāka* with *shaddah* of the *yāʾ* in
both places; ʿAmr b. Qāʾid read *iyāka* with *kasrah* of the *hamzah* and lightening of the *yāʾ*** — and
"that is a reading to be turned away from, **for the meaning becomes *your sun we worship*, or *your light***
— *iyāt al-shams* (with *kasrah* of the *hamzah*) is its light" (with the verse *saqathu iyātu l-shamsi illā
lithātihi*). "And **al-Faḍl al-Raqāshī read *iyyāka* with *fatḥah* of the *hamzah*** — a well-known dialect;
and **Abū al-Sawwār al-Ghanawī read *hiyyāka* in the two places** — it is a dialect: *fa-hiyyāka wa-l-amru
lladhī in tawassaʿat / mawāriduhu ḍāqat ʿalayka maṣādiruh*."
"The twenty-sixth — *wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn*: **the coordination of a sentence upon a sentence**; and **Yaḥyā b.
Waththāb and al-Aʿmash read *nastaʿīn* with *kasrah* of the *nūn***, which is the dialect of Tamīm, Asad,
Qays and Rabīʿah, to show that it is from *istaʿāna*, the *nūn* being vowelled with *kasrah* as the *alif*
of conjunction is. And the root of *nastaʿīn* is *nastaʿwin*: the vowel of the *wāw* was moved to the *ʿayn*
and it became *yāʾ*; the *maṣdar* is *istiʿānah*, whose root is *istiʿwān*, the vowel of the *wāw* moved to
the *ʿayn* and it turned to *alif*, and two vowelless letters not meeting, the second *alif* was dropped
because it was extra (and it is said the first, because the second is for the meaning), and the *hāʾ*
remained as a compensation."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The major grammatical argument of the verse is the repetition of *iyyāka*: “the *kāf* in
*iyyāka* is the *kāf* which attaches to the verb — to *naʿbudu* — were it placed after the verb; it is the
pronoun of the addressed name in the accusative by the verb, and it is buttressed by *iyyā* when placed
first, since isolated names in the speech of the Arabs are not left on a single letter.” Hence “it is the
most eloquent speech to repeat it with every verb, as in *we worship You, we seek help from You, we praise
You, we thank You* — just as repeating it with each verb after the verb is the eloquent form, and the
dropping of the repetition permissible.” He rejects the claim that it resembles *bayna* in the poets' use
(ʿAdī b. Zayd: *wa-jāʿili l-shamsi miṣran lā khafāʾa bihi / bayna l-nahāri wa-bayna l-layli qad faṣalā*;
Aʿshā Hamdān: *bayna l-ashajji wa-bayna Qaysin bādhikhun / bakh bakh li-wālidihi wa-li-l-mawlūd*): “the
likeness is from the ignorance of the one who says it; for *iyyāka*'s portion is to be repeated with every
verb… while the ruling of *bayna* is not that: it does not stand alone with one of the two names it
requires, and if it did, the speech would be impossible, as were one to say ‘the sun has separated between
the day’.”

**As-Saʿdī.** The grammar carries the theology. Fronting the object before the verb — *iyyāka* before
*naʿbud* — yields **exclusivity** (*al-ḥaṣr*), which is the affirming of the ruling for the one
mentioned and its negation from all else. Placing worship before seeking help is a case of the general
before the specific (*taqdīm al-ʿāmm ʿalā al-khāṣṣ*).

**Ibn Kathīr.** “Linguistically, *ʿIbādah* means *subdued*. For instance, a road is described as
*Muʿabbadah*, meaning ‘paved’. In religious terminology, *ʿIbādah* implies the utmost love, humility and
fear.” On the fronting of the object (*iyyāka* before the verb) and on the change of grammatical person
from the third to the second, see V2 above.

**The Study Quran.** *Naʿbudu* as indicating “servitude, obedience, and adoration”; the fronting of the
direct object before the verb reflecting complete servitude (Ṭb); the shift from third-person description
to second-person address (IK, Q; Ṭb).

### V9 · Variant readings (*qirāʾāt*)

**Al-Qurṭubī.** As above: the *shaddah* of the majority; ʿAmr b. Qāʾid's *iyāka* (rejected); al-Faḍl
al-Raqāshī's *iyyāka* with *fatḥah*; Abū al-Sawwār al-Ghanawī's *hiyyāka*; Yaḥyā b. Waththāb's and
al-Aʿmash's *nastaʿīn* with the *nūn* vowelled *kasrah*.

### V11 · Creed & theology (*ʿaqīdah*)

**Al-Baghawī.** The doctrinal hinge is explicit in his answer above: "**we put *tawfīq* and *istiʿānah* with
the act**" — against those who put capacity (*istiṭāʿah*) before the act — and the definition of
*al-ʿibādah* as obedience joined with abasement, with *al-ʿabd* named for his submission.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The affirmation is in the *āyah* and is the author's point: "the utterance of the
obligation-bound one by it is **an acknowledgement of lordship and a verification of His worship**, since the
rest of people worship other than Him" (V2); and al-Farghānī's word that "**whoever acknowledges *iyyāka
naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn* is quit of *jabr* and *qadar***" — in the sense that the servant both worships and
asks help, neither compelled nor self-sufficient.

**Al-Ṭabarī.** Two doctrinal arguments are made from this verse:

* **On the need to ask God's help in obeying Him.** To the question — if He has commanded obedience and
  granted the enablement, why ask? — he answers: “the believer who asks his Lord to help him to obey Him is
  asking for help in what remains of his life, not in what has passed of his righteous acts; and the
  servant's asking is valid because Allah's giving him that, with His enabling his limbs for what He has
  imposed on him, is a favour from Him and a subtlety He has shown him.”
* **Against the *Qadariyyah* of *tafwīḍ*.* “And in Allah's commanding His servants to say *iyyāka naʿbudu
  wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn*, in the sense of asking Him for help in worship, there is the most telling proof of the
  corruption of the saying of those of *al-Qadariyyah* who hold *al-tafwīḍ*, who declare it impossible that
  Allah should command a servant with a command, or impose an obligation, without first giving him the help
  to do it and to leave it. Were it as they say, the desire of Allah in help for His obedience would be
  void, since on their saying the giving of help would be a right owing from Allah to the servant whether he
  asked or not.” His proof: “the consensus of the people of Islam in approving the words *O Allah, we seek
  help from You* and in rejecting the words *O Allah, do not be unjust to us*” — that is, no one says the
  second.

**Ibn Kathīr.** The verse is presented under the headings **Tawḥīd al-Ulūhiyyah** — Ibn ʿAbbās's “It is You
whom we single out, Whom we fear and Whom we hope in” — and **Tawḥīd al-Rubūbiyyah** — “to obey You and in
all of our affairs”. “The first part is a declaration of innocence from Shirk.” He also notes that “Allah
called His Messenger an *ʿAbd* (servant) when He mentioned sending down His Book, the Prophet's
involvement in inviting to Him, and when mentioning the Isrāʾ… these are the Prophet's most honorable
missions”, citing 18:1, 72:19 and 17:1.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** Under “Seeking Allah's Help Directly and Indirectly” the two forms of association in
seeking help are set out: “one becomes guilty of association, if one seeks the help of an angel or prophet
or saint or any creature believing him to be omnipotent like Allah”; and the second, “the form adopted by
idolaters and associators. They admit that God alone is Omnipotent, but also believe that He has delegated
a part of His power to an angel or a prophet or a saint… and to whom one may pray for help in matters
within his jurisdiction. This is the supplication which the Holy Qur'an forbids, and against which it warns
us in the phrase ‘to You alone we pray for help’.” On miracles: “Miracles and wonders are the direct acts of
Allah, but they are manifested through prophets and saints so that people may recognize their spiritual
station — prophets and saints themselves have no powers to make such things happen”, with 8:17 — *When you
threw, it was not you that threw, but Allah threw*; 11:33; and 14:11 — *We cannot give you proof, except by
Allah's will.* The analogy: “In your room, you receive light from a lamp and air from a fan, but the lamp
and the fan do not possess in themselves an absolute power… Similarly, saints, prophets and angels, all
depend on Allah in everything they do.” And the conclusion on *wasīlah*: “Taking prophets and saints as a
medium for drawing divine mercy upon oneself is neither absolutely permissible nor absolutely forbidden…
If one does so, believing a prophet or a saint to be all-powerful, it becomes an act of *shirk* and is
hence forbidden. But if one takes a prophet or a saint to be no more than a medium or a means, it is
permissible.”

**The Study Quran.** “a privilege given in Islam to all human beings, who are not in need of a distinct
priestly class to function as intermediaries between human beings and God”; and *ʿibādah* as “the greatest
degree of humility and self-effacement before God (Aj), which is necessary in order to recognize one's
complete reliance upon Him”.

### V14 · Spiritual purification & etiquette (*tazkiyah · ādāb*)

**Al-Baghawī.** The two clauses together as the servant's disposition: **making one (*tawḥīd*) and obeying
humbly**, then asking help for it and for all affairs — the sequence the author harmonises.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "The twenty-fourth" above is quoted among the *ādāb* of speech before God: **the servant
does not put his own mention or his worship before the mention of the Worshipped, and the repetition of the
name guards the heart from imagining another addressed.**

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The inner sense of *ʿubūdiyyah* is abasement before God: “servitude with all the Arabs is in
origin abasement… and from it the slave is called *ʿabd* for his abasement before his master”; and the
man's confession “*to You we humble ourselves and abase ourselves and submit*” is the disposition the verse
teaches.

**As-Saʿdī.** Establishing the worship of Allah and seeking help from Him is the means to eternal
felicity and deliverance from all evils; there is no way to deliverance except by establishing both.
And worship is only worship when it is taken from the Messenger of Allah ﷺ and intended for Allah's
face — by these two matters it becomes worship. Seeking help is mentioned after worship although it is
included in it because the servant needs Allah's help in all his acts of worship: if Allah does not help
him, he will not obtain what he wants of doing what is commanded and avoiding what is forbidden.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “Acts of worship (*ʿibādah*) are not limited merely to prescribed prayers or
fasting. Imam al-Ghazzālī in his book *Arbaʿīn* has enumerated ten forms which worship can take: (1)
Prayers; (2) Prescribed alms-giving; (3) Fasting; (4) Hajj; (5) Reciting the Holy Qur'an; (6) Remembrance
of Allah in all possible situations; (7) Earning one's livelihood in accordance with the regulations of the
Sharīʿah; (8) Fulfilling one's obligations towards one's companions and neighbours; (9) Persuading people
to act righteously and dissuading them from what is reprehensible and forbidden; (10) To follow the Sunnah
of the Holy Prophet ﷺ.” And the content of tawḥīd in worship: “one should not love or fear or depend on
anyone else as one loves, or fears, or depends on Allah, nor repose one's hope in anyone else, nor consider
obedience or submission or service to another as obligatory as the worship of Allah, nor make a votive
offering or consecrate anything to anyone… nor show complete self-abasement and total humility before
anyone as one is required to do before Allah, nor engage in the particular God-oriented acts of worship
for anyone other than Allah… such as *rukūʿ* and *sujūd*.”

_Cross-heads: V16 — the treatment of *wasīlah* and of the two extremes (outright rejection or exaggerated
veneration) answers a live contemporary dispute and is recorded under contemporary application at 1:7._

**The Study Quran.** The three kinds of worship (Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq, above): “some people worship God out of
love, and that is the worship of those who are free, and that is the most excellent worship”; and the
requirement of presence: the worshipper's “attention should not be divided between God and something else”.

### V15 · Wisdom, counsel & admonition (*ḥikam · tadabbur*)

**Al-Baghawī.** The ordering-aporia above and its resolution ("seeking help is a kind of servitude… the
whole of worship first, then what is of its details") is his contribution of wisdom on the verse's
arrangement.

**Al-Qurṭubī.** Following from the above: the *iyyāka* form is "from the concern and the placing of the most
important first" — the pattern of attention the sūrah teaches; and "(they) repeated the name lest it be
imagined" — the concern for clarity of intention in worship.

**Al-Ṭabarī.** On the order of the two clauses: to the question why worship is mentioned before the asking
of help, when worship is only by means of help, he answers that the two are inseparable and equal in rank —
“it is the same whether you say *O Allah, You alone we worship, so help us to worship You*, or *O Allah,
help us to worship You, for You alone we worship*” — with the analogy of the man who has done you a service
and done it well: “you may say *you did my need and you were kind to me* or *you were kind to me and you did
my need*, for he is not a doer of your need but a benefactor to you, nor a benefactor to you but a doer of
your need.” And he rejects the view that the verse is of the placed-first meaning the postponed, “as
Imruʾ al-Qays: *wa-law anna mā asʿā li-adnā maʿīshatin / kafānī wa-lam aṭlub qalīlun mina l-māl*” — that
pattern does not apply here, because in the poet's case the sufficiency does not entail the giving-up of the
search.

**Ibn Kathīr.** The order of the verse teaches the method of supplication: “This method is more appropriate
and efficient in bringing about a positive answer to the pleas, and this is why Allah recommended this
better method” — praise first, then the request; and he adds the two other manners: stating one's need
(Mūsā, 28:24) and praising the one asked without mentioning the need.


## 1:6 — اهْدِنَا الصِّرَاطَ الْمُسْتَقِيمَ

*[context]* Guide us to the straight path.

### V2 · Running exposition (*bayān*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "*Ihdinā* — **guide us** (*arshidnā*); and **ʿAlī and Ubayy b. Kaʿb said: keep us firm
(*thabbitnā*)** — as one says to a man standing, *stand until I return to you*, that is, remain on what you
are on. **And this supplication from the believers, though they are already on guidance, means firmness and
means asking for more guidance, because the kindnesses and guidances of Allah do not come to an end — on the
doctrine of *Ahl al-Sunnah*.**"

**Al-Jalālayn.** “Guide us to the straight path — that is, ‘show us the way to it’.”

_Cross-heads: V8 — the sense of this verse is completed by the substitution that follows in 1:7, recorded
there._

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The twenty-seventh** — *ihdinā l-ṣirāṭa l-mustaqīm*: ***ihdinā* is a supplication and a
desire from the subject to the Lord**, and the meaning is ***show us the straight path and guide us to
it, and show us the way of Your guidance that leads to Your intimacy and nearness***." And the sūrah's
placing: "one of the scholars said: **Allah placed the greatness of supplication, and its sum, in this
sūrah: half of it is the gathering of praise and half of it is the gathering of needs**; and He made this
supplication better than the one a suppliant makes himself, because this speech was spoken by the Lord of the
worlds, so you supplicate with a supplication that is His speech — and in the ḥadīth: *nothing is more
honourable to Allah than supplication*." And: "it was said: the meaning is ***guide us by the use of the
Sunnah in the performance of Your ordinances***; and it was said: **the root in it is inclining (*al-imālah*)**
— from His word *innā hudnā ilayka* (7:156), that is, we inclined; and the Prophet ﷺ went out in his illness
*yatahādā* between two men, that is, swaying; and from it *al-hadiyyah*, because it is inclined from a king
to a king; and from it *al-hady* for the animal driven to the sanctuary — **so the meaning is: incline our
hearts to the truth.**"

**Al-Ṭabarī.** “The sense of *ihdinā l-ṣirāṭa l-mustaqīm* in this place, in our view, is: **grant us success in
steadfastness upon it** (*waffiqnā li-l-thabāti ʿalayh*)”, as reported from Ibn ʿAbbās (no. 173, weak chain,
through Jibrīl): “*Say, O Muḥammad:* ihdinā l-ṣirāṭa l-mustaqīm — that is, *inspire us with the guiding
road*”; and his inspiring it is His granting success in it. He then specifies the sense of the petition: it
is “the asking of the servant from his Lord success in steadfastness upon acting in His obedience, and in
hitting the truth and the right in what He has commanded and forbidden, in what remains of his life, not in
what has passed.” The whole verse he paraphrases: “*O Allah, You alone we worship, alone, with no partner —
devotion sincere to You apart from the gods and idols besides You; so help us to worship You, and grant us
success in what You granted success to those You blessed of Your prophets and the people of Your obedience,
of way and method.*”

**As-Saʿdī.** The sense is: direct us, guide us and grant us success to the straight path — the clear
road that leads to Allah and to His Paradise, which is the knowledge of the truth and action upon it. So
we ask for guidance *to* the path and guidance *in* the path. **Guidance to the path** is adherence to
the religion of Islam and the abandonment of every other religion; **guidance in the path** includes
guidance in all the details of the religion, in knowledge and in deed.

**Ibn ʿAbbās (as rendered here).** Three reports are given for "Guide us to the straight path":

1. "Guide us to the established Religion that You are pleased with, i.e. Islam."
2. "It is also said that this means: make us firm in holding fast to it."
3. "It is also said that the straight path refers to Allah's Book, and guidance to this means guidance to that which it prescribes as lawful or unlawful and to the exposition of its content."

**Ibn Kathīr.** “The guidance mentioned in the Surah implies being directed and guided to success… meaning
guide, direct, lead and grant us the correct guidance.” On the straight path, at-Ṭabarī is quoted: “The
Ummah agreed that *Ṣirāṭ al-Mustaqīm* is the clear path without branches, according to the language of the
Arabs”, with Jarīr bin ʿAṭiyyah's poem: “The Leader of the faithful is on a path that will remain straight
even though the other paths are crooked”; and at-Ṭabarī again: “The Arabs use the term *Ṣirāṭ* in
reference to every deed and statement whether righteous or wicked… The straight path mentioned in the
Qur'an refers to Islam.” The answer to why the guided believer still asks: “The servant needs Allah the
Exalted every hour of his life to help him remain firm on the path of guidance and to make him even more
firm and persistent on it… Hence, *Guide us to the straight way* means, ‘Make us firm on the path of
guidance and do not allow us to deviate from it.’”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “A problem of highest significance arises here. The teaching with regard to the
prayer for being guided in the straight path is addressed equally to all men or all Muslims and to saints
and prophets who have already received guidance and are even a source of guidance for other men. Why
should these repeatedly pray for something they already possess? The answer… depends on knowing all that is
implied by guidance.” The answer is given in degrees, after al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī: “*Hidāyah* signifies
leading someone towards his destination, gently and kindly; while guidance, in the real sense, issues forth
from Allah alone, and it has several degrees.” The **first degree** “is general, and covers everything that
exists in the universe — minerals, plants, animals etc.”: “Allah has given every creature a particular
nature and function, and guided it in a way which should correspond to its station in the scheme of
things.” The **second degree** “is not general but particular. It is limited to those creatures which are
considered to be rational, that is, men and jinns. This kind of guidance comes to every man through
prophets and revealed books.” The **third degree** “is still more particular, being special to true
believers (*Muʾminīn*) and the God-fearing (*Muttaqīn*)… and it is called *Tawfīq*. That is to say, Allah's
grace provides a man with internal and external means and circumstances which should make it easy, and
even pleasant for him to accept and act upon the guidance of the Holy Qur'an, and difficult to ignore or
oppose it. The scope of the third degree of guidance is limitless, and its levels indefinite… The agency of
this progress is the performance of virtuous deeds. All increase in virtuous deeds brings with it an
increase in divine guidance.” Hence “of all the prayers man can address to Allah, the most important is the
prayer for guidance, which has been taught to us in the very first Surah of the Holy Qur'an”. Three notes
of caution are drawn: the general and the special senses of guidance remove the apparent contradiction in
the Qurʾān's statements that Allah guides all and that He does not guide the unjust; and “The first and the
third degrees of guidance pertain to a direct act of divine grace, and no prophet can have anything to do
with it, for the function of the prophets is related only to the second degree”, so that 28:56 — *You
cannot guide whom you please* — concerns the third degree.

**The Study Quran.** “Following upon the previous phrase, in which one asks for God's help in servitude and
obedience, *guide us* can be understood as a prayer for perseverance in following the straight path and
thus for continued aid (Ṭ) and for being made firm in following the way of truth (Ṭs), since *for those who
believe and perform righteous deeds, their Lord guides them by their faith* (10:9; Ṭs). The request for
guidance also implies seeking to be led to God Himself and thus a desire for intimacy with Him, nearness to
Him (Q), knowledge of Him, and love for Him. In this sense, it is also understood to mean, ‘Make our hearts
incline unto Thee; direct our aspirations unto Thee; and be our guide from Thee, unto Thee, in order that we
not be cut off by Thee from that which is Thine’ (Bq, Su). On the intellectual level, following the straight
path can be understood to mean using one's God-given intelligence correctly in order to reach the truth.”
The path: “*The straight path* indicates a clear road with no crookedness or deviation (Ṭ). From a religious
perspective, it is a middle way that avoids worldliness, on the one hand, and extreme asceticism, on the
other. It thus connotes a way of life that combines the outward way of the Law with the inward way of
spirituality, or the exoteric with the esoteric, so that one's outward nature is in servitude, while one's
inner nature is free (Aj).” And: “To be on the straight path… can also be understood as walking with God to
God; to God because God guides the believers unto Himself upon a straight path (4:175), and with God
because God Himself is upon a straight path, as when the Prophet Hūd says… *Truly my Lord is upon a
straight path* (11:56).”

### V4 · Qurʾān cross-references (*tafsīr al-Qurʾān bi-l-Qurʾān*)

**Al-Ṭabarī.** 37:23, *fa-hdūhum ilā ṣirāṭi l-jaḥīm*, is adduced for the sense of “leading into”: “as the
woman is led to her husband, and as a gift is led to a man, and as the foot leads the leg”, with Ṭarafah's
verse *li-l-fatā ʿaqlun yaʿīshu bihi / ḥaythu tahdī sāqahu qadamuh* — “meaning, he brings them in”.

**Ibn Kathīr.** 90:10: *And shown him the two ways*; 16:121: *He chose him and guided him to a straight
path*; 37:23: *And lead them on to the way of flaming Fire*; 42:52: *And verily, you are indeed guiding to
the straight path*; 7:43: *All praise be to Allah, Who has guided us to this*; 4:16: *O you who believe!
Believe in Allah…* — “this command is not redundant since what is sought here is firmness and
continuity”; and 3:8: *Our Lord! Let not our hearts deviate after You have guided us.*

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** 20:50: *He gave to everything its distinctive form, and then guided it*; 87:1–2:
*Who has created all things, well proportioned them, and Who has determined and guided them*; 19:93;
17:44: *Nothing exists that does not celebrate His praise*; 24:41; 47:17: *As for those who follow the
straight path, Allah will increase their guidance*; 64:11: *And whoever believes in Allah, He guides his
heart*; 29:69: *Those who strive for Us, We will surely guide them in Our paths*; 48:20; 28:56.

**The Study Quran.** 10:9; 42:52–53c (the Prophet guiding his community upon this path); 2:143 (the
community “described as a middle community”); 4:175; 11:56.

### V5 · Prophetic traditions (ḥadīth)

**Ibn Kathīr.** Imam Aḥmad recorded in his *Musnad* that an-Nawwās bin Samʿān said that the Prophet ﷺ
said: “*Allah has set an example: a Ṣirāṭ (straight path) that is surrounded by two walls on both sides,
with several open doors within the walls covered with curtains. There is a caller on the gate of the Ṣirāṭ
who heralds, ‘O people! Stay on the path and do not deviate from it.’ Meanwhile, a caller from above the
path is also warning any person who wants to open any of these doors, ‘Woe unto you! Do not open it, for
if you open it you will pass through.’ The straight path is Islam, the two walls are Allah's set limits,
while the doors resemble what Allah has prohibited. The caller on the gate of the Ṣirāṭ is the Book of
Allah, while the caller above the Ṣirāṭ is Allah's admonishment in the heart of every Muslim.*”

**The Study Quran.** The parable of the path, reported from the Prophet: “*God has set forth a parable: a
straight path that is surrounded by two walls on both sides, with several open doors within the walls
covered by curtains. A caller on the gate of the path calls out, “O people! Stay on the path and do not
deviate from it.” Meanwhile, a caller from above the path is also warning any person who wants to open any
of these doors, “Woe unto you! Do not open it, for if you open it you will fall through.” The straight path
is Islam, the two walls are the limits set by God, while the doors refer to what God has prohibited. The
caller on the gate of the path is the Book of God, while the caller above the path is God's admonishment
present in the heart of every Muslim*’ (IK).”

### V7 · Exegesis of the classical mufassirūn (*aqwāl al-mufassirīn*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "***Al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm*: Ibn ʿAbbās and Jābir said: it is Islam** — and it is the saying of
**Muqātil**; **Ibn Masʿūd said: it is the Qurʾān**; **and it is related from ʿAlī *marfūʿan*: *the straight
path is the Book of Allah***; **Saʿīd b. Jubayr: the road of the Garden**; **Sahl b. ʿAbd Allāh: the way of
the Sunnah and the community**; **Bakr b. ʿAbd Allāh al-Muzanī: the way of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ**; **and
Abū al-ʿĀliyah and al-Ḥasan: the Messenger and his family and his two companions**; **and its root in the
language is the clear road (*al-ṭarīq al-wāḍiḥ*).**"

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**al-Fuḍayl b. ʿIyāḍ**: *al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm* is **the road of the Ḥajj** — and this is
specific, and the general is worthier. **Muḥammad b. al-Ḥanafiyyah**: "it is **the religion of Allah which
accepts no other** from the worshippers"; and **ʿĀṣim al-Aḥwal from Abū al-ʿĀliyah**: "*al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm*
is **the Messenger of Allah ﷺ and his two companions after him**" — and ʿĀṣim said: I said to al-Ḥasan: Abū
al-ʿĀliyah says *al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm* is the Messenger of Allah ﷺ and his two companions — he said:
"**Abū al-ʿĀliyah spoke the truth and gave good counsel.**""

**Al-Ṭabarī.** From the verse's own plain sense: “the community of the people of explanation are agreed that
*al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm* is the clear path in which there is no crookedness; and that is so in the language of
all the Arabs” — with Jarīr's line *amīru l-muʾminīna ʿalā ṣirāṭin / idhā iʿwajja l-mawāridu mustaqīm*, and
Abū Dhuʾayb's *ṣabaḥnā arḍahum bi-l-khayli ḥattā / taraknāhā adaqqa mina l-ṣirāṭ*. Then: “the Arabs borrow
*al-ṣirāṭ* and use it for every saying and act described as straight or crooked.” His preferred tafsīr:
“that it means: *grant us success in steadfastness upon what You are pleased with, and upon what You granted
success to those You blessed of Your servants, in word and deed* — and that is the straight path. For
whoever is granted success in what the blessed of Allah were granted success in, of the prophets, the
truthful, the witnesses and the righteous, has been granted success in Islam, in affirming the messengers,
in holding to the Book, in acting by what Allah commanded and refraining from what He forbade, and in
following the method of the Prophet ﷺ and the method of Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān and ʿAlī, and every
righteous servant of Allah — and all of that is of the straight path.” The reports on the identification:

* **ʿAlī from the Prophet ﷺ** (nos. 174–175, two chains): “He mentioned the Qurʾān and said: *It is the
  straight path*.” And **from ʿAlī** directly (no. 176): “*The straight path is the Book of Allah.*”
* **Ibn Masʿūd** (no. 177): “*The straight path is the Book of Allah.*”
* **Jābir b. ʿAbd Allāh** (no. 178): “It is Islam — and it is wider than what is between the heaven and the
  earth.”
* **Ibn ʿAbbās** (no. 179, weak chain): “*inspire us with the guiding road* — and it is the religion of Allah
  in which there is no crookedness”; and (no. 180): “that is Islam”; and (no. 183): “*the path*”.
* **Ibn al-Ḥanafiyyah** (no. 181): “the religion of Allah, which He accepts from the servants no other.”
* **Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and Companions** through al-Suddī (no. 182): “it is Islam.”
* **Abū al-ʿĀliyah** (no. 184): “it is the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, and his two companions after him, Abū Bakr
  and ʿUmar” — and when this was mentioned to al-Ḥasan, he said: “Abū al-ʿĀliyah spoke the truth and gave
  good counsel.”
* **ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. Zayd** (no. 185): “Islam.”
* **Nawwās b. Samʿān** from the Prophet ﷺ (nos. 186–187, two chains through Muʿāwiyah b. Ṣāliḥ): the parable
  of the straight path surrounded by two walls with open doors and curtains, the caller at its head, and the
  caller above it — “*and the path is Islam*.”

**The Study Quran.** “Some see the straight path as a reference to the Quran itself (Ṭ, Ṭū, Ṭs), while others
say that it refers to Islam (Bq, Ṭū, Ṭs) or to the Prophet (Q). According to many Shiite commentaries, the
straight path refers to the first Shiite Imam, ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib (Qm, Ṭs), or to the Prophet and the Imams
(Ṭū, Ṭs). In contrast, several Sunni commentaries say that it refers to the Prophet and his two Companions
(Q, Ṭ), meaning the first two Caliphs, Abū Bakr… and ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb.” The work's comment: “Each of
these interpretations indicates something that different groups of Muslims believe will assure one of being
upon the straight path, as long as one follows it.”

### V8 · Language, lexicon & grammar (*lughah · iʿrāb · balāghah*)

**Al-Ṭabarī.** *Hidāyah* in the speech of the Arabs means success-granting (*al-tawfīq*): “that in their
speech is more plentiful and more evident than can be counted” — with the poets' lines: *lā taḥrimannanī
hadāka llāhu masʾalatī* (“may Allah grant you success in my need”), and *wa-lā tuʿjilannī hadāka l-malīku /
fa-inna li-kulli maqāmin maqālā*. Hence “*wa-llāhu lā yahdī l-qawma l-ẓālimīn* does not mean He does not make
clear to the wrongdoers what He has imposed on them of His ordinances — for He has made the explanation
general to all those under obligation — but it means He does not grant them success, nor open their breasts
to the truth and to faith.” He also records the three Arabic constructions all used in the Qurʾān: “*hadaytu
fulānan al-ṭarīq*, *hadaytuhu li-l-ṭarīq*, and *hadaytuhu ilā l-ṭarīq*” — 7:43, *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi lladhī
hadānā li-hādhā*; 16:121, *ijtabāhu wa-hadāhu ilā ṣirāṭin mustaqīm*; and 1:6 itself.

**Ibn Kathīr.** The lexicography of *ṣirāṭ* from at-Ṭabarī: “the clear path without branches”; the noun
used by the Arabs “in reference to every deed and statement whether righteous or wicked”, so that “the
Arabs would describe the honest person as being straight and the wicked person as being crooked”.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** The lexical discussion of *hidāyah* — “leading someone towards his destination,
gently and kindly” — and its three degrees, from al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī's *Mufradāt al-Qurʾān*.

**The Study Quran.** *The straight path* as “a clear road with no crookedness or deviation (Ṭ)”; the
middle-way sense of the term.

### V9 · Variant readings (*qirāʾāt*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "*Al-ṣirāṭ* and *sirāṭ* with the *sīn* — related by Uways from Yaʿqūb — **and it is the
root, named *sirāṭ* because it swallows (*yasraṭu*) the traveller**; and it is read with the *zāʾ*; and
**Ḥamzah read with *ishmām* of the *zāʾ***; **all of them are sound dialects; and the choice is the *ṣād*
with most of the reciters, for agreement with the *muṣḥaf*.**"

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The twenty-eighth** — the root of *al-ṣirāṭ* in the speech of the Arabs is the road" —
with ʿĀmir b. al-Ṭufayl's *shaḥannā arḍahum bi-l-khayli ḥattā / taraknāhum adhalla mina l-ṣirāṭ* and Jarīr's
*amīru l-muʾminīna ʿalā ṣirāṭin / idhā ʿiwajja l-mawāridu mustaqīm*, and the rājiz's *fa-ṣudda ʿan nahji
l-ṣirāṭi l-qāṣid*. "And **al-Naqqāsh related: *al-ṣirāṭ* is the road in the Byzantine tongue (*bi-lughati
l-Rūm*)** — Ibn ʿAṭiyyah said: **this is very weak**." And: "**it was read *al-sirāṭ* (with *sīn*) from
*al-istirāṭ*, meaning the swallowing, as though the road swallows whoever travels it; and it was read between
the *zāʾ* and the *ṣād*; and it was read with pure *zāʾ*, and *sīn* is the root**; **Salamah related from
al-Farrāʾ**: *al-zirāṭ* with pure *zāʾ* is a dialect of ʿUdhrah, Kalb and Banū al-Qayn — and these (Kalb and
the like) say *azdaq* for *aṣdaq*; and they said *al-Azd* and *al-Asd*, and *lasiqa bihi* and *laṣiqa bihi*."
And the *iʿrāb*: "***al-ṣirāṭ* is in the accusative as the second object, because the verb of guidance
transgresses to the second object with a preposition — *fa-hdūhum ilā ṣirāṭi l-jaḥīm* (37:23) — and without
it, as in this verse**; *al-mustaqīm* is a description of *al-ṣirāṭ*, and it is the one in which there is no
crookedness and no deviation, as in *wa-anna hādhā ṣirāṭī mustaqīman fa-ttabiʿūh* (6:153); and its root is
*mustaqwim*, the vowel moved to the *qāf* and the *wāw* turning to *yāʾ* for the *kasrah* before it."

### V10 · Juristic rulings (*aḥkām*)

**Al-Ṭabarī.** Two rulings of interpretation, both stated as exclusions: the verse cannot mean asking for
the **explanation** of the obligations — “for Allah does not impose an ordinance on a servant except after
making it clear to him and establishing the proof against him; and to ask for explanation of what has been
imposed would be absurd, since He imposes nothing but what is made clear”; and it cannot mean “**increase
us in guidance**” in the sense of explanation, since what remains of the obligations is not yet imposed. It
must therefore mean the granting of success for what remains of life. He likewise refutes the reading which
makes the verse “*lead us into the path of Paradise*” (the *asliknā* sense), holding that “the exegetes of
the Companions and the Successors are agreed that the sense of *al-ṣirāṭ* here is other than what the
speaker of this view said.”

**As-Saʿdī.** This supplication is among the most comprehensive and most beneficial of supplications for
the servant; and for that reason it is obligatory for a person to ask Allah by it in every cycle (*rakʿah*)
of his prayer, because of his need for it.

### V13 · History, sīrah & parables (*qiṣaṣ · sīrah*)

**The Study Quran.** “The straight path is also understood as a reference to the traverse that human beings
and jinn must cross as the final stage of Judgment before entering Paradise or the Garden. According to the
Prophet, this traverse is ‘finer than a hair and sharper than a sword’; some will pass over it like wind,
and some will fly across it like birds; others will trot over it like camels. Yet others will only be able
to crawl, and some will be cast from it into Hellfire; see the essay ‘Death, Dying, and the Afterlife in the
Quran.’”

### V15 · Wisdom, counsel & admonition (*ḥikam · tadabbur*)

**Ibn Kathīr.** Why the believer asks for guidance in every prayer: “The servant does not have the power to
benefit or harm himself, except by Allah's permission. Therefore, Allah directed the servant to invoke Him
constantly, so that He provides him with His aid and with firmness and success. Indeed, the happy person
is he whom Allah guides to ask of Him.”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “To sum up, the Qurʾānic prayer ‘guide us in the straight path’ is most
comprehensive, and certainly, one of the most important prayers taught to man. No member of the human
family can claim not to need it… Particularly so, for man lost in the anxieties of mortal life, the prayer
for the straight path is an elixir, though people do not realize it.”

### V17 · Specific virtues & supplications (*faḍāʾil · duʿāʾ*)

**As-Saʿdī.** "Guide us to the straight path" is one of the most comprehensive of supplications and the
most beneficial of them for the servant — it gathers the request of guidance in knowledge and in deed,
in the root and in the branches.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** The verse teaches the manner of supplication: “The proper method is that one should
begin by fulfilling one's obligation to praise Allah. Then, one should offer the pledge of complete
allegiance to Allah… Finally, one should pray for what one wishes to have. And there is every hope that a
prayer made in this manner will be granted.” And the rule of comprehensiveness: “in praying to Allah, one
should pray for something so comprehensive that it includes in essence all possible human goals — for
example, pray for being guided in the straight path.”


## 1:7 — صِرَاطَ الَّذِينَ أَنْعَمْتَ عَلَيْهِمْ غَيْرِ الْمَغْضُوبِ عَلَيْهِمْ وَلَا الضَّالِّينَ

*[context]* The path of those whom You have blessed — not of those who incurred wrath, nor of those who are astray.

### V2 · Running exposition (*bayān*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "*Ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim* — meaning **the path of those with whom You are angry**
(*ghaḍibta ʿalayhim*); **and *al-ghaḍab* is the will of vengeance on the disobedient** — **and Allah's
wrath, Mighty and Exalted, does not reach the disobedient among the believers; it reaches only the
unbelievers.** *Wa-lā l-ḍāllīn* — that is, **and not of those astray from guidance**; **and the root of
*al-ḍalāl* is perishing and disappearing** — one says *ḍalla l-māʾu fī l-laban*, when it perished and
disappeared."

**Al-Jalālayn.** The three groups are identified: *those against whom there is wrath* are “the Jews”, and
*those who are astray* are “the Christians”. “The subtle meaning implied by this substitution is that the
guided ones are neither the Jews nor the Christians.”

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The twenty-ninth** — *ṣirāṭa lladhīna anʿamta ʿalayhim*: ***ṣirāṭ* is an apposition to
the first, the apposition of a thing to a thing** — as you say *Zayd came to me, your father*; **and its
meaning is: *keep our guidance enduring* — for a man may be guided to the road and then be cut off on it.**
And it was said: it is **another path**, and its meaning is the knowledge of Allah, Mighty and Exalted, and
the understanding of Him (Jaʿfar b. Muḥammad)." And on the word *alladhīna*: "the language of the Qurʾān is
*alladhīna* in the nominative, the accusative and the genitive; Hudhayl says *al-ladhūna* in the nominative,
and some Arabs say *al-ladhū*, and some *alladhī*." And on *ʿalayhim*: "in *ʿalayhim* there are ten dialects,
of which six readings are transmitted from the Imams of reciters: (1) *ʿalayhim* with *ḍammah* of the *hāʾ*
and silence of the *mīm* — the reading of the generality; (2) with *kasrah* of the *hāʾ* and silence of the
*mīm*; (3) *ʿalayhimī* with *kasrah* of the *hāʾ* and the *mīm* and a *yāʾ* after the *kasrah*; (4)
*ʿalayhimū* with *kasrah* of the *hāʾ* and *ḍammah* of the *mīm* and a *wāw* after the *ḍammah*; (5)
*ʿalayhumū* with *ḍammah* of both and a *wāw* after the *mīm*; (6) *ʿalayhum* with *ḍammah* of both without
the added *wāw*. And four faces are related from the Arabs, not cited from the reciters: *ʿalayhumī* with
*ḍammah* of the *hāʾ* and *kasrah* of the *mīm* and a *yāʾ* (related by al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī from the Arabs);
*ʿalayhumi* with the *mīm* vowelled *kasrah*, no *yāʾ*; *ʿalayhimu* with *kasrah* of the *hāʾ* and *ḍammah*
of the *mīm* without a *wāw*; and *ʿalayhimi* with both vowelled *kasrah*, no *yāʾ* after the *mīm*; **and
all of them are correct** (Ibn al-Anbārī)."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** “*Ṣirāṭa lladhīna anʿamta ʿalayhim* is a clarification of the straight path — which path it
is — since every road of the roads of truth is a straight path.” And the wording given: “Say, O Muḥammad:
*Guide us, our Lord, to the straight path — the path of those You blessed by Your obedience and Your
worship, of Your angels, Your prophets, the truthful, the witnesses and the righteous*”, with 4:66–69 as the
Qurʾānic parallel naming those blessed. The reports: **Ibn ʿAbbās** (no. 188, weak chain): “the path of
those You blessed by Your obedience and worship, of the angels and the prophets and the truthful and the
witnesses and the righteous, who obeyed You and worshipped You”; **al-Rabīʿ** (no. 189): “the prophets”;
**Ibn ʿAbbās** (no. 190): “the believers”; **Wakīʿ** (no. 191): “the Muslims”; **ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. Zayd**
(no. 192): “the Prophet ﷺ and those with him.”

**As-Saʿdī.** *The path of those whom You have blessed* — among the prophets, the truthful
(*al-ṣiddīqīn*), the martyrs and the righteous. *Not* the path of *those who incurred wrath* — those who
knew the truth and abandoned it, like the Jews and their like. Nor the path of *those who are astray* —
those who abandoned the truth out of ignorance and misguidance, like the Christians and their like.

**Ibn ʿAbbās (as rendered here).** On "The path of those whom Thou hast favoured": "(the Religion of
those whom You have blessed with the Religion and who are the followers of Moses, before the blessings
of Allah deserted them … It is also said that (the path of those whom Thou hast favoured) refers to the
prophets." On "Not of those who earn Thine anger": "not the religion of the Jews who earned Your anger,
whom You forsook and whose hearts You did not protect until they became complaisant." On "Nor of those
who go astray": "nor the religion of the Christians who erred from Islam."

**Ibn Kathīr.** “*The way of those upon whom You have bestowed Your grace* defines the path”, and those
blessed are “those mentioned in Surat An-Nisāʾ (4:69): *And whoever obeys Allah and the Messenger, then
they will be in the company of those on whom Allah has bestowed His grace, the Prophets, the Siddīqīn, the
martyrs, and the righteous.*” The two wrong paths are then described: “help us to avoid the path of those
whom Allah is angry with, whose intentions are corrupt, who know the truth, yet deviate from it. Also,
help us avoid the path of those who were led astray, who lost the true knowledge and, as a result, are
wandering in misguidance, unable to find the correct path.” The distribution of the two descriptions:
“These two paths are the paths of the Christians and Jews… The path of the believers is knowledge of the
truth and abiding by it. In comparison, the Jews abandoned practicing the religion, while the Christians
lost the true knowledge. This is why ‘anger’ descended upon the Jews, while being described as ‘led
astray’ is more appropriate of the Christians.” And the warning: “both the Christians and the Jews have
earned the anger and are led astray, but the anger is one of the attributes more particular of the Jews.”

*On the omission of the agent:* “On mentioning anger, Allah said, *Not of those who earned Your anger*,
without mentioning the subject, although it is He Who has sent down the anger on them”, with 58:14; and
“Allah relates the misguidance of those who indulged in it, although they were justly misguided according
to Allah's appointed destiny” — 18:17 and 7:186.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “The term signifies the particular way of Faith which equally avoids the two
extremes of excess and deficiency. One who follows the straight path would, in matters of doctrine and
practice both, neither go beyond the limits nor fall short of them.” The four categories of those blessed,
from 4:69: “Among them all, the prophets are the greatest. The *Ṣiddīqīn* (the constantly true) are those
who acquire spiritual perfection, and thus attain the highest rank among the followers of a prophet. In
common parlance, they are called Men of Allah, or saints. The *Shuhadāʾ* (martyrs) are those who sacrifice
even their lives for the sake of their faith (or, who bear witness to the truth, as the word admits of both
meanings). The righteous (*Ṣāliḥīn*) are those who follow the Sharīʿah completely, not only in the matter
of obligations (*wājibāt*) but also with regard to commendable (*mustaḥabb*) actions.” And the two wrong
paths: “Those who have incurred Allah's wrath are the people who, in spite of being quite familiar with the
commandments of Allah, wilfully go against them out of a calculated perversity or in the service of their
desires, or, in other words, who are deficient in obeying divine injunctions. This, for example, was the
general condition of the Jews… As for *those who go astray*, they are the people who, out of ignorance or
lack of thought, go beyond the limits appointed by Allah, and indulge in excess and exaggeration in
religious matters. This, for example, has generally been the error of the Christians who exceeded the
limits in their reverence for a prophet and turned him into a god.” The conclusion: “we do not ask for the
path of those who are the slaves of their desires… nor the path of those who are ignorant or unmindful or
misled, and indulge in excess and exaggeration in religious matters, but wish for a path between these two
extremes.”

**The Study Quran.** “The first part offers clarification regarding those who will be guided upon the
straight path, which is further elaborated in 4:69… (Q, Ṭ, Ṭs). Those who are blessed by God are also said
to be those whom God has purified, so that they are able to attain unto Him (Qu), and those whom He has
blessed by causing them to be mindful of the observance of the Law and its decrees; the latter experience
the realization of spiritual realities, but do not go beyond the limits of knowledge or violate any of the
decrees of the Law (Qu).” The reported interpretations of the two wrong groups: “According to some, *those
who incur wrath* are the idolaters, and *those who are astray* are the hypocrites (Q). Others relate the
terms to two categories of sin in which disbelief incurs wrath and lesser sin results from being astray
(M). Still others relate the terms to those who exhibit various moral shortcomings, such as those who are
heedless and those who are profligate (Ni). In this sense, the two terms together can be understood as a
reference to all the various degrees of those who are spiritually debased and lost and preoccupied with
procuring some share of the fortunes of this world (Qu).” The reading of the final verse: “Read in light of
the many verses that refer to incurring wrath and being astray, the final verse of this sūrah can be
