understood as a prayer (1) to follow the way of those whom God raises in degrees… meaning those who
believe and those who have been given knowledge (58:11) and those who believe and who do not obscure their
belief through wrongdoing (6:82), and (2) to avoid the way of the disbelievers, some of whom are astray and
with some of whom God is wroth.” And: “From one perspective, vv. 6–7 refer to the manner in which all human
beings are moving toward their Lord (Ṭb)… This verse is thus a request to be among those who proceed toward
God with honor and dignity rather than with shame and disgrace.”

### V3 · Context & occasion of revelation (*asbāb al-nuzūl*)

**Al-Baghawī.** (from the *idghām* rules of 1:4 and the discussions of the *hāʾ*/*mīm* here noted) — the
recitation-variants of *ʿalayhim*: "**Ḥamzah read *ʿalayhim*, *ladayhim* and *ilayhim* with *ḍammah* of
their *hāʾ*s**; **Yaʿqūb gives *ḍammah* to every *hāʾ* preceded by a vowelless *yāʾ*, dual and plural,
except *bayna aydīhinna wa-arjulihinna* (60:12)**; **and the others read with *kasrah* of both: whoever gives
the *hāʾ* *ḍammah* returns it to the root, since it is *ḍammah* when isolated; and whoever gives it *kasrah*,
it is because of the vowelless *yāʾ* — and *kasrah* is the sister of the *yāʾ*. And Ibn Kathīr and Abū
Jaʿfar gave full measure (*ishbāʿ*) to every *mīm* of plural in continuous recitation when no vowelless
letter meets it; if a vowelless letter meets it, they do not; Nāfiʿ leaves the choice; and Warsh gives
*ḍammah* at the *alif* of severance; and when it is met by an *alif* of conjunction, and before the *hāʾ*
there is *kasrah* or a vowelless *yāʾ*, **Ḥamzah and al-Kisāʾī give *ḍammah* to the *hāʾ* and the *mīm*,
and Abū ʿAmr gives *kasrah* to them, and Yaʿqūb likewise when what precedes is vowelled *kasrah*; and the
others read with *ḍammah* of the *mīm* and *kasrah* of the *hāʾ* in all of it, for the *yāʾ* or the *kasrah*
before it, and the *mīm*'s *ḍammah* on the root.**"

### V4 · Qurʾān cross-references (*tafsīr al-Qurʾān bi-l-Qurʾān*)

**Al-Qurṭubī.** The identification of the two groups rests on the Book: for "those who incurred wrath" —
*wa-bāʾū bi-ghaḍabin mina llāh* (2:61, 3:112) and *wa-ghaḍiba llāhu ʿalayhim* (48:6); for "those who went
astray" — *qad ḍallū min qablu wa-aḍallū kathīran wa-ḍallū ʿan sawāʾi l-sabīl* (5:77); and for the blessed —
4:69 (above).

**Ibn Kathīr.** 4:69–70 for those blessed; 5:60 for the Jews — *Those who incurred the curse of Allah and
His wrath*; 5:77 for the Christians — *Who went astray before and who misled many, and strayed themselves
from the right path*; 58:14; 18:17; 7:186; and 3:7 for the warning against following what is unclear:
*So as for those in whose hearts there is a deviation, they follow that which is not entirely clear
thereof, seeking Al-Fitnah and seeking for its hidden meanings*.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** 4:69: *Those whom Allah has blessed, namely, the prophets, the Ṣiddīqīn, the
Shuhadāʾ, and the righteous* — the positive identification of the straight path.

**The Study Quran.** 4:69 — *Whosoever obeys God and the Messenger, they are with those whom God has
blessed, the prophets, the truthful ones, the witnesses, and the righteous*; on wrath: 16:106, 42:16, 48:6,
4:93, and with the Children of Israel 2:61; 2:90; 3:112; 5:60; on straying: 3:90, 2:108, 4:136, cf. 4:167,
28:50, and 5:77; on the return: 84:6, 5:18 (cf. 2:285; 3:28; 24:42; 31:14; 35:18; 40:3; 42:15; 50:43;
60:4; 64:3), 67:22, 3:83, 13:15; 26:20 and 93:7 for being astray before guidance; 6:83c and 58:11c; 58:11;
6:82.

### V5 · Prophetic traditions (ḥadīth)

**Ibn Kathīr.** Imam Aḥmad recorded that ʿAdī bin Ḥātim said: “The horsemen of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ
seized my paternal aunt…”, the account of his coming to Islam, ending with the Prophet's words: “*Those
who have earned the anger are the Jews and those who are led astray are the Christians.*” At-Tirmidhī
collected it and graded it Ḥasan Gharīb. And the ḥadīth on those who follow the unclear: “*When you see
those who follow what is not so clear in it, then they are those whom Allah has mentioned; hence, avoid
them.*”

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** On the key to the path being men, not a book only: “According to a ḥadīth, when the
Holy Prophet informed his Companions that, like earlier communities, his Ummah too would be divided into
seventy or seventy-two sects, and that only one among them would be on the right path, they wanted to know
as to which group it would be. The answer he gave also leads on to certain men of Allah, for he said:
‘*That which follows my way and the way of my Companions*’.”

**The Study Quran.** The ḥadīth on Āmīn: “*When the prayer leader says, Not of those who incur wrath, nor
of those who are astray, you must say, Āmīn, for if one's utterance of āmīn coincides with that of the
angels, his past sins will be forgiven* (IK, Q).” And the note that the identification of the two groups
with Jews and Christians rests on “a saying attributed to the Prophet, though not considered to be of the
highest degree of authenticity (IK, JJ, Q, Ṭ, Z)”.

### V6 · Companions & Successors (āthār)

**Al-Baghawī.** The identifications he transmits: **ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb's** reading *ṣirāṭa man anʿamta
ʿalayhim ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim wa-ghayri l-ḍāllīn*; and "*al-maghḍūb ʿalayhim* are **the Jews** and the
astray **the Christians**, **because Allah judged the Jews with wrath — *man laʿanahu llāhu wa-ghaḍiba
ʿalayhi* (5:60) — and judged the Christians with straying — *wa-lā tattabiʿū ahwāʾa qawmin qad ḍallū min
qablu* (5:77)**"; and **Sahl b. ʿAbd Allāh: *ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim* by innovation, and not the astray
from the Sunnah.**

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The transmitted identifications of the two groups:

* ***Al-maghḍūb ʿalayhim*.** ʿAdī b. Ḥātim from the Prophet ﷺ (nos. 193, 194, 195 — three chains, including
  Shuʿbah → Simāk → ʿAbbād b. Ḥubaysh): “*the maghḍūb ʿalayhim* are the Jews”; and the report of the man who
  came to the Prophet ﷺ while he was besieging Wādī al-Qurā (nos. 196–199, four chains through ʿAbd Allāh b.
  Shaqīq): he asked “who are these?” and the Prophet said, “*these are those who incurred wrath — the Jews*.”
  And from the Successors and the early authorities: **Ibn ʿAbbās** (nos. 200, 204), **al-Suddī from Ibn
  ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd and Companions** (no. 201), **Mujāhid** (no. 202), **al-Rabīʿ** (no. 203), **ʿAbd
  al-Raḥmān b. Zayd** (nos. 205, 206): all “the Jews”.
* ***Al-ḍāllīn*.** ʿAdī b. Ḥātim from the Prophet ﷺ (nos. 207, 208, 209): “*al-ḍāllīn* are the Christians”;
  and the Wādī al-Qurā report (nos. 210–213): “*these are the astray — the Christians*.” **Mujāhid** (no.
  214), **Ibn ʿAbbās** (nos. 215, 216), **al-Suddī from Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and Companions** (no. 217),
  **al-Rabīʿ** (no. 218), **ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. Zayd** (nos. 219–220): “the Christians”. Ibn ʿAbbās's longer
  report (no. 215, weak chain): “*and not the path of the Christians whom Allah led astray by their
  fabricating against Him*… *so inspire us with Your true religion, which is* lā ilāha illā llāh *alone,
  with no partner, so that You are not angry with us as You were angry with the Jews, and do not lead us
  astray as You led the Christians astray, punishing us as You punish them; prevent that from us by Your
  gentleness, Your mercy and Your power*.”

**Ibn Kathīr.** “There are several Hadiths and reports from the Salaf on this subject”, including the
account of Zayd bin ʿAmr bin Nufayl before Islam, when “the Jews said to him, ‘You will not become a Jew
unless you carry a share of the anger of Allah that we have earned’”, and “the Christians said to him, ‘If
you become one of us you will carry a share in Allah's discontent’”; he remained in his pure nature,
“neither a Jew nor Christian”, and Waraqah bin Nawfal, one of his companions, believed when the Prophet ﷺ
was sent.

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** The Companion ʿAdī Ibn Ḥātim relates: “when he embraced Islam and presented himself
before the Holy Prophet ﷺ with a cross hanging round his neck, the Holy Prophet ﷺ asked him to remove this
idol. Although at this time the cross did not have the kind of signification for ʿAdī which it has for
Christians, yet he was asked to shun a symbol of ‘association’ externally as well.” The conclusion: “Among
the symbols of ‘association’ are included practices like bowing (*rukūʿ*) or prostrating (*sajdah*) oneself
before anyone except Allah, or going round a person or thing in the prescribed manner of the *ṭawāf* of the
Kaʿbah.”

### V7 · Exegesis of the classical mufassirūn (*aqwāl al-mufassirīn*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "*Ṣirāṭa lladhīna anʿamta ʿalayhim* — that is, **whom You blessed with guidance and
success**. **ʿIkrimah**: whom You blessed with **firmness in faith and uprightness — and they are the
prophets**; and it is said: **they are everyone whom Allah kept firm in faith, of the prophets and the
believers whom Allah mentioned in His word** *fa-ulāʾika maʿa lladhīna anʿama llāhu ʿalayhim mina
l-nabiyyīna* (4:69) **to the end of the verse**. **Ibn ʿAbbās: they are the people of Moses and Jesus before
they changed their religion.** **ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. Zayd: they are the Prophet ﷺ and those with him.** **Abū
al-ʿĀliyah: they are the family of the Messenger ﷺ, Abū Bakr and ʿUmar, and his household.** **Shahr b.
Ḥawshab: they are the companions of the Messenger of Allah and his household.**"

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The thirtieth** — **ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb and Ibn al-Zubayr** recited *ṣirāṭa man anʿamta
ʿalayhim*." And: "**the people differed over those blessed**: the majority of the exegetes said **He meant the
path of the prophets, the truthful, the witnesses and the righteous**, deducing it from His word *wa-man
yuṭiʿi llāha wa-l-rasūla fa-ulāʾika maʿa lladhīna anʿama llāhu ʿalayhim mina l-nabiyyīna wa-l-ṣiddīqīna
wa-l-shuhadāʾi wa-l-ṣāliḥīn* (4:69); **the verse shows that these are on a straight path, and that is what is
sought in the verse of praise — and all that has been said comes back to this, so there is no meaning in
listing the sayings.**"

**Al-Ṭabarī.** On *al-ḍāll*: “every one who swerves from the aim of the road, and travels other than the
upright method, is *ḍāll* with the Arabs, for his losing the face of the road; and for that Allah named the
Christians *ḍullāl*, for their erring from the method of the road in the truth, and their taking religion in
other than the straight way.” And to the question whether that is not also a description of the Jews —
“*Yes*” — he answers: “both groups are astray and those who incurred wrath, but Allah marked each group
with the description by which His servants know it… He named neither of the two except by what is truly its
description, even if it has further descriptions of blame.”

**The Study Quran.** The reported interpretations of the two groups (Q for idolaters/hypocrites; M for the
two categories of sin; Ni for the moral shortcomings; Qu for the summary reading), and the widely held
identification: “those who incur wrath and those who are astray refer to Jews and Christians, respectively
(IK, JJ, Q, Ṭ, Z)”, with the supporting verses 2:61; 2:90; 3:112; 5:60 and 5:77.

### V8 · Language, lexicon & grammar (*lughah · iʿrāb · balāghah*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "**And *ghayr* here is in the sense of *lā*, and *lā* is in the sense of *ghayr*, and
therefore the coordination is permissible** — as one says *fulānun ghayru muḥsinin wa-lā mujmil* (so-and-so
is not a doer of good nor a fair dealer); **but if *ghayr* were in the sense of *siwā*, the coordination
with *lā* would not be permissible**: it is not permissible in speech, *ʿindī siwā ʿAbdillāh wa-lā Zayd*
(I have other than ʿAbdullāh and not Zayd)."

**Al-Jalālayn.** The grammar of the transition from 1:6: “*the path of those whom You have favoured* — with
guidance; *alladhīna* together with its relative clause is substituted by *ghayri l-maghdūbi ʿalayhim*
(‘not the path of those against whom there is wrath’)” — the substitution (*badal*) is what carries the
negation of the two wrong paths, and the commentary draws the meaning: “the guided ones are neither the
Jews nor the Christians.”

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The thirty-fifth** — *lā* in His word *wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*: it was differed over. **It was
said: it is redundant (*zāʾidah*) — said by al-Ṭabarī** (*wa-minhu* His word *mā manaʿaka allā tasjuda*,
7:12). **And it was said: it is an emphasis, entering lest it be imagined that *al-ḍāllīn* is coordinated on
*alladhīna*** (related by Makkī and al-Mahdawī). **And the Kūfans said: *lā* is in the sense of *ghayr*, and
it is the reading of ʿUmar and Ubayy** — as has passed." And: "on *al-ghaḍab*: *ghaḍiba ʿalayhim* is the
sense, and in the language *al-ghaḍab* is **severity** (*al-shiddah*): a man *ghaḍūb*, of severe temper;
*al-ghaḍūb* is the malignant serpent for its severity; *al-ghaḍbah* is a shield of camel hide folded one part
on another, named for its hardness. **And the meaning of *al-ghaḍab* in Allah's attribute is the will of
punishment (*irādat al-ʿuqūbah*), so it is an attribute of the Essence** — and Allah's will is of His
attributes of Essence — **or the punishment itself, and from it the ḥadīth: *charity extinguishes the wrath
of the Lord*, so it is an attribute of act.**" And: "**the thirty-third** — *wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*: *al-ḍalāl* in
the speech of the Arabs is **the going away from the line of the aim and the road of truth** — from it *ḍalla
l-labanu fī l-māʾ*, the milk disappeared in the water; and *a-idhā ḍalalnā fī l-arḍ* (32:10), that is, we
disappeared in death and became dust (the poet: *a-lam tasʾal fa-tukhbiraka l-diyāru / ʿani l-ḥayyi
l-muḍallali ayna sārū*); and *al-ḍuḍalah* is a smooth stone turned over by the water in the valley; and
*al-ghaḍbah* likewise is a rock in the mountain of a colour different from it (*aw ghaḍbatun fī haḍbatin mā
amnaʿā*)."
And "**the thirty-second** — *ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*: they differed over who the
*maghḍūb ʿalayhim* and the *ḍāllīn* are; **the majority: the *maghḍūb ʿalayhim* are the Jews and the
*ḍāllīn* the Christians, and this came explained from the Prophet ﷺ in the ḥadīth of ʿAdī b. Ḥātim and the
story of his Islam** (Abū Dāwūd al-Ṭayālisī, Tirmidhī)" — and the Qurʾānic witness for it (V4 above). "And
it was said: *al-maghḍūb ʿalayhim* the idolaters and *al-ḍāllīn* the hypocrites; and it was said: *al-maghḍūb
ʿalayhim* is the one who drops the obligation of this sūrah in prayer, and *al-ḍāllīn* from its blessing —
related by al-Sulamī in *Ḥaqāʾiq* and al-Māwardī in his *Tafsīr*, **and it is nothing**; **al-Māwardī said:
this face is rejected, because what the reports contradict and the *āthār* oppose and disagreement spreads
over, this ruling may not be applied to it.** And it was said: *al-maghḍūb ʿalayhim* by following innovations
and *al-ḍāllīn* from the ways of guidance. **I say: this is good, but the Prophet's ﷺ explanation is higher
and better.**"

**Al-Ṭabarī.** Three grammar-and-meaning matters:

* **The elided blessing.** To the question what blessing is meant, he answers from the ellipsis principle
  established earlier (with al-Nābighah: *ka-annaka min jimāli Banī Uqayshin / yuqaʿqiʿu khalfa rijlayhi
  bi-shann* — “a camel” dropped; and al-Farazdaq: *tarā arbāqahum mutaqallidīhā* — “them” dropped): the
  blessing meant is the upright method and the straight path itself, made clear from the preceding words.
* ***Ghayr* and the two ways of *khafḍ*.** “The reciters are agreed on reading *ghayr* with *jarr* of the
  *rāʾ*; and the *khafḍ* comes to it from two directions: either *ghayr* is a description and attribute of
  *alladhīna*, so it takes the *khafḍ* that *alladhīna* has; or *alladhīna* is taken as definite and *ghayr*
  is put in the genitive by the intention of repeating *al-ṣirāṭ* — so that you say: *the path of those You
  blessed, the path of other than those who incurred wrath*.” On the first: “it is permitted for *ghayr* to
  be an attribute of *alladhīna* though *alladhīna* is definite and *ghayr* indefinite, because *alladhīna*
  with its relative clause is not a delimited definite like Zayd and ʿAmr, but is like the unknown
  indefinites” — cf. *lā ajlisu illā ilā l-ʿālimi ghayri l-jāhili*.
* **The forbidden *naṣb* and the dispute over it.** “It is permissible to put *ghayr* in the accusative…
  though I dislike reciting by it, for its departure from the recitation of the reciters; and what departs
  from the readings on which the community has come by a clear, widespread transmission is an opinion
  contrary to the truth.” He gives the face of the accusative reading (as a description of the *hāʾ* and
  *mīm* in *ʿalayhim*, which is in the position of the accusative by *anʿamta*): *the path of those You
  guided, in blessing from You upon them, not those who incurred wrath*. Then the grammarians' dispute: the
  Baṣran took the accusative as **exception** (*istithnāʾ*) — “*except those who incurred wrath*, who were
  not blessed in their religions and not guided to the truth; so do not make us of them” — and the Kūfans
  rejected this: “had it been as the Baṣran claimed, it would be an error to say *wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*; for *lā*
  is a negation and denial, and nothing is coordinated with a denial but a denial. We have not found in any
  of the speech of the Arabs an exception coordinated with it by a denial; they coordinate an exception with
  an exception, and a denial with a denial; but *the people stood except your father and not your brother*
  we have not found.” Hence “*ghayr* is in the sense of **negation**, not exception, and the one who made it
  exception is in error.”

**The Study Quran.** “*Not of* renders the exceptive particle *ghayr*, which is understood as being
attached to *the path*. It could also be read as being attached to *them*, in which case it would mean,
‘The path of those whom Thou hast blessed, not those who incur wrath’ (Ṭ, Ṭs). Nonetheless, both readings
yield the same essential meaning (Ṭ).” And the grammar of the three categories: “*Those whom Thou hast
blessed* conveys an action performed by God in the past tense, thus giving a sense of finality and
certitude… *Those who incur wrath* does not name an active agent or say definitively that the outcome has
occurred, only that they are subject to wrath. This indicates that although they have already acted in a
manner that may warrant Divine Punishment and Retribution, God's Wrath has not yet come upon them and the
door of repentance remains open, since *God guides to Himself whosoever turns in repentance* (13:27).”

### V9 · Variant readings (*qirāʾāt*)

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The thirty-fourth** — **ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb and Ubayy b. Kaʿb read *ghayri l-maghḍūbi
ʿalayhim wa-ghayri l-ḍāllīn***, and from them the *naṣb* and the *khafḍ* are related **in both words**:
**the *khafḍ* as an apposition to *alladhīna*, or to the *hāʾ* and *mīm* in *ʿalayhim*, or a description of
*alladhīna*** — "and *alladhīna* is definite, and the definite is not described by the indefinite nor the
indefinite by the definite, except that *alladhīna* is not meant as a particular — it is general, so the
speech is as though you said *I pass by the like of you and honour him*; or because *ghayr* has become
definite by its being between two things with no middle between them, as you say *the living is other than
the dead*, *the still is other than the moving*, *the standing is other than the sitting* — two sayings, the
first for al-Fārisī and the second for al-Zamakhsharī." And "**the *naṣb* in the *rāʾ* is on two faces: the
*ḥāl* from *alladhīna*, or from the *hāʾ* and *mīm* in *ʿalayhim* — as though you said *You blessed them, not
wrath upon them*; or on the *istithnāʾ*, as though you said *except those who incurred wrath*; and the *naṣb*
by *aʿnī* is permissible, related from al-Khalīl.**" And "**the thirty-sixth** — the root of *al-ḍāllīn* is
*al-ḍāllilīn*: the vowel of the first *lām* was dropped, then the *lām* was merged into the *lām*, and two
vowelless letters met — the *alif*'s prolongation and the merged *lām*; and **Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī read *wa-lā
l-ḍaʾlīn* with an unprolonged *hamzah***, as though he fled from the meeting of the two vowelless ones — and
it is a dialect. Abū Zayd related: I heard **ʿAmr b. ʿUbayd** recite *fa-yawmaʾidhin lā yusʾalu ʿan dhanbihi
insun wa-lā jāʾinn* (55:39) and I thought he had erred, until I heard from the Arabs *daʾabbah* and
*shaʾabbah*."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The unanimity on *jarr* and the rejected *naṣb*, above; and, at the end of the verse, the
Baṣran claim on *lā* with which the Kūfans deal: “some of the Baṣrans claimed that the *lā* with *al-ḍāllīn*
was brought in to complete the speech and its meaning is void”, adducing al-ʿAjjāj, *fī biʾri lāḥūrin sarā
wa-mā shaʿar* (“in a pit of perdition he travelled and did not perceive”), and Abū al-Najm, *fa-mā alūmu
l-bīḍa an lā taskharā*, and al-Aḥwas, *wa-yalḥaynanī fī l-lahwi an lā uḥibbah*, and 7:12, *mā manaʿaka allā
tasjuda* — reading *an lā* as the redundant *lā*. Al-Ṭabarī sides with the Kūfans: “*the second saying is
worthier of correctness than the first*, since there is not found in the speech of the Arabs a beginning of
speech with *lā* in the sense of omission with no negation before it; nor is it permitted to coordinate by
it upon *siwā* or upon the particle of exception.” And the conclusion: “*ghayr* in the speech of the Arabs
has three senses: exception, negation, and *siwā*; and when it is established that *lā* cannot be void and
cannot be coordinated on *illā* or on *siwā*, and it is found coordinated by the *wāw* — then it is
established that *ghayr* here can only be in the sense of **negation and denial**, and *wa-lā l-ḍāllīn* can
only be coordinated on *ghayr al-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim*.” The resulting sense of the verse he gives as: “*Guide
us to the straight path, the path of those You blessed, not those who incurred wrath and not those who are
astray*.”

### V11 · Creed & theology (*ʿaqīdah*)

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The thirty-first** — **in this verse is a refutation of the *Qadariyyah*, the
*Muʿtazilah* and the *Imāmiyyah*, because they hold that the will of man is sufficient for the issuing of his
acts from him — be it obedience or disobedience — since with them man is the creator of his acts, so he has
no need, in their issuing from him, of his Lord. And Allah has declared them liars in this verse, since they
asked Him for guidance to the straight path: were the affair theirs and the choice in their hands without
their Lord, they would not have asked Him for guidance, nor repeated the request in every prayer** — and
likewise their humbling themselves to Him in the warding off of the disliked, which is what contradicts
guidance, when they said *ṣirāṭa lladhīna anʿamta ʿalayhim ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*: as they
asked Him to guide them, they asked Him not to lead them astray; and likewise they supplicate, saying *rabbanā
lā tuzigh qulūbanā baʿda idh hadaytanā* (3:8)."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The doctrinal point he draws from the verse itself: “in this verse there is a clear proof that
the obedience of Allah is not attained by the obedient except by Allah's blessing it upon them and His
granting them success in it. Does he not hear Him say *the path of those You blessed* — attributing all
that was theirs of guidance, obedience and worship to its being a blessing from Him upon them?” — a
rejoinder to *al-Qadariyyah*, continued at the end of the sūrah.

**Al-Ṭabarī.** Three creedal matters:

* **The identification of the two groups rests on the *āthār***: his first proof for the *maghḍūb ʿalayhim*
  is 5:60, *man laʿanahu llāhu wa-ghaḍiba ʿalayhi wa-jaʿala minhumu l-qiradata wa-l-khanāzīra wa-ʿabada
  l-ṭāghūt*, and for the *ḍāllīn* 5:77, *qad ḍallū min qablu wa-aḍallū kathīran wa-ḍallū ʿan sawāʾi
  l-sabīl*.
* **The attribute of wrath.** “The people differed over the description of Allah's wrath: some said Allah's
  wrath on those of His creation with whom He is angry is His bringing down His punishment upon them, in
  this world or the next — as in 43:55, *fa-lammā āsafūnā ntaqamnā minhum*; some said it is His blame of
  them and of their acts, and His reviling them in speech; and some said the wrath from Him is a meaning
  understood, like what is known of the meanings of wrath, but — though it is so in affirmation — its
  meaning differs from the wrath of human beings which agitates and moves and harms them, because Allah's
  essence is not subject to defects; rather it is an attribute of His, as knowledge is an attribute of His
  and power is an attribute of His, **affirmed** as it is understood, though its meaning differs from the
  meanings of the knowledge of servants and their powers.”
* **Guidance and straying are from Allah.** Against those of the *Qadariyyah* who argued from the fact that
  straying is attributed to the Christians and not to Allah (*al-ḍāllīn*, not *al-muḍallīn*): “had it been as
  the dull one supposed, it would be an error to say ‘the tree moved’ when the winds moved it, and ‘the
  earth shook’ when the earthquake moved it.” And: “the Qurʾān came down in the tongue of the Arabs… it is
  their habit to attribute the act to the one it was found from, even if its cause is other than the one it
  was found from, and sometimes to its cause, even if the one from whom the act was found is other than it” —
  the proof being 10:22, *wa-jarayna bihim bi-rīḥin ṭayyibah*, the sailing attributed to the ships though
  the wind drives them; and 45:23, *a-fa-raʾayta mani ttakhadha ilāhahu hawāhu wa-aḍallahū llāhu ʿalā ʿilmin
  wa-khatama ʿalā samʿihi wa-qalbihi*, “declaring that He is the One who leads astray and guides, apart from
  any other.”

**Ibn Kathīr.** From the closing argument of the sūrah: “These and several other Ayat testify to the fact
that Allah alone is the One Who guides and misguides, contrary to the belief of the Qadariyyah sect, who
claimed that the servants choose and create their own destiny. They rely on some unclear Ayat avoiding what
is clear and contradicts their desires… Verily, no innovator in the religion could ever rely on any
authentic evidence in the Qur'an that testifies to his innovation. The Qur'an came to distinguish between
truth and falsehood, and guidance and misguidance.”

**The Study Quran.** “The absence of an active agent in this phrase also reflects the general Quranic
principle that God does not wrong human beings in the least, but rather human beings wrong themselves
(10:44)”; and the varieties of straying: “Being astray can result from something intentional or something
done by mistake (Aj). Thus those who are astray may not have committed actions that merit Divine
Retribution, as is made clear when Moses says that he was one of those who are astray (26:20) before the
beginning of his mission… and when God… asks the Prophet Muhammad, *Did He not… find thee astray and
guide?* (93:7). In this sense, being astray describes all those who do not actively live in accord with
Revelation.”

### V13 · History, sīrah & parables (*qiṣaṣ · sīrah*)

**Ibn ʿAbbās (as rendered here).** Within the report on "those whom Thou hast favoured" stands a
wilderness narrative: the blessing of the followers of Moses is described as the time when
"*He shaded them with a white cloud and sent down on them honey and quails when they were in the
wilderness*".

**Ibn Kathīr.** The ʿAdī bin Ḥātim account (see V5) is a sīrah narrative: his aunt's capture, her release,
ʿAdī's flight and return, his observation “that he was not a king like Kisra or Caesar”, and his Islam at
the Prophet's words. Also the pre-Islamic journey of Zayd bin ʿAmr bin Nufayl to Syria in search of the
true religion (see V6).

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** The historical characterisation of the two communities: “the rebelliousness of the
Jews who not only refused to listen to the prophets of Allah but went on to kill them; on the other hand,
there is the excessive zeal of the Christians who deified a prophet.”

**The Study Quran.** The historical identification of the groups — the Wrath upon the Children of Israel
and upon the Jews (2:61; 2:90; 3:112; 5:60) and the Christians read with 5:77 — and the symbolic reading:
“When the straight path is understood as the vertical path of ascent toward God, those who incur wrath can
be understood as a reference to those upon a path of descent away from God, while those who are astray
meander horizontally away from the path that leads toward the Transcendent. These three possibilities then
correspond to the three dimensions of space and symbolize all the possibilities of the human state.”

### V14 · Spiritual purification & etiquette (*tazkiyah · ādāb*)

**Al-Jalālayn.** The file closes with the author's customary sign-off, quoted as it stands: “But God knows
best what is right and to Him is the Return and the final Resort. May God bless our master Muhammad (s),
his Family and Companions, and grant them everlasting peace. Sufficient is God for us; an excellent
Guardian is He. There is no power and no strength save in God, the High, the Tremendous.” *(A closing
formula rather than an element of the verse; recorded here for completeness.)*



**End of source record — Tafsīr al-Jalālayn, chapter 1.**

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "The thirty-second" above carries the author's *ādāb* ruling on the reported view that
identifies these two groups with those who neglect the sūrah: **"it is nothing"**, and al-Māwardī: "this
face is rejected, because what reports contradict, the ruling may not be applied to it" — and al-Qurṭubī:
"this is good, but the Prophet's explanation is better."

**Al-Ṭabarī.** Under the report of Ibn ʿAbbās (no. 215, weak chain), the verse is read as a servant's
petition formed by the two examples: “*so that You are not angry with us as You were angry with the Jews,
and do not lead us astray as You led the Christians astray… prevent that from us by Your gentleness, Your
mercy and Your power*.”

### V15 · Wisdom, counsel & admonition (*ḥikam · tadabbur*)

**As-Saʿdī.** The two closing clauses name the two diseases of the religious life: the one who knows
and abandons is the object of wrath, and the one who abandons out of ignorance and misguidance is the
one astray. Deliverance lies in knowledge joined to action, which is the straight path of 1:6 itself.



**End of source record — tafsir as-Saʿdī, chapter 1.**

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** Under “The Key to the Straight Path”: the Qurʾān “has thus indicated that if one
wishes to follow the straight path, one should seek such and such men… and adopt their way. Here, the Holy
Qurʾān does not ask us to follow the ‘path of the Qurʾān’, for a book alone is not sufficient for the
grooming of man; nor does it ask us to follow ‘the path of the prophet’, for the Holy Prophet was not to be
in this world for ever… In defining the straight path too, He has also enumerated those of His servants who
find special favour with Him — all of which argues that, in trying to understand the Book of Allah and to
act upon it, one cannot solely rely on one's own study and judgment, but must turn to someone who knows.”
And the balance: “Two things are necessary for the physical and spiritual well-being and success of man —
the Book of Allah which contains guidance for every sphere of human life, and the Men of Allah who help in
making this guidance effective.” The couplet of Akbar is quoted: “Courses teach words. But men train men.”

**The Study Quran.** The closing request “to be among those who proceed toward God with honor and dignity
rather than with shame and disgrace”; and the reading of vv. 6–7 as the movement of all human beings toward
their Lord: “*O mankind! Truly thou art laboring unto thy Lord laboriously, and shall meet Him!* (84:6)”.

### V16 · Contemporary issues & practical application

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** Two modern errors are addressed. On self-study: “It is common observation that many
people today, though otherwise educated, cherish the erroneous notion that one can acquire a masterly
knowledge of the Holy Qur'an and Ḥadīth merely by reading a translation or at best a commentary. But the
error of such an enterprise is self-evident. Had a book in itself been sufficient for the guidance of men,
there was no need for the prophets to be sent.” On *wasīlah*: “one finds that in this matter people
generally adopt either of the two extreme positions, outright rejection of *wasīlah* or exaggerated
veneration. The truth, however, lies between the two.” And on sectarian division: “there are two kinds of
deviations… Some people elected to follow the Book of Allah alone, ignored the Men of Allah totally and
gave no value to their teachings and explanations. Conversely, others adopted the Men of Allah as the only
criterion of truth and became indifferent to the Book of Allah. Both these ways lead to fatal error.”

### V17 · Specific virtues & supplications (*faḍāʾil · duʿāʾ*)

**Al-Baghawī.** "**It is *sunnah* for the reciter to say when he finishes the recitation of the Fātiḥah
*Āmīn*, with a pause separating it from the Fātiḥah; it is lightened, and the grammarians allow it
lengthened and shortened; and its meaning is *O Allah, hear and answer* (*allāhumma smaʿ wa-stajib*).
**Ibn ʿAbbās and Qatādah**: its meaning is **so let it be** (*kadhālika yakūn*). **Mujāhid**: **it is one of
Allah's names**. And it is said: **it is the stamp of supplication**; and it is said: **it is Allah's seal
upon His servants, by which He repels the afflictions from them, like the seal of the book that protects it
from corruption and the revealing of what is in it.**"
And the ḥadīth with its chain: "Abū ʿAlī al-Ḥusayn b. Muḥammad al-Qāḍī and Abū Ḥāmid Aḥmad b. ʿAbd Allāh
al-Ṣāliḥī → Abū Bakr Aḥmad b. Ḥasan al-Ḥīrī → Abū ʿAlī Muḥammad b. Aḥmad al-Maydānī → Muḥammad b. Yaḥyā →
ʿAbd al-Razzāq → Maʿmar → al-Zuhrī → **Ibn al-Musayyab** → **Abū Hurayrah**: the Prophet ﷺ said: **When the
imam says *ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*, say *Āmīn* — for the angels say *Āmīn* and the imam
says *Āmīn*, and whoever's *Āmīn* agrees with the *Āmīn* of the angels, his previous sins are forgiven.**
_(ṣaḥīḥ.)_"



**End of source record — al-Baghawī, chapter 1.**

**Al-Qurṭubī.** "**The first** — it is *sunnah* for the reciter of the Qurʾān, after finishing the Fātiḥah,
after **a pause on the *nūn* of *wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*, to say *Āmīn* — so that what is Qurʾān be distinguished
from what is not.** **The second** — it is established in the *Ummahāt* from the ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah that
the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: **When the imam says *Āmīn*, say *Āmīn* — for whoever's *Āmīn* agrees with the
*Āmīn* of the angels, his previous sins are forgiven**" — "our scholars said: forgiveness is arranged upon
four premisses contained in this ḥadīth: the imam's *Āmīn*; the *Āmīn* of those behind him; the *Āmīn* of the
angels; and the agreement of the *Āmīn* — it is said in the answer, and it is said in the time, and it is
said in the quality, of sincere supplication, by his saying ﷺ: *Call upon Allah while certain of the answer,
and know that Allah does not answer a supplication from a heedless, inattentive heart*."
"**The third** — Abū Dāwūd from **Abū Muṣabbiḥ al-Muqrāʾī**: 'we used to sit with **Abū Zuhayr al-Numayrī**,
a Companion, and he would tell the best of ḥadīth; and when one of us supplicated he said: *Seal it with
*Āmīn*, for *Āmīn* is like the seal on the page.*' Abū Zuhayr said: 'Shall I not tell you of that? We went
out with the Messenger of Allah ﷺ one night and came upon a man who was importunate in asking, and the
Prophet ﷺ stood listening to him and said: ***He has made it binding, if he seals***. A man of the people
said: *With what should he seal?* He said: ***With*** *Āmīn* — for if he seals with* Āmīn, *he has made it
binding***.'… And **Wahb b. Munabbih**: '*Āmīn* is four letters; Allah creates from every letter an angel
saying: *O Allah, forgive the one who says* Āmīn*.* And in the report: '*Jibrīl dictated to me* Āmīn *at the
end of Fātiḥat al-Kitāb and said: it is like the seal on the book*'; and in another ḥadīth: ***Āmīn* is the
seal of the Lord of the worlds**'; and another: ***Āmīn is a degree in the Garden***; the meaning: a letter by
which its sayer earns a degree in the Garden (Abū Bakr al-Harawī's explanation: 'it is Allah's stamp on His
servants, since by it He repels from them the afflictions and calamities, so it is like the seal of the book
that preserves it')."
"**The fourth** — **the meaning of *Āmīn* with most of the people of knowledge is: *O Allah, answer us*
(*allāhumma stajib lanā*)**, put in the place of supplication; and some said it is one of Allah's names —
related from Jaʿfar b. Muḥammad, Mujāhid and Hilāl b. Yasāf, and Ibn ʿAbbās related it from the Prophet ﷺ
**and it is not established** (Ibn al-ʿArabī); and it was said its meaning is *thus let it be* (al-Jawharī);
and al-Kalbī from Abū Ṣāliḥ from Ibn ʿAbbās: "I asked the Messenger of Allah ﷺ what *Āmīn* means, and he
said: ***O Lord, do***"; and Muqātil: "it is a strength for supplication and a drawing down of blessing";
and Tirmidhī: "its meaning is **do not disappoint our hope**."
"**The fifth** — *Āmīn* has two dialects: the lengthened (*madd*), on the pattern *fāʿīl*, like *yāsīn*; and
the shortened, on the pattern *yamīn*" — with the poets: *yā rabbi lā taslubnī ḥubbahā abadan / wa-yarḥamu
llāhu ʿabdan qāla āmīnā* and *āmīna āmīna lā arḍā bi-wāḥidatin / ḥattā uballighahā alfayni āmīnā*; and the
short: *tabāʿada minnī Fuṭḥulu idh saʾaltuhu / amīna fa-zāda llāhu mā baynanā buʿdā*; "**and *shaddah* of
the *mīm* is an error** (al-Jawharī) — though it is related from al-Ḥasan and Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq, and it is the
saying of al-Ḥusayn b. al-Faḍl, from *amma* when he aimed — *we are aimers toward You* — as in *wa-lā
āmmīna l-bayta l-ḥarām* (5:2); and al-Jawharī: it is built on the *fatḥah*, like *ayna* and *kayfa*."
"**The sixth** — the scholars differed whether the imam says it and whether he says it aloud. **Al-Shāfiʿī,
and Mālik in the report of the Madinans, held he says it aloud**; **the Kūfans and some of the Madinans: he
does not say it aloud — the saying of al-Ṭabarī, and Ibn Ḥabīb of our scholars; Ibn Bukayr: it is his
choice**; and Ibn al-Qāsim related from **Mālik that the imam does not say *Āmīn*, but those behind him say
it** — the saying of Ibn al-Qāsim and the Egyptians of Mālik's companions, whose proof is the ḥadīth of **Abū
Mūsā al-Ashʿarī**: "the Messenger of Allah ﷺ addressed us and explained our Sunnah and taught us our prayer,
saying: *When you pray, straighten your rows, then let one of you lead you; when he says* ghayri
l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim wa-lā l-ḍāllīn, *say* Āmīn, *and Allah will answer you*" (Muslim), and the like from
Sumayy from Abū Hurayrah (Mālik). **And the sound is the first, by the ḥadīth of Wāʾil b. Ḥujr**: "the
Messenger of Allah ﷺ, when he recited *wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*, said *Āmīn*, **raising his voice with it** (Abū
Dāwūd, Dāraqutnī — and Abū Bakr said: 'this is a *sunnah* with which the people of Kūfah were alone; this is
sound'). And al-Bukhārī headed a chapter: *the imam's saying* Āmīn *aloud*. And ʿAṭāʾ said: '*Āmīn* is a
supplication; Ibn al-Zubayr would say *Āmīn* and those behind him until the mosque surged with it.' And
Tirmidhī: more than one of the people of knowledge among the Companions and those after them held that a man
raises his voice with *Āmīn*, not concealing it — and al-Shāfiʿī, Aḥmad and Isḥāq say it. And in the *Muwaṭṭaʾ*
and the two *Ṣaḥīḥs*, Ibn Shihāb: 'the Messenger of Allah ﷺ used to say *Āmīn*.' And in the *Sunan* of Ibn
Mājah from Abū Hurayrah: '*The people have abandoned* Āmīn — and the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, when he said
*ghayri l-maghḍūbi ʿalayhim wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*, said *Āmīn* until the people of the first row heard it and the
mosque echoed with it*.' And the ḥadīth of Abū Mūsā and Sumayy: their meaning is to inform of the place where
*Āmīn* is said, namely when the imam says *wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*, so that the two sayings be together and they not
precede it with *Āmīn*, because of his saying ﷺ: *When the imam says* Āmīn, *say* Āmīn." And Ibn Nāfiʿ in
Ibn al-Ḥārith's book: "the follower does not say it unless he hears the imam say *wa-lā l-ḍāllīn*; and if he
is far off and does not hear him, he does not say it"; Ibn ʿAbdūs: "he estimates the measure of the recitation
and says *Āmīn*."
"**The seventh** — the companions of **Abū Ḥanīfah** said: **concealing *Āmīn* is better than saying it
aloud**, because it is a supplication and Allah said *udʿū rabbakum taḍarruʿan wa-khufyatan* (7:55); and
their proof: what is related in the interpretation of His word *qad ujībat daʿwatukumā* (10:89) — "Mūsā used
to supplicate and Hārūn would say *Āmīn*, and Allah named them both suppliants." **The answer**: "concealing
supplication was better only because of what enters it of ostentation; but what pertains to the congregational
prayer is the witnessing of an outward rite, and the manifesting of a truth to which the servants are called
— and the imam was called to make manifest the recitation of the Fātiḥah, which comprises supplication and
*Āmīn* at its end; so when the supplication is of what *sunnah* has aloud, the *Āmīn* upon the supplication
follows it and runs its course — and this is clear."
"**The eighth** — **the word *Āmīn* was not before us except for Mūsā and Hārūn** (Tirmidhī al-Ḥakīm in
*Nawādir al-Uṣūl* from Anas: the Prophet ﷺ said: *Allah has given my community three things not given to
anyone before them: the* salām — *which is the greeting of the people of the Garden; the rows of the angels;
and* Āmīn — *except what was from Mūsā and Hārūn*), its meaning: **Mūsā supplicated against Pharaoh and
Hārūn said *Āmīn*, and Allah said *qad ujībat daʿwatukumā*, and did not mention Hārūn's speech; and Mūsā
said *rabbanā*, and the *Āmīn* was from Hārūn, so He named him a suppliant in His revelation, since He made
that from him a supplication**." And: "it has been said that *Āmīn* is peculiar to this community, by what is
related from the Prophet ﷺ: *I have not envied you the Jews for anything as I envied you for* Āmīn" (Ibn
Mājah from Ḥammād b. Salamah from Suhayl b. Abī Ṣāliḥ from his father from ʿĀʾishah; and from Ibn ʿAbbās:
"*The Jews envied you for nothing as they envied you for* Āmīn, *so say* Āmīn *much*") — "**our scholars
said: the people of the Book envied us only because its beginning is praise of Allah and extolling Him, then
submission to Him and humbling before Him, then a supplication for us for guidance to the straight path,
then a supplication against them, with our saying *Āmīn*.**"



**End of source record — al-Qurṭubī, chapter 1.**

**Al-Ṭabarī.** The sūrah closes with the ḥadīth of the division of the prayer, transmitted from Abū Hurayrah
in three chains (nos. 221, 222, 223, one of them *mawqūf*), and from Jābir b. ʿAbd Allāh (no. 224): the
Prophet ﷺ said: “*Allah, mighty and exalted, said: I have divided the prayer between Me and My servant into
two halves, and My servant shall have what he asks. When the servant says* al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi
l-ʿālamīn*, Allah says: My servant has praised Me. When he says* al-Raḥmāni l-Raḥīm*, He says: My servant
has extolled Me. When he says* māliki yawmi l-dīn*, He says: My servant has magnified Me — this is Mine, and
what remains.*” (In the Jābir report the words “My servant has praised Me / has extolled Me / has magnified
Me” are given for the first three clauses, and the last portion is “this is Mine”.) Al-Ṭabarī appends:
“The end of the explanation of the sūrah Fātiḥat al-Kitāb.”

**Ibn ʿAbbās (as rendered here).** On *Āmīn*, with which the sūrah closes, three reports are given:
"thus shall be its custodians; it is also said that Amen means let it be so. It is also said that it
means: O our Lord! Do with us as we have requested You. And Allah knows best."



**End of source record — Tafsīr Ibn ʿAbbās, chapter 1.**

**Ibn Kathīr.** “Saying Āmīn”: “It is recommended to say Āmīn after finishing the recitation of
Al-Fatihah. Āmīn means, ‘O Allah! Accept our invocation.’” The evidence: Aḥmad, Abū Dāwūd and at-Tirmidhī
recorded from Wāʾil bin Ḥujr, “I heard the Messenger of Allah ﷺ recite, *Not of those who earned Your
anger, nor of those who went astray*, and he said ‘Āmīn’ extending it with his voice”; Abū Hurayrah
narrated that the Prophet ﷺ would say Āmīn “until those who were behind him in the first line could hear
him”; Bilāl said, “O Messenger of Allah! Do not finish saying Āmīn before I can join you.” The ruling:
“Saying Āmīn is recommended for those who are not praying and is strongly recommended for those who are
praying, whether alone or behind the Imam”, with the ḥadīth: “*When the Imam says ‘Āmīn’, then say ‘Āmīn’,
because whoever says ‘Āmīn’ with the angels, his previous sins will be forgiven.*” Its meaning: “At-Tirmidhī
said that ‘Āmīn’ means, ‘Do not disappoint our hope’, while the majority of scholars said that it means,
‘Answer our invocation.’” And the merit: “*They will not envy us for anything more than they envy us for
Friday which we have been guided to, while they were led astray from it, and for the Qiblah…, and for our
saying ‘Āmīn’ behind the Imam.*”



**End of source record — Tafsīr Ibn Kathīr, chapter 1.**

**Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.** “As we have said before, the prayer which the Holy Qur'an has chosen to recommend to
everyone, in every situation and for everything one does, is the prayer for being guided in the straight
path. Just as success in the Hereafter depends on taking the straight path which leads one to Paradise, in
the same way… success in all worldly concerns too depends on keeping to the straight path… In view of the
need for the straight path in worldly and other-worldly concerns both, this is the prayer which should
constantly be on the lips and in the heart of a true Muslim — never as an empty verbal exercise, but with a
sincere intention and with the meaning of the words fully present in the mind.” The reflection on man's
natural praise of Allah is added here: “If man only considers his own being, he would find that it is a
microcosm which contains in analogical form everything contained in the macrocosm”, with 76:28 — *It is We
Who created them, and it is We Who endowed their joints with strength* — and 14:34 and 16:18 on the
countless blessings.



**End of source record — Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, chapter 1.**

**The Study Quran.** On Āmīn: “Upon the completion of the Fātiḥah, it is thus customary among Sunni
Muslims to say *Āmīn* after a brief moment of silence, which serves to distinguish this utterance from the
Quran itself (Q). It is not considered obligatory, but is reported to have been the custom of the Prophet
and is strongly recommended by Sunni scholars (IK). Some advocate saying it aloud, while others recommend
saying it under one's breath (Q). Most understand *āmīn* to mean, ‘O God! Answer us,’ which functions as a
supplication (IK, Q). Others say that it is one of the Names of God (Q), while others say it means, ‘Thus
shall it be’ (Q). This last meaning is closest to the meaning of the Hebrew cognate ‘Amen.’ In this sense,
it is a declaration of affirmation and is understood as the seal upon one's prayers (Q).”



**End of source record — The Study Quran, chapter 1.**


---

## Appendix · the ten sources and their structural notes (verbatim)

### `tafsir-al-alusi.md`

**Source work:** *Rūḥ al-Maʿānī fī Tafsīr al-Qurʾān al-ʿAẓīm* — Shihāb al-Dīn Maḥmūd al-Ālūsī (d. 1270/1854).

**Corpus file:** `tafsir-al-alusi/001.txt` (Arabic).

**Elements found:** S1, S2, S3, S4, S5, S6 · V1, V2, V4, V5, V6, V7, V8, V10, V11, V13, V14, V15, V16, V17.

> **Structural note (applies to this file only).** This corpus file has a **defect with a twist**, and it costs
> verse-level coverage. The seven `## 1:n` sections are not seven verse-commentaries:
>
> * `## 1:1` carries one body (≈63.9 k chars) = the printed edition's **pages 39–53**, al-Alūsī's ʿilmī section
>   on the *basmalah*;
> * `## 1:2`–`## 1:7` each carry **one and the same** body (≈22.8 k chars) = the edition's **pages 34–38**,
>   the sūrah introduction.
>
> So the file's unique content is pages 34–53 twice over: the sūrah-level introduction plus the basmalah
> discussion. **Al-Alūsī's verse-by-verse commentary on 1:2–1:7 would follow page 53 and is not present in the
> corpus file at all.** Verses 1:2–1:7 are therefore marked `verse-specific: none` below, and their content is
> recorded only where al-Alūsī treats it inside the sūrah-level material (S5/S6). The corpus itself signals the
> loss with the editor's note at the end of page 53: *"the author's speech was abridged on account of its great
> length."*

### `tafsir-al-baghawi.md`

**Source work:** *Maʿālim al-Tanzīl* — Abū Muḥammad al-Ḥusayn b. Masʿūd al-Baghawī (d. 516/1122).

**Corpus file:** `tafsir-al-baghawi/001.txt` (Arabic).

**Elements found:** S1, S2, S3, S4, S5 · V1, V2, V3, V4, V6, V7, V8, V9, V11, V13, V14, V15, V17.

> **Structural note (applies to this file only).** This corpus file has a **defect**: the same body of
> commentary (≈28.7 KB, al-Baghawī's whole chapter 1) is repeated verbatim under each of the seven
> `## 1:n` headings. The record below follows the **internal order of that single body** — the sūrah
> introduction first, then the *basmalah*, then 1:2 to 1:7 with the *Āmīn* and the faḍāʾil — and files each
> part under its own verse; the verse headings in the corpus carry no verse-specific material at all.

### `tafsir-al-jalalayn.md`

**Source work:** *Tafsīr al-Jalālayn* — Jalāl al-Dīn al-Maḥallī (d. 864/1459) and Jalāl al-Dīn al-Suyūṭī (d. 911/1505), in the English rendering of the corpus file.

**Corpus file:** `tafsir-al-jalalayn/001.txt` (English).

**Elements found:** V2, V4, V5, V8, V9, V11 (cross-head), V14 (cross-head).

> **Structural note (applies to this file only).** In the corpus file the commentary on the basmalah is
> filed under `## 1:2`, while the basmalah itself stands alone under `## 1:1`; the basmalah commentary has
> therefore been re-mapped to **1:1**. `## 1:2` consequently holds no material of its own, and 1:2 (the
> verse *al-ḥamd*) is not commented on in this file. The substitution note that begins at the end of
> `## 1:6` (“This is substituted by …”) is completed in `## 1:7` and is recorded here at 1:7, V8, with a
> cross-reference from 1:6.

### `tafsir-al-qurtubi.md`

**Source work:** *al-Jāmiʿ li-aḥkām al-Qurʾān* — Abū ʿAbd Allāh Muḥammad b. Aḥmad al-Qurṭubī (d. 671/1273); in the corpus file the work is divided as the author arranges it — twenty-eight *masāʾil* on the basmalah, then "the explanation of Sūrat al-Fātiḥah" in four *abwāb* (virtues and names; revelation and rulings; the *Āmīn*; the meanings, readings and *iʿrāb*).

**Corpus file:** `tafsir-al-qurtubi/001.txt` (Arabic; footnotes stripped for reading).

**Elements found:** S1, S2, S3, S4, S5, S6 · V1, V2, V4, V5, V6, V7, V8, V9, V10, V11, V13, V14, V15, V16, V17.

> **Structural note (applies to this file only).** (1) Al-Qurṭubī's *first* two chapters — the twenty-eight
> *masāʾil* on the basmalah — are a treatise on the whole sūrah's constitution (its being a verse or not, its
> place in prayer, its derivation), so the sūrah-level elements S1–S6 begin only with "the explanation of
> Sūrat al-Fātiḥah"; the basmalah questions are filed under 1:1 as their matter requires. (2) His *bāb* on
> *Āmīn* is filed at V17 under 1:7, since it follows the end of the sūrah. (3) His *bāb* three (the *Āmīn*
> chapter) also carries the sūrah's division-of-the-prayer material used under S5.

### `tafsir-al-tabari.md`

**Source work:** *Jāmiʿ al-Bayān ʿan taʾwīl āy al-Qurʾān* — Abū Jaʿfar Muḥammad ibn Jarīr al-Ṭabarī (d. 310/923); in the corpus file the text is given with the editor's footnotes, from which the chain-appraisals below are drawn.

**Corpus file:** `tafsir-al-tabari/001.txt` (Arabic, with numbered reports).

**Elements found:** S5 · V1, V2, V4, V5, V6, V7, V8, V9, V10, V11, V13, V14, V17.

> **Structural note (applies to this file only).** (1) This work is an isnād-tafsīr: almost every exposition
> is carried by a chain, so the chains are given with the reports and the corpus editor's appraisal of a
> weak chain is recorded as such — the record distinguishes what al-Ṭabarī transmits from what he argues.
> (2) The corpus file has **no sūrah-level introduction** (no names, no Makkī/Madanī, no verse count); the
> one sūrah-level matter it contains — the argument over why al-Fātiḥah says in seven verses what two
> verses could carry — is recorded at the end, under S5. (3) Al-Ṭabarī's ruling that the *basmalah* is not
> a verse of al-Fātiḥah is a premise of his commentary on 1:3; it is recorded under 1:3, V2, where he uses it.

### `tafsir-as-saadi.md`

**Source work:** *Taysīr al-Karīm al-Raḥmān fī tafsīr kalām al-Mannān* — ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Nāṣir al-Saʿdī (d. 1376/1956).

**Corpus file:** `tafsir-as-saadi/001.txt` (Arabic).

**Elements found:** S2, S5 · V1, V2, V8, V10, V11, V14, V15, V17.

> **Structural note (applies to this file only).** In the corpus file the entire sūrah commentary is
> repeated byte-identical under all seven headings `## 1:1` … `## 1:7`. The repetition has been removed
> and the material re-mapped to the verses the author's own markers `{1} {2} {4} {5} {6} {7}` name. The
> file contains **no material keyed to 1:3** — al-Saʿdī explains *al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm* within the basmalah
> and does not give 1:3 a separate exposition.

### `tafsir-ibn-abbas.md`

**Source work:** *Tanwīr al-Miqbās min tafsīr Ibn ʿAbbās*, the report-based tafsīr ascribed to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās (d. 68/687) and transmitted in this form through its chains.

**Corpus file:** `tafsir-ibn-abbas/001.txt` (English rendering).

**Elements found:** V1, V2, V8, V11 (cross-head), V13 (cross-head), V17.

> **Attribution note (applies to this file only).** This work is a transmitted report-tafsīr throughout:
> every exposition below is a report carried back to Ibn ʿAbbās. The transmitter is therefore named in
> each record's attribution line, and **V6 (āthār) is not used as a separate head** — filing these
> reports under both V2 and V6 would double-file the same material. Where the corpus file gives several
> alternative reports for one phrase, they are enumerated within the record.
>
> **Structural note.** The corpus file carries no separate exposition for 1:1 beyond the rendering of
> the basmalah itself; 1:2 opens the commentary proper.

### `tafsir-ibn-kathir.md`

**Source work:** *Tafsīr al-Qurʾān al-ʿAẓīm* — Ismāʿīl ibn ʿUmar Ibn Kathīr (d. 774/1373), in the English rendering of the corpus file (Abridged edition's translation).

**Corpus file:** `tafsir-ibn-kathir/001.txt` (English, with the Arabic of the ḥadīths retained).

**Elements found:** S1, S2, S3, S4, S5 · V1, V2, V4, V5, V6, V8, V10, V11, V13, V14, V15, V17.

> **Structural note (applies to this file only).** The corpus file places the whole sūrah-level
> introduction — the sūrah's names, place of revelation, verse count, word and letter count, and virtues —
> inside `## 1:1`, together with the basmalah discussion and the long excursus on *istiʿādhah*. Those
> sūrah-level elements have been lifted out to the head of this record; the *istiʿādhah* material, which
> belongs to the etiquette of recitation rather than to verse 1:1, is recorded under 1:1 as V14 with that
> stated plainly.

### `tafsir-maarif-ul-quran.md`

**Source work:** *Maʿārif al-Qurʾān* — Muḥammad Shafīʿ (Muftī Shafīʿ, d. 1396/1976) and Muḥammad Taqī ʿUthmānī, in the English rendering of the corpus file.

**Corpus file:** `tafsir-maarif-ul-quran/001.txt` (English, with Arabic and Urdu quotation retained).

**Elements found:** S5, S6 · V1, V2, V4, V5, V6, V8, V10, V11, V13, V14, V15, V16, V17.

> **Structural note (applies to this file only).** The corpus file's `## 1:2` section opens with the
> sūrah-level statement of the sūrah's structure (three verses of praise, three of petition, one between);
> that is recorded here as S5. Its three-states-of-time analysis of vv. 1–4 is recorded as S6. The rulings
> proper to the basmalah — istiʿādhah, recitation of the basmalah in and out of prayer, handling the verse —
> are recorded under 1:1 as V10.

### `tafsir_initial.md`

**Source work:** *The Study Quran: A New Translation and Commentary* (the corpus's super-source; the file's citation sigla — Q, Ṭ, Ṭb, Ṭs, Ṭū, IK, Qu, Bq, M, Ni, Aj, Sh, Z, My, Su, Qm, JJ, K, R, W — are its own references to the works behind its commentary).

**Corpus file:** `tafsir_initial/001.md` (English).

**Elements found:** S1, S2, S3, S4, S5, S6 · V1, V2, V4, V5, V6, V7, V8, V10, V11, V13, V14, V15, V17.

> **Structural note (applies to this file only).** The corpus file is the one source that supplies a full
> sūrah introduction (`## Introduction`) separately from the verse commentary; its sūrah-level material is
> therefore recorded whole under S1–S6 and not repeated under 1:1. Its commentary reports the positions of
> many earlier works by siglum; where a view is thus attributed, the attributing work is named in the
> record, so the merge can place it alongside that work's own source file.
